   1 | 5
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
   4 | Introduction
   5 | The importance of a conceptual framework
   6 | • A conceptual framework is a set of theoretical principles and concepts that
   7 | underlie the preparation and presentation of financial statements.
   8 | • If no conceptual framework existed, then accounting standards would be
   9 | produced on a haphazard basis as particular issues and circumstances
  10 | arose. These accounting standards might be inconsistent with one another,
  11 | or perhaps even contradictory.
  12 | • A strong conceptual framework means that there are principles in place
  13 | from which all future accounting standards draw. It also acts as a reference
  14 | point for the preparers of financial statements if no accounting standard
  15 | governs a particular transaction (although this will be extremely rare).
  16 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  17 | 6
  18 | Your Notes
  19 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  20 | Introduction
  21 | Purpose
  22 | To assist the IASB to develop IFRS Accounting Standards based on
  23 | consistent concepts
  24 | To assist preparers in developing consistent accounting policies
  25 | when no Standard applies or a Standard allows a choice
  26 | To assist all parties to understand and interpret the Standards
  27 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   5 | Conceptual Framework
   6 | I. The objective of general-purpose financial reporting
   7 | II. The qualitative characteristics of useful financial information

  13 | VIII. Concepts of capital and capital maintenance
  14 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  15 | 8
  16 | Your Notes
  17 | 1. The objective of general purpose financial reporting
  18 | The Conceptual Framework states that the purpose of financial reporting is to
  19 | provide information to current and potential investors, lenders and other
  20 | creditors that will enable them to make decisions about providing economic
  21 | resources to an entity.
  22 | An entity’s potential future cash flows, and
  23 | Management’s stewardship of the entity’s economic resource
  24 | If investors, lenders and creditors are going to make these decisions then
  25 | they require information that will help them assess the:
  26 | Economic resources of the entity e.g. assets
  27 | Economic claims against the entity e.g. liabilities and equity
  28 | To assess an entity’s future cash flows, users need information about:
  29 | Changes in economic resources and claims e.g. income and expenses
  30 | 1
  31 | 2
  32 | I. THE OBJECTIVE OF GENERAL-PURPOSE
  33 | FINANCIAL REPORTING
  34 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

================================================================================

   1 | 9
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Going concern assumption
   4 | I. THE OBJECTIVE OF GENERAL-PURPOSE
   5 | FINANCIAL REPORTING
   6 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
   7 | KEY POINT
   8 | The going concern assumption underlies the preparation of financial statements
   9 | Note:
  10 | Conceptual Framework 2010: Going Concern and Accrual Basis
  11 | However, Following Conceptual Framework 2018 has only Going Concern
  12 | Going concern assumes that the entity will continue in
  13 | operation for the foreseeable future. This basis is presumed to
  14 | apply unless users of financial statements are told otherwise
  15 | (in the notes to the FS).
  16 | Therefore, there is neither the intention nor the need to enter
  17 | liquidation or cease trading.
  18 | If the going concern is in doubt, a capital reconstruction may
  19 | be necessary.
  20 | 01
  21 | 02
  22 | 03
  23 | 10
  24 | Your Notes
  25 | II. THE QUALITATIVE CHARACTERISTICS OF
  26 | USEFUL FINANCIAL INFORMATION
  27 | 1. Fundamental qualitative characteristics
  28 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  29 | Relevance
  30 | Relevant information is capable of making a difference in the
  31 | decisions made by users. Financial information is capable of
  32 | making a difference in decisions if it has predictive value,
  33 | confirmatory value or both.
  34 | Information is material if omitting, misstating or obscuring it
  35 | could reasonably be expected to influence decisions that the
  36 | primary users of general-purpose financial statements make
  37 | on the basis of those financial statements.
  38 | Materiality
  39 | Faithful
  40 | Representation
  41 | A faithful representation reflects substance rather than legal
  42 | form and is:
  43 | • Complete – all information necessary for understanding.
  44 | • Neutral – without bias, supported by the exercise of
  45 | prudence.
  46 | • Free from error – processes and descriptions are without
  47 | error. This does not mean perfectly accurate in all respects.

================================================================================
PAGE 6
================================================================================
   1 | 11
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Enhancing qualitative characteristics
   4 | In addition to the two fundamental qualitative characteristics, there are four
   5 | enhancing qualitative characteristics of useful financial information:
   6 | Comparability: Investors should be able to compare an entity’s
   7 | financial information year-on-year and on entity’s financial information
   8 | with another
   9 | Timeliness: Older information is less useful
  10 | Understandability: Information should be presented as clearly
  11 | and concisely as possible
  12 | Verifiability: Knowledgeable users should be able to agree that a
  13 | particular depiction of a transaction offers a faithful representation
  14 | II. THE QUALITATIVE CHARACTERISTICS OF
  15 | USEFUL FINANCIAL INFORMATION
  16 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  17 | 12
  18 | Your Notes
  19 | III. FINANCIAL STATEMENTS AND
  20 | REPORTING ENTITY
  21 | 1. Financial statements
  22 | The Conceptual Framework notes that financial statements are a particular type of
  23 | financial report. The purpose of financial statements is to provide information to
  24 | users about an entity’s:
  25 | Assets
  26 | Liabilities
  27 | Equity
  28 | Income
  29 | Expenses
  30 | A statement of financial position
  31 | Statement of financial performance
  32 | This information is provided in:
  33 | Other statements, such as statements of cash flows and notes
  34 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   1 | 13
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 01
   4 | A reporting entity is one that prepares financial statements (either
   5 | through choice or as a result of legal requirements)
   6 | Financial
   7 | statements
   8 | produced
   9 | for
  10 | two
  11 | or
  12 | entities
  13 | that
  14 | are
  15 | not
  16 | parent/subsidiaries are called ‘combined financial statements’. It can be
  17 | difficult in these circumstances to determine the boundary of the
  18 | reporting entity
  19 | 02
  20 | •
  21 | Financial statements produced for a reporting entity that comprises
  22 | a parent company and its subsidiaries are called ‘consolidated
  23 | financial statements’. These financial statements show the parent
  24 | and its subsidiaries as a single economic entity.
  25 | •
  26 | This information is important for investors in the parent because
  27 | their economic returns are dependent on distributions from the
  28 | subsidiary to the parent.
  29 | 03
  30 | 2. The reporting entity
  31 | III. FINANCIAL STATEMENTS AND
  32 | REPORTING ENTITY
  33 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  34 | 14
  35 | The elements are building blocks of financial statements:
  36 | •
  37 | Statements of financial position report assets, liabilities and equity
  38 | •
  39 | Statements of financial performance report income and expenses
  40 | IV. THE ELEMENTS OF FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
  41 | 1. The elements of financial statements
  42 | Economic resource
  43 | Asset
  44 | A present economic resource controlled by an entity as a result of a
  45 | past event
  46 | Economic claim
  47 | Liability
  48 | A present obligation of the entity to transfer an economic resource as a
  49 | result of a past event
  50 | Equity
  51 | The residual interest in the net assets of an entity
  52 | Changes in economic
  53 | resources and claims as a
  54 | result of financial
  55 | performance
  56 | Income
  57 | Increases in assets or decreases in liabilities that result in an increase
  58 | in equity (excluding contributions from equity holders)
  59 | Expenses
  60 | Decreases in assets or increases in liabilities that result in decreases in
  61 | equity (excluding distributions to equity holders)
  62 | Other changes in economic
  63 | resources and claims
  64 | Contributions form, and distributions to, equity holders.
  65 | Exchanges of assets and liabilities that do not increase or decrease
  66 | equity.
  67 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   1 | 15
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | V. RECOGNITION AND DERECOGNITION
   4 | 1. Recognition
   5 | Recognition – the process of capturing for inclusion in the statement of financial
   6 | position or statement of financial performance an item that meets the definition
   7 | of an element
   8 | Recognition
   9 | involves
  10 | The depiction of the item in words
  11 | and by a monetary amount
  12 | The inclusion of that amount in one or
  13 | more totals in that statement
  14 | Only items that meet the definition of an element may be recognised. However, not
  15 | all items that do meet these definitions are recognised
  16 | KEY POINT
  17 | Inappropriate recognition can never be rectified by disclosure of
  18 | the accounting policies used nor by notes or explanatory material.
  19 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  20 | 16
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 2. Recognition Criteria
  23 | Items are only recognised in the financial statements if they meet the definition of
  24 | one of the elements. However, not all items meeting these definitions are
  25 | recognised.
  26 | Elements are recognized if recognized provide users with financial information.
  27 | Relevant information.
  28 | A faithful representation of
  29 | the
  30 | asset
  31 | or
  32 | liability, and resulting income, expenses or
  33 | equity movements.
  34 | Recognised
  35 | must provide
  36 | 01
  37 | Recognition might not provide relevant information if there is uncertainty
  38 | over the existence of the element or if there is a low probability of an
  39 | inflow or outflow of economic resources.
  40 | 02
  41 | Recognition of an element might not provide a faithful representation if
  42 | there is a very high degree of measurement uncertainty.
  43 | 03
  44 | If an asset or liability is not recognised, disclosures may be required to
  45 | ensure users fully understand the reporting entity’s economic transactions
  46 | and the implications that these may have on future earnings and future
  47 | cash flows.
  48 | V. RECOGNITION AND DERECOGNITION
  49 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

================================================================================
PAGE 9
================================================================================
   1 | 17
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Recognition Criteria
   4 | Example 1 (Question)
   5 | Bottle operates in the publishing industry. The Bottle brand is highly respected
   6 | and, as a result, the books published by Bottle receive extensive coverage both
   7 | online and in national and international press. The brand is internally generated
   8 | and, in accordance with IAS 38 Intangible Assets, is not recognised in Bottle’s
   9 | financial statements.
  10 | Required:
  11 | Discuss the extent to which the accounting treatment of the Bottle brand is
  12 | consistent with the Conceptual Framework
  13 | V. RECOGNITION AND DERECOGNITION
  14 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  15 | 18
  16 | Your Notes
  17 | 2. Recognition Criteria
  18 | Example 1 (Answer)
  19 | •
  20 | The brand is an economic resource controlled by Bottle. It has the potential
  21 | to bring economic benefits because of the exposure that Bottle-branded
  22 | books receive.
  23 | •
  24 | Despite meeting the definition of an element, the brand is not recognised in
  25 | the financial statements. However, the Conceptual Framework states that
  26 | elements should only be recognised if they provide relevant information or
  27 | a faithful representation of the asset or liability. If there is a high degree of
  28 | measurement uncertainty then recognition may not provide a faithful
  29 | representation.
  30 | •
  31 | The cost of an internally generated brand cannot be reliably measured. This
  32 | is because the costs of setting up and developing the brand cannot be
  33 | separated from the operating costs of the business. The fair value of a
  34 | brand is also very difficult to determine because the brands are unique.
  35 | •
  36 | Thus, it would seem that the accounting treatment of the brand, per IAS 38
  37 | Intangible Assets, is consistent with the Conceptual Framework
  38 | V. RECOGNITION AND DERECOGNITION
  39 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   1 | 19
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 3. Derecognition
   4 | Derecognition is the removal of some or all of an asset or liability from the
   5 | statement of financial position. This normally occurs when the entity:
   6 | Loses control of the asset, or
   7 | 1
   8 | Has no present obligation for liability
   9 | 2
  10 | Accounting for derecognition should faithfully represent the changes in an entity’s
  11 | net assets, as well as any assets or liabilities retained.
  12 | This is achieved by:
  13 | Derecognising
  14 | any
  15 | transferred,
  16 | expired or consumed component
  17 | Recognising a gain or loss on
  18 | the above
  19 | Recognising any retained component
  20 | V. RECOGNITION AND DERECOGNITION
  21 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  22 | 20
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 3. Derecognition
  25 | Example
  26 | An entity sells a building for $2 million and retains the right to buy it back for $3
  27 | million in five year’s time. At the date of sale, the building had a fair value of $7
  28 | million. Property prices are expected to rise.
  29 | The entity does not derecognise the building from its statement of financial
  30 | position. The entity has not lost control over the building because its ability to
  31 | buy the building back for substantially less than fair value enables it to benefit
  32 | from future price rises.
  33 | The cash received would be recognized as a loan liability.
  34 | V. RECOGNITION AND DERECOGNITION
  35 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   3 | VI. MEASUREMENT
   4 | 1. Historical cost and Current value
   5 | Historical
   6 | cost
   7 | Current
   8 | value
   9 | Reflect changes in value. The Conceptual framework
  10 | refers to three current value measurements:
  11 | The Conceptual Framework refers to two main measurement bases: historical cost
  12 | and current value
  13 | Based on the transaction price when an asset was
  14 | acquired/created or a liability was incurred. This
  15 | measurement
  16 | basis
  17 | does
  18 | not
  19 | generally
  20 | reflect
  21 | changes in values
  22 | Fair value
  23 | The price received to sell an asset
  24 | or paid to transfer a liability
  25 | Value in use/
  26 | Fulfilment
  27 | value
  28 | Value in use is the present value of
  29 | cash flows expected to be derived
  30 | from the use of an asset and its
  31 | ultimate disposal.
  32 | Fulfilment
  33 | value
  34 | is
  35 | the
  36 | present
  37 | value of resources transferred to
  38 | fulfil a liability.
  39 | Current cost
  40 | The
  41 | cost
  42 | of
  43 | an
  44 | equivalent
  45 | asset/consideration that would be
  46 | received for an equivalent liability,
  47 | at the measurement date.
  48 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  49 | 22
  50 | Your Notes
  51 | 2. Selection of a Measurement Basis
  52 | Management should refer to the fundamental qualitative characteristics of
  53 | relevance and faithful representation when selecting a measurement basis.
  54 | Enhancing characteristics should also be considered
  55 | How the asset/liability contributes to future cash flows
  56 | Historical cost or current cost is likely to provide relevant
  57 | information for assets (e.g. PPE) which indirectly contribute to
  58 | future cash flows when used in combination with other assets
  59 | The
  60 | characteristics
  61 | of
  62 | the
  63 | asset
  64 | or
  65 | liability
  66 | (and
  67 | related
  68 | income/expense), e.g. if the value of an asset is subject to
  69 | market fluctuations then fair value may be more relevant than
  70 | historical cost
  71 | 2
  72 | 1
  73 | Relevance of information is affected by:
  74 | VI. MEASUREMENT
  75 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   1 | 23
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Selection of a Measurement Basis
   4 | Measurement inconsistency
   5 | Using different measurement bases for related assets and
   6 | liabilities can result in measurement inconsistency (accounting
   7 | mismatch).
   8 | More
   9 | useful
  10 | information
  11 | may be
  12 | provided
  13 | by
  14 | selecting the same measurement basis for related assets and
  15 | liabilities.
  16 | Measurement uncertainty arises when a measure must be
  17 | estimated and cannot be determined by observing prices in an
  18 | active market, high levels of measurement uncertainty may result
  19 | in information that is not as faithful representation.
  20 | Faithful representation is affected by:
  21 | 1
  22 | 2
  23 | VI. MEASUREMENT
  24 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  25 | 24
  26 | Your Notes
  27 | 2. Selection of a Measurement Basis
  28 | Example 2 (Question)
  29 | Africant is about to hold its annual general meeting with shareholders and the
  30 | directors wish to prepare for any potential questions which may be raised at the
  31 | meeting. There have been discussions in the media over the fact that the most
  32 | relevant measurement method should be selected for each category of assets
  33 | and liabilities. This ‘mixed measurement approach’ is used by many entities
  34 | when preparing financial statements. There have also been comments in the
  35 | media about the impact that measurement uncertainty and price volatility can
  36 | have on the quality of financial information.
  37 | Required:
  38 | Discuss the impact which the above matters may have on the analysis of
  39 | financial statements by investors in Africant
  40 | VI. MEASUREMENT
  41 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

================================================================================
PAGE 13
================================================================================
   1 | 25
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Selection of a Measurement Basis
   4 | Example 2 (Answer)
   5 | •
   6 | Some investors might argue in favour of a single measurement basis for all
   7 | recognised assets and liabilities as the resulting totals and subtotals can
   8 | have little meaning if different measurement methods are used.
   9 | •
  10 | Similarly, profit or loss may lack relevance if it reflects a combination of
  11 | flows based on historical cost and value changes for items measured on a
  12 | current value basis
  13 | •
  14 | However, the majority of investors would tend to favour a mixed
  15 | measurement approach, whereby the most relevant measurement method
  16 | is selected for each category of assets and liability. This approach is
  17 | consistent with how investors analyse financial statements
  18 | •
  19 | The problems of mixed measurement are outweighed by the greater
  20 | relevance achieved if the most relevant measurement basis is used for
  21 | each of the assets and liabilities.
  22 | •
  23 | Most accounting measures of assets and liabilities are uncertain and
  24 | require
  25 | estimation.
  26 | While
  27 | some
  28 | measures
  29 | of
  30 | historical
  31 | cost
  32 | are
  33 | straightforward as it is the amount paid or received, there are many
  34 | occasions when the measurement of cost can be uncertain – particularly
  35 | recoverable cost, for which impairment and depreciation estimates are
  36 | required.
  37 | VI. MEASUREMENT
  38 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  39 | 26
  40 | Your Notes
  41 | 2. Selection of a Measurement Basis
  42 | Example 2 (Answer)
  43 | •
  44 | High measurement uncertainty might reduce the quality of information
  45 | available to investors. High price volatility may make analysing an
  46 | investment in that entity more challenging. If a relevant measure of an
  47 | asset or liability is volatile, this should not be hidden from investors
  48 | •
  49 | To conceal its volatility would decrease the usefulness of the financial
  50 | statements. Of course, such volatile gains and losses do need to be clearly
  51 | presented and disclosed, because their predictive value may differ from
  52 | that provided by other components of performance.
  53 | VI. MEASUREMENT
  54 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

   1 | 27
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | VII. PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE
   4 | 1. As Communication Tools
   5 | Effective presentation and disclosure require:
   6 | A
   7 | focus
   8 | on
   9 | presentation
  10 | and
  11 | disclosure objectives and principles:
  12 | achieving a balance between flexibility
  13 | to provide relevant information that
  14 | faithfully represents a transaction and
  15 | achieving comparability
  16 | Effective communication in financial statements makes information more relevant,
  17 | contributes to a faithful representation of a financial position and performance and
  18 | enhances the understandability and comparability of information
  19 | Classification
  20 | based
  21 | on
  22 | shared
  23 | characteristics: grouping similar items,
  24 | separating
  25 | dissimilar
  26 | items
  27 | and,
  28 | in
  29 | general,
  30 | not
  31 | offsetting
  32 | assets
  33 | and
  34 | liabilities
  35 | Aggregation of items that are
  36 | included
  37 | in
  38 | the
  39 | same
  40 | classification:
  41 | achieving
  42 | a
  43 | balance between summarizing
  44 | a large volume of detail and
  45 | concealing detail
  46 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  47 | 28
  48 | Your Notes
  49 | 2. Profit or loss and other comprehensive income
  50 | The statement of profit or loss is the primary source of information about an
  51 | entity’s performance. As such, income and expenses
  52 | should normally be
  53 | recognized in this statement
  54 | Profit or loss provides more
  55 | relevant information
  56 | A more faithful representation
  57 | is
  58 | provided
  59 | of
  60 | an
  61 | entity’s
  62 | performance
  63 | When developing or revising standards, the Board notes that it might require
  64 | an income or expense to be presented in other comprehensive income if it
  65 | results from remeasuring an item to current value and if this means that:
  66 | However,
  67 | the
  68 | Board
  69 | may
  70 | decide the reclassification is
  71 | not appropriate if there is no
  72 | clear basis for identifying the
  73 | amount
  74 | or
  75 | timing
  76 | of
  77 | the
  78 | reclassification.
  79 | Income
  80 | and
  81 | expenditures
  82 | included in other comprehensive
  83 | income should be reclassified to
  84 | profit or loss when doing so
  85 | results in profit or loss providing
  86 | more relevant information.
  87 | VII. PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE
  88 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

PAGE 15
================================================================================
   1 | 29
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Profit or loss and other comprehensive income
   4 | Example 3 (Question)
   5 | Cryptocurrencies are digital currencies that operate independently of a central
   6 | bank. Some businesses now accept cryptocurrencies in place of traditional
   7 | currencies.
   8 | The market price of cryptocurrency is highly volatile. Investors can earn large
   9 | returns by buying cryptocurrency on an exchange when the quoted price is low
  10 | selling on an exchange when the quoted price is low and selling on an
  11 | exchange when the quoted price rises.
  12 | Cryptocurrencies have proved problematic with regards to financial reporting
  13 | because they do not seem to fall within the scope of an issued IFRS or IAS
  14 | Standard. As such, preparers of financial statements must use the Conceptual
  15 | Framework to devise an accounting treatment that provides useful information
  16 | to financial statement users.
  17 | Required:
  18 | Using the Conceptual Framework, discuss how an entity might account for an
  19 | investment in cryptocurrency that it holds to trade.
  20 | VII. PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE
  21 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  22 | 30
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 2. Profit or loss and other comprehensive income
  25 | Example 3 (Answer)
  26 | •
  27 | The definition of an asset in the Conceptual Framework is an economic
  28 | resource controlled by an entity from a past event. Cryptocurrency meets
  29 | this definition because it can be traded or used to buy goods and therefore
  30 | has the potential to produce economic benefits.
  31 | •
  32 | Items should be recognised in the financial statements if they meet the
  33 | definition of an element and if recognition provides relevant information
  34 | and a faithful representation of the underlying item. If cryptocurrency is
  35 | traded then information about such investments will help users when
  36 | assessing an entity’s future cash flows. As such, recognition is appropriate.
  37 | •
  38 | The historical cost of cryptocurrency may differ significantly from its
  39 | current value. As such, the shareholders are likely to be interested in the
  40 | current value of the investment because the eventual sale will have a
  41 | significant impact on future net cash flows.
  42 | •
  43 | Moreover, under historical cost, information about value changes is not
  44 | normally
  45 | reported
  46 | until
  47 | disposal.
  48 | Therefore
  49 | it
  50 | would
  51 | seem
  52 | that
  53 | a
  54 | measurement based on current value – such as fair value – will provide
  55 | relevant and timely information to shareholders
  56 | VII. PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE
  57 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK

================================================================================
PAGE 16
================================================================================
   1 | 31
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. Profit or loss and other comprehensive income
   4 | Example 3 (Answer)
   5 | •
   6 | Whilst the value of cryptocurrency is highly volatile, that value is likely to be
   7 | extracted from a short-term sale. As such, it would seem that reporting
   8 | gains and losses in profit or loss would provide the most relevant
   9 | information about economic performance in the period.
  10 | •
  11 | In conclusion, by applying the Conceptual Framework, an appropriate
  12 | accounting treatment for an investment in cryptocurrency would be to
  13 | remeasure the investment to fair value at the reporting date and to present
  14 | the resulting income or expense in the statement of profit or loss.
  15 | VII. PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE
  16 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  17 | 32
  18 | VIII. CONCEPTS OF CAPITAL AND CAPITAL MAINTENANCE
  19 | 1. Concepts of capital and capital maintenance
  20 | A financial concept of capital is adopted by most entities.
  21 | There are two concepts of capital maintenance:
  22 | A profit is earned if the financial (money) amount of the net assets at the end of the period exceeds the net assets at the beginning of
  23 | the period, excluding distributions to/contributions from holders of equity claims during the period.
  24 | Financial capital maintenance
  25 | 01
  26 | A profit is made if the physical productive capacity (or operating capability) of the entity at the end of the period exceeds the physical
  27 | productive capacity at the beginning of the period (excluding any distributions to/contributions from holders of equity claims during
  28 | the period).
  29 | Physical capital maintenance
  30 | 02
  31 | CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK
  32 | There are two concepts relating to capital:
  33 | • Financial concept of capital where capital refers to the net assets or equity of an entity
  34 | • Physical concept of the capital where capital is regarded as the productive capacity of
  35 | the entity, for example, units of output per day

   7 | According
   8 | to
   9 | the
  10 | International
  11 | Financial
  12 | Reporting
  13 | Standards
  14 | (IFRS)
  15 | framework, an asset is a resource controlled by an entity as a result of past
  16 | events and from which future economic benefits are expected to flow to the
  17 | entity. Here's how the terms relate to this definition:
  18 | •
  19 | Control: The entity must have control over the resource, meaning it can
  20 | direct its use and obtain its benefits.
  21 | •
  22 | Future economic benefits: The asset must be expected to provide
  23 | future economic benefits to the entity.
  24 | •
  25 | Past transaction: The asset arises from past events or transactions.
  26 | Question 4:
  27 | The correct answer is C
  28 | Legally, the entity may not own the leased asset, but for accounting
  29 | purposes, if the lease transfers substantially all the risks and rewards of
  30 | ownership to the lessee, the asset is treated as if the lessee owns it. This
  31 | ensures that the financial statements reflect the true economic substance of
  32 | the transaction, providing a faithful representation of the entity's financial
  33 | position.

   4 | I. WHERE HAVE IFRS STANDARDS BEEN ADOPTED?
   5 | This module covers the adoption of IFRS Standards throughout the world, the growth of the Board and IFRS Standards and IFRS Standards for
   6 | small and medium-sized entities.
   7 | Individual jurisdictions may permit or require certain companies to apply IFRS Standards:
   8 | Europe

  14 | New

  23 | apply IFRS Standards
  24 | •

  26 | •

  31 | Standard equivalent national standards

  34 | similar to IFRS Standard

  40 | national standards
  41 | B.
  42 | The IFRS for SMEs was developed by the IASB based on a number of
  43 | existing national standards for SMEs
  44 | C.
  45 | The IFRS for SMEs may be adopted by any jurisdiction, regardless of
  46 | whether it has adopted full IFRS
  47 | D.
  48 | The IFRS for SMEs allows the same accounting options as full IFRSs
  49 | but reduces the level of required disclosures significantly
  50 | Module 2 Quiz
  51 | THE STATUS AND USE OF IFRS
  52 | STANDARDS AROUND THE WORLD

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 43
   3 | Module 2 Quiz
   4 | Answer
   5 | Question 1:
   6 | The correct answer is D.
   7 | China prohibits using IFRSs by domestic listed companies because it has its
   8 | own set of accounting standards, known as the Accounting Standards for
   9 | Business Enterprises (ASBE), which are mandatory for all listed companies
  10 | in China. Although China has been working towards convergence with IFRS,
  11 | it has not yet adopted IFRS for domestic listed companies.
  12 | Question 2:
  13 | The correct answer is B.
  14 | The IFRS for SMEs is a simplified version of IFRS that is designed for small
  15 | and medium-sized entities (SMEs). It is not suitable for listed companies or
  16 | banks, regardless of their size.
  17 | Therefore, option 1 (an unlisted bank) and option 2 (a listed company that
  18 | meets size thresholds for a small entity) are not eligible to use the IFRS for
  19 | SMEs.
  20 | THE STATUS AND USE OF IFRS
  21 | STANDARDS AROUND THE WORLD
  22 | Your Notes
  23 | 44
  24 | Module 2 Quiz
  25 | Answer
  26 | Question 3:
  27 | The correct answer is C.
  28 | The IFRS for SMEs is a self-contained standard that can be adopted by any
  29 | jurisdiction, regardless of whether it has adopted full IFRSs. It was
  30 | developed by the International Accounting Standards Board (IASB) based on
  31 | full IFRS, but it is not a subset of full IFRS.
  32 | The IFRS for SMEs is designed to be easier and less costly to implement
  33 | than the full IFRS, making it more suitable for small and medium-sized
  34 | entities (SMEs).
  35 | Option A is not true, as the IFRS for SMEs can be adopted by jurisdictions
  36 | that have their own national standards.
  37 | Option B is not true, as the IFRS for SMEs was not developed based on a
  38 | number of existing national standards for SMEs.
  39 | Option D is not true, as the IFRS for SMEs does not allow the same
  40 | accounting options as full IFRSs. It has a reduced set of accounting options
  41 | and reduced disclosure requirements compared to full IFRSs.
  42 | THE STATUS AND USE OF IFRS
  43 | STANDARDS AROUND THE WORLD

  49 | Question 1:
  50 | Should the going concern assumption be dropped when part of a
  51 | reporting entity is suffering financial difficulty?
  52 | You should refer to the text of the standard when answering all exercises.
  53 | Your answer:
  54 | Chapter 2 Quiz
  55 | PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE IN
  56 | FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

================================================================================
PAGE 30
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 59
   3 | Chapter 2 Quiz
   4 | Answer
   5 | Question 1:
   6 | According to the text of the standard, the going concern assumption should
   7 | not be dropped when part of a reporting entity is suffering financial difficulty.
   8 | The going concern assumption presumes that a company will continue
   9 | normal business operations into the future. When liquidation is imminent,
  10 | the liquidation basis of accounting is used instead.
  11 | The standard requires management to decide whether there are conditions
  12 | or events that raise substantial doubt about the company's ability to
  13 | continue as a going concern within one year after the date that the financial
  14 | statements are issued. Substantial doubt exists when relevant conditions
  15 | and events, considered in the aggregate, indicate that it's probable that the
  16 | company won't be able to meet its current obligations as they become due.
  17 | In the case of a reporting entity suffering financial difficulty, management
  18 | should assess whether there are conditions or events that raise substantial
  19 | doubt about the company's ability to continue as a going concern. If
  20 | substantial
  21 | doubt
  22 | exists,
  23 | management
  24 | should
  25 | consider
  26 | whether
  27 | any
  28 | mitigating plans will alleviate the substantial doubt.
  29 | Therefore, the going concern assumption should not be dropped simply
  30 | because part of a reporting entity is suffering financial difficulty. Instead,
  31 | management
  32 | should
  33 | assess
  34 | the
  35 | situation
  36 | and
  37 | provide
  38 | appropriate
  39 | disclosures in the financial statements.
  40 | PRESENTATION AND DISCLOSURE IN
  41 | FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
  42 | 60

  16 | that it was appropriate to retrospectively reduce the price to $450 per laptop.
  17 | By contrast, revenue will be adjusted in quarter 2 (not retrospectively adjust in
  18 | quarter 1)

  20 | •
  21 | Revenue of additional 320 laptops = 320 laptops x $450 = $144,000
  22 | •
  23 | Less the revenue of 70 laptops regconised in the quarter ended 30
  24 | September 20X5 = $35,000 (70 laptops x$500)

   5 | Example 9: Answer
   6 | At the year ended 31 December 20X1, only 40% of the goods were sold to the
   7 | final customer, so only 40% of the sale ($42,000) should be recognized as
   8 | revenue.

  10 | August 20X1 is wrong.
  11 | The wholesaler sells at a mark-up of 20% on cost:
  12 | Sale
  13 | $42,000
  14 | 120%
  15 | COGS
  16 | ($42,000 x 100/120)
  17 | $35,000
  18 | 100%
  19 | Profit
  20 | ($42,000 x 20/120)
  21 | $7,000
  22 | 20%
  23 | The adjustments needed to correct the wholesaler’s financial statements for
  24 | the year ended 31 December 20X1 are as follow:
  25 | Dr Revenue
  26 | ($42,000 – $42,000 x 40%)
  27 | $25,200
  28 | Cr Trade receivable
  29 | $25,200
  30 | Dr Inventory
  31 | ($35,000 – $35,000 x 40%)
  32 | $21,000
  33 | Cr COGS

  39 | II. COMMON TYPES OF TRANSACTION
  40 | 5. Bill-and-hold arrangements
  41 | Sell goods (billing only)
  42 | Recognize revenue (criteria attached*)
  43 | Seller
  44 | Buyer
  45 | Seller’s warehouse
  46 | Bear all risks of goods
  47 | Hold goods
  48 | (*) For a customer to have obtained control of a product in a bill and hold
  49 | arrangement, the following criteria must all be met:
  50 | •
  51 | The reason for the bill-and-hold must be substantive.
  52 | •
  53 | The product must be separately identified as belonging to the customer.
  54 | •
  55 | The product must be ready for physical transfer to the customer.
  56 | •
  57 | The entity cannot have the ability to use the product or to transfer it to
  58 | another customer.
  59 | A bill-and-hold arrangement is a contract under which an entity bills a customer
  60 | for a product but the entity retains physical possession of the product until it is
  61 | transferred to the customer at a point in time in the future.
  62 | REVENUE FROM CONTRACTS

  29 | The entity can recognize revenue on 31 December 20X9 because criteria are
  30 | met:
  31 | •

  39 | •

  41 | “The product must be ready for physical transfer to the customer” is

  44 | The entity does not have the ability to use the spare parts or direct them to
  45 | another customer →“The entity cannot have the ability to use the product

  26 | Your Notes
  27 | ACCOUNTING POLICIES, CHANGES IN
  28 | ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES, AND ERRORS
  29 | I. ACCOUNTING POLICIES
  30 | Accounting policies are the specific principles, bases, conventions, rules and
  31 | practices adopted by an entity in preparing and presenting financial statements.
  32 | The definition of some relevant terminologies:
  33 | Retrospective
  34 | Applying a new accounting policy to transactions, other events and
  35 | conditions as if that policy had always been applied
  36 | Retrospective restatement
  37 | Correcting the recognition, measurement and disclosure of amounts
  38 | of elements of financial statements as if a prior period error had
  39 | never occurred.
  40 | Prospective
  41 | • Applying the new accounting policy to transactions, other events
  42 | and conditions occurring after the date as at which the policy is
  43 | changed.
  44 | • Recognising the effect of the change in the accounting estimate
  45 | in the current and future periods affected by the change.
  46 | Impracticable
  47 | Applying a requirement is impracticable when the entity cannot
  48 | apply it after making every reasonable effort to do so
  49 | Practicable
  50 | Applying a requirement is practicable when the entity can apply it
  51 | after making every reasonable effort to do so

  10 | Use that standard or interpretation

  23 | ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES, AND ERRORS
  24 | I. ACCOUNTING POLICIES
  25 | 2. Change in accounting policy
  26 | Accounting
  27 | policies
  28 | must be consistently
  29 | applied
  30 | for similar transactions,
  31 | categories, other events and conditions. Accounting policies only change if it
  32 | satisfies 2 following conditions:
  33 | Conditions to change accounting policies
  34 | Required by a new
  35 | standard or interpretation
  36 | Provide more relevant and
  37 | reliable information.
  38 | We can identify whether a change is change in accounting policies or not rely on
  39 | following feature:
  40 | Recognition
  41 | e.g. an expense is
  42 | now recognized
  43 | rather than an
  44 | asset
  45 | Presentation
  46 | e.g. depreciation is now
  47 | included in cost of sales
  48 | rather than administrative
  49 | expenses
  50 | Measurement basis
  51 | e.g. stating assets at
  52 | replacement cost rather
  53 | than historical cost.
  54 | Sign of change of accounting policy

   4 | ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES, AND ERRORS
   5 | II. ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES
   6 | Definition
   7 | Accounting estimates are judgements or assumptions
   8 | used in applying an accounting policy when, because of
   9 | estimation uncertainty, an item in financial statements
  10 | cannot be measured with precision.
  11 | Example of accounting estimate:
  12 | •
  13 | Useful-life of depreciable assets
  14 | •
  15 | Provision for obsolescence of inventory
  16 | A change in
  17 | accounting
  18 | estimate
  19 | A change in accounting estimate is an adjustment of the
  20 | carrying amount of an asset or a liability or the amount of
  21 | the periodic consumption of an asset that results from the
  22 | assessment of the present status of, and expected future
  23 | benefits and obligations associated with, assets and
  24 | liabilities
  25 | Principle of
  26 | change in
  27 | accounting
  28 | estimate
  29 | Recognise the change prospectively in profit or loss in:
  30 | •
  31 | Period of change, if it only affects that period only
  32 | •
  33 | Period of change and future periods, if the change
  34 | affects both
  35 | Your Notes
  36 | 108

  42 | Specific principles,

  44 | and practices

  46 | Past, current and future
  47 | period
  48 | · Nature and amount of
  49 | the change for the
  50 | current period and for
  51 | each prior period
  52 | presented.
  53 | · The fact that effect of
  54 | comparative information
  55 | has been restated.

  57 | Criteria
  58 | Principle
  59 | Disclosure

  62 | accounting policy

  64 | Current and future period

  66 | of the periodic

  70 | change that has an effect

  75 | of future periods is NOT
  76 | disclosed because of
  77 | impracticality.

  79 | estimate

   4 | ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES, AND ERRORS
   5 | II. ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES
   6 | Example 1:
   7 | 1. An entity has previously charged interest incurred in connection with the
   8 | construction of tangible non-current assets to the statement of profit or loss.
   9 | Following the revision of IAS 23 Borrowing Costs, and in accordance with the
  10 | revised requirements of that standard, it now capitalises this interest.
  11 | 2. An entity has previously depreciated vehicles using the reducing balance
  12 | method at 40% pa. It now uses the straight-line method over a period of five
  13 | years.
  14 | 3. An entity has previously shown certain overheads within cost of sales. It now
  15 | shows those overheads within administrative expenses.
  16 | 4. An entity has previously measured inventory at weighted average cost. It now
  17 | measures inventory using the first in first out (FIFO) method.
  18 | Answer
  19 | 1. This is a change in recognition and presentation. Therefore this is a change in
  20 | accounting policy.
  21 | 2.
  22 | Depreciation
  23 | method
  24 | is
  25 | only
  26 | an
  27 | assumption
  28 | without
  29 | involvement
  30 | in
  31 | recognition, presentation and measurement. This is only a change in accounting
  32 | estimate.
  33 | 3. This is a change in presentation and therefore a change in accounting policy.
  34 | 4. This is a change in measurement basis and therefore a change in accounting
  35 | policy.
  36 | Your Notes
  37 | 110
  38 | ACCOUNTING POLICIES, CHANGES IN
  39 | ACCOUNTING ESTIMATES, AND ERRORS
  40 | III. PRIOR PERIOD ERRORS
  41 | Prior period errors are omissions from, and misstatements in, the entity's
  42 | financial statements for one or more prior periods arising from a failure to use, or
  43 | misuse of, reliable information.
  44 | Principle of treatment for errors
  45 | Disclosure
  46 | Principle of treatment for errors
  47 | •
  48 | Correct RETROSPECTIVELY as if
  49 | the error had never occurred.
  50 | •
  51 | Either restating the comparative
  52 | amounts for the prior period(s) in
  53 | which the error occurred, or
  54 | •
  55 | When the error occurred before
  56 | the earliest prior period presented,
  57 | restating the opening balances of
  58 | assets, liabilities and equity for
  59 | that period.
  60 | •
  61 | Only where it is impracticable to
  62 | determine the cumulative effect of
  63 | an error on prior periods can an
  64 | entity correct an error
  65 | prospectively.
  66 | Disclosure
  67 | •
  68 | Nature of the prior period error
  69 | •
  70 | For each prior period, to the
  71 | extent practicable, the amount
  72 | of the correction:
  73 | o
  74 | For each financial statement
  75 | line item affected
  76 | o
  77 | If IAS 33 applies, for basic and
  78 | diluted earnings per share
  79 | •
  80 | The amount of the correction at
  81 | the beginning of the earliest
  82 | period presented
  83 | •
  84 | If retrospective restatement is
  85 | impracticable, an explanation
  86 | and description of how the error
  87 | has been corrected is required

   2 | Your Notes
   3 | INTRODUCTION
   4 | It should be noted that an asset is not necessarily something which is owned
   5 | but which is controlled.
   6 | For example, a company that leases an item to use may have an asset if it
   7 | controls that item. Of course, for an item to be an asset at all, it must produce
   8 | some future benefit to the entity.
   9 | An asset
  10 | Not all of an entity’s assets should be recognised in the
  11 | statement of financial position. Some of them cannot be
  12 | measured with sufficient reliability to be included. It is
  13 | necessary to have some measure of either cost or value
  14 | Recognition of an asset
  15 | Having decided to recognise an asset there might then
  16 | be several ways in which it could be measured, such as
  17 | depreciated cost or current market value
  18 | Measurement of an asset

  25 | I. DEFINITION AND TERMINOLOGIES
  26 | 1. Definition of PPE
  27 | Property, plant and equipment (PPE) are tangible assets that:
  28 | •
  29 | Held for use in the production or supply of goods or services, for rental to
  30 | others, or for administrative purposes.
  31 | •
  32 | Are expected to be used during more than one period.
  33 | PROPERTY, PLANT, EQUIPMENT &
  34 | BORROWING COSTS

   5 | II. IAS 16 - PPE
   6 | 1. Recognition of PPE under IAS 16
   7 | As a kind of asset, PPE must simultaneously satisfy two (02) criteria of asset
   8 | recognition:
   9 | Example: A company buys a machine for producing tire for $200,000, and
  10 | expects to use the machine for the next five years. Based on this
  11 | information, machine is recognized as an asset under IAS 16 because:
  12 | •
  13 | It can bring future economic benefits from producing tire for company
  14 | (next five years)
  15 | •
  16 | The cost of machine can be measured reliably (at $200,000)
  17 | Probable
  18 | future
  19 | economic
  20 | benefits associated with the
  21 | asset will flow to the entity.
  22 | The cost of the asset to the
  23 | entity
  24 | can
  25 | be
  26 | measured
  27 | reliably
  28 | 01
  29 | 02
  30 | Your Notes
  31 | 120
  32 | PROPERTY, PLANT, EQUIPMENT &
  33 | BORROWING COSTS
  34 | II. IAS 16 - PPE
  35 | 2. Measurement of PPE under IAS 16
  36 | Initial measurement
  37 | 2.1
  38 | Initial cost of PPE includes:
  39 | 01
  40 | Purchase price less any trade discount or rebate (*)
  41 | 02
  42 | Import duties, non-refundable tax
  43 | 03
  44 | Initial estimate of dismantling (**), removing, restoring costs
  45 | 04
  46 | Directly attributable costs
  47 | 05
  48 | Borrowing costs (IAS 23)
  49 | Note:
  50 | (*) Less trade discount instead of settlement discount because trade discount impact
  51 | directly on the cost of PPE, while settlement discount increases the finance income but
  52 | exert no effect on initial cost of PPE.
  53 | (**) Dismantling costs – the present value (PV) of these costs should be capitalized,
  54 | with an equivalent liability set up. You may need to use the interest rate given and apply
  55 | the discount fraction where:
  56 | PV = Dismantling costs x Discounted factor with:
  57 | r: interest rate; and
  58 | Discount factor =
  59 | 1
  60 | 1+r n
  61 | n: number of years to
  62 | settlement.

   6 | 2. Measurement of PPE under IAS 16
   7 | Initial measurement
   8 | 2.1
   9 | Example 1: Calculation of initial cost
  10 | Tusco bought 20 machines to producing cotton on 1 Jan 2020 with cost of
  11 | $100,000 for each and company receives a trade discount at $1,000 for each.
  12 | Furthermore, for enhancing the capacity of these machines, Tusco paid more
  13 | $400 for each machine to upgrade rotating wheel of the machines. The project
  14 | related to these PPE last for 4 years. When the project ended at 31/12/2023.
  15 | The dismantling cost is $50,000 for each, discount rate 10%. Calculate the
  16 | initial cost of the machines.
  17 | Answer:
  18 | Initial costs for each machine = ($100,000 - $1,000) + $400 + $50,000
  19 | (1+0.1)4 ≈
  20 | $133,550
  21 | Initial costs for all machine = 20 x $133,550 ≈$2,671,000.

  30 | PROPERTY, PLANT, EQUIPMENT &
  31 | BORROWING COSTS
  32 | II. IAS 16 - PPE
  33 | 2. Measurement of PPE under IAS 16
  34 | Subsequent measurement
  35 | 2.2
  36 | Example 2:
  37 | NCC Co acquired a non-current asset on 1 January 20X4 for $160,000. It had
  38 | no residual value and a useful life of ten years. On 1 January 20X7 the
  39 | remaining useful life was reviewed and revised to four years.
  40 | Required: What will be the depreciation charge for 20X7?
  41 | Answer:
  42 | Step 1: Calculate accumulated depreciation before reviewing
  43 | Depreciation per year = $160,000/10 = $16,000
  44 | Accumulated depreciation 20X4 – 20X6 = $16,000 * 3 = $48,000
  45 | Step 2: Calculate carrying amount = Original cost – Accumulated depreciation
  46 | Carrying amount of PPE (20X6) = $160,000 - $48,000 = $112,000
  47 | Step 3: Calculate depreciation after reviewing =
  48 | Carrying amount
  49 | Remaining life after review
  50 | Depreciation (20X7) = $112,000/4 = $28,000

  20 | which it belongs within the revalued at
  21 | the same time

  34 | PROPERTY, PLANT, EQUIPMENT &

  36 | II. IAS 16 - PPE
  37 | 2. Measurement of PPE under IAS 16
  38 | Subsequent measurement
  39 | 2.2
  40 | Example 4:
  41 | NCC Co bought an asset for $20,000 at the beginning of 20X7. It had a useful
  42 | life of five years. On 31 December 20X8 the asset was revalued to $24,000. The
  43 | expected useful life has remained unchanged (i.e three years remain).
  44 | Required: State the treatment for depreciation from 20X9 onwards.
  45 | Answer:
  46 | State the treatment for depreciation from 20X9
  47 | Step 1: Calculate new depreciation = Revaluation amount/remaining useful life
  48 | New depreciation = $24,000/3 = $8,000
  49 | Step 2: Compare new depreciation in step 1 to old depreciation and calculate
  50 | the change in depreciation
  51 | The change in depreciation = $8,000 – $4,000 = $4,000
  52 | Step 3: Compensate the effect of additional depreciation by transferring from
  53 | revaluation surplus to retained earnings
  54 | Debit Revaluation surplus
  55 | $4,000
  56 | Credit Retained earnings
  57 | $4,000

   6 | 2. Measurement of PPE under IAS 16
   7 | Subsequent cost of PPE
   8 | Subsequent measurement
   9 | 2.2
  10 | Subsequent expenditure on PPE is only recognized as an asset when the
  11 | expenditure improves the condition of the asset beyond the previous performance.
  12 | In terms of costs incurred subsequently to add to, replace part of, or service the
  13 | item, the practical application is that:
  14 | •
  15 | Repairs and maintenance expenditure should not be capitalized.
  16 | •
  17 | Replacement parts should be capitalized.
  18 | •
  19 | Overhaul should be capitalized and cost of overhaul would be capitalized as a
  20 | separate component
  21 | Improvement of
  22 | PPE
  23 | Modification of an item of plant to extend its
  24 | useful life, including increased capacity
  25 | Upgrade of machine parts to improve the
  26 | quality of output
  27 | Add a new function for the property

  39 | III. BORROWING COST (IAS 23)
  40 | 1. Scope of IAS 23
  41 | Borrowing cost
  42 | Interest expenses
  43 | under IFRS 9
  44 | Finance charges on
  45 | lease liabilities under
  46 | IFRS 16
  47 | Exchange differences
  48 | arising from foreign
  49 | currency borrowings
  50 | IAS 23 looks at the treatment of borrowing costs related to self-constructed
  51 | assets and inventory which take a substantial period of time to get ready for
  52 | intended use or sale.
  53 | Some relevant terminologies:
  54 | IAS
  55 | Terminologies
  56 | Definition
  57 | IAS 23
  58 | Borrowing costs
  59 | The costs incurred by an entity in connection
  60 | with
  61 | the borrowing
  62 | of
  63 | funds
  64 | in
  65 | order
  66 | to
  67 | construct an asset.
  68 | A qualified asset
  69 | An asset that necessarily takes a substantial
  70 | period of time to get ready for its intended use
  71 | or sale.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 139
   3 | PROPERTY, PLANT, EQUIPMENT &
   4 | BORROWING COSTS
   5 | III. BORROWING COST (IAS 23)
   6 | 2. Capitalization
   7 | Determine capitalized borrowing costs
   8 | 2.2
   9 | Example 6: (Specific Borrowings)
  10 | SKT Co buys asset for $4,000 - takes 2 years to build. Get a $4,000 10% loan.
  11 | SKT Co reinvest any money not used in an 8% deposit account. In year 1, SKT
  12 | Co spends 2,400.
  13 | Required: How many borrowing costs (interests) to be capitalized (added to
  14 | the cost of the asset)?
  15 | Answer:
  16 | Step 1: No information on when construction start assume it starts in line
  17 | with the borrowing timing.
  18 | Step 2: Calculate the interest paid on the specific loans
  19 | Interest paid on loan: $4,000 × 10% = $400
  20 | Step 3: Calculate any interest received on loans proceeds not used
  21 | Interest received on unused loan amount = ($4,000 - $2,400) × 8% = $128
  22 | Step 4: Calculate capitalized costs = $400 - $128 = $272.
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 140
  25 | PROPERTY, PLANT, EQUIPMENT &
  26 | BORROWING COSTS
  27 | III. BORROWING COST (IAS 23)
  28 | 2. Capitalization
  29 | Determine capitalized borrowing costs
  30 | 2.2
  31 | Example 7: (General Borrowings)
  32 | SKT Co takes 3 different current sources of borrowings for its purchase of an
  33 | asset of $10,000, taking 1 year to build. 5% overdraft 2,000; 8% Loan 6,000 and
  34 | 10% Loan 4,000.
  35 | Required: How much interest would go to the cost of the asset?
  36 | Answer:
  37 | Step 1: No information on when construction start assume it starts in line
  38 | with the borrowing timing.
  39 | Step 2: Weighted average of costs = $2,000 × 5% + $6,000 × 8% + $4,000 × 10%
  40 | $2,000 + $6,000 + $4,000
  41 | = 8.17%
  42 | Step 3: Calculate interest amount applicable
  43 | Total interest to be capitalized = $10,000 × 8.17% = $817

  51 | $3m = 8%
  52 | •
  53 | Therefore the amount to be capitalized = 8% x $600,000 x 8/12 = $32,000

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 151
   3 | Chapter 5 Quiz
   4 | Question 6:
   5 | The correct answer is $602,500
   6 | •
   7 | The interest can only be capitalized during the period of construction,
   8 | which is from 1 February 20X6. Therefore the interest can be capitalised
   9 | for 10 months, from 1 February to 30 November 20X6. This gives
  10 | $625,000 ($7.5 million x 10% x 10/12)
  11 | •
  12 | Any temporary investment income earned during this period should be
  13 | netted off the amount capitalized. The amount earned from 1 February
  14 | to 1 May 20X6 is $22,500 ($2 million × 4.5% × 3 /12).
  15 | •
  16 | Therefore the amount to be capitalised is $625,000 – $22,500 =
  17 | $602,500.
  18 | Note:
  19 | All interest incurred and earned in January 20X6 is before the construction
  20 | period and therefore is recorded in the statement of profit or loss.

  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 154
  23 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
  24 | I. OVERVIEW ABOUT INTANGIBLE ASSETS
  25 | 1. Definition of intangible assets
  26 | Intangible asset (ITA) is a non-monetary asset without physical substance that
  27 | is:
  28 | •
  29 | Identifiable (is separable and arises from contractual or other legal rights)
  30 | •
  31 | Controlled by entity as a result of past events (power to obtain benefits from
  32 | the asset)
  33 | •
  34 | Future economic benefits (such as revenues or reduced future costs) are
  35 | expected

================================================================================
PAGE 78
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 155
   3 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
   4 | I. OVERVIEW ABOUT INTANGIBLE ASSETS
   5 | 2. Some relevant terminologies
   6 | IAS
   7 | TERMINOLOGIES
   8 | DEFINITION
   9 | IAS 38
  10 | Research
  11 | Research
  12 | is
  13 | original
  14 | and
  15 | planned
  16 | investigation undertaken with the prospect
  17 | of gaining new scientific knowledge and
  18 | understanding.
  19 | Development
  20 | Development is the research of findings or
  21 | other knowledge to a plan or design for the
  22 | production of new or substantially improved
  23 | materials,
  24 | devices,
  25 | products,
  26 | processes,
  27 | systems or services before the start of
  28 | commercial production or use.
  29 | Amortization
  30 | Amortization is an accounting technique
  31 | used to periodically lower the book value of
  32 | intangible asset over a set period of time.
  33 | Amortization is similar to depreciation.
  34 | IFRS 3
  35 | Goodwill
  36 | Goodwill is the difference between the value
  37 | of a business as a whole and the aggregate
  38 | of the fair values of its separable net assets.
  39 | Your Notes
  40 | 156
  41 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
  42 | I. OVERVIEW ABOUT INTANGIBLE ASSETS
  43 | 3. Scope of IAS 38
  44 | IAS 38 should be followed when accounting for Intangible NCA unless another
  45 | international accounting standard requires a different treatment.
  46 | IAS 38 does NOT apply to the following:
  47 | 01
  48 | 02
  49 | 03
  50 | 04
  51 | 05
  52 | Financial assets under IAS 32
  53 | Exploration and evaluation assets under IFRS 6
  54 | Expenditure on the development and extraction
  55 | of
  56 | minerals,
  57 | oil,
  58 | natural
  59 | gas,
  60 | and
  61 | similar
  62 | resources
  63 | Intangible
  64 | assets
  65 | arising
  66 | from
  67 | insurance
  68 | contracts issued by insurance companies
  69 | Intangible assets covered by another IFRS, such
  70 | as intangibles held for sale, deferred tax assets,
  71 | lease
  72 | assets,
  73 | assets
  74 | arising
  75 | from
  76 | employee
  77 | benefits

   4 | II. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT OF
   5 | INTANGIBLE ASSETS
   6 | 1. Recognition of ITA under IAS 38
   7 | Note: In other cases, customer list owned by company should be not recognized as
   8 | an intangible non-current asset because it cannot be measured reliably.
   9 | Example: Company X buys a customer list for $50,000, and expects to use for
  10 | the next four years to expand customer size. Customer lists is recognized as
  11 | an asset under IAS 38 because:
  12 | •
  13 | It can bring future economic benefits from expand the customer size of
  14 | company (next four years).
  15 | •
  16 | The cost of machine can be measured reliably (at $50,000).
  17 | It is probable that future
  18 | economic benefits
  19 | associated with the asset
  20 | will flow to the entity
  21 | Criteria
  22 | As a kind of asset, ITA must simultaneously satisfy two criteria of asset recognition:
  23 | The cost of the asset can
  24 | be measured reliably

   5 | INTANGIBLE ASSETS
   6 | 2. Measurement of ITA under IAS 38
   7 | Measurement of internally-acquired ITA
   8 | 2.2
   9 | Generally, internally-generated intangible assets cannot be capitalized, as the costs
  10 | associated with these cannot be identified separately from the costs associated
  11 | with running the business.
  12 | The following internally-generated items may never be recognized:
  13 | Mastheads
  14 | Goodwill (Internal goodwill)
  15 | Brands
  16 | Publishing titles
  17 | Customer lists

  20 | INTANGIBLE ASSET

  22 | INTANGIBLE ASSETS
  23 | 3. Disposal and Retirement
  24 | Note: Disposal or retirement of Intangible NCA is closely analogous with disposal
  25 | of PPE.
  26 | Gain from disposal
  27 | Loss from disposal
  28 | Debit Accumulated depreciation
  29 | Debit Cash
  30 | Credit Gain on disposal
  31 | Credit PPE
  32 | Debit Accumulated depreciation
  33 | Debit Cash
  34 | Debit Loss on disposal
  35 | Credit PPE
  36 | DE-RECOGNIZATION OF ITA
  37 | No further economic
  38 | benefits expected
  39 | from its use or
  40 | disposal
  41 | Disposal/retirement
  42 | Step 1: Accounted for in the SOPL
  43 | of the period in which the disposal
  44 | occurs.
  45 | Step 2: Any balance on the
  46 | revaluation surplus should now be
  47 | transferred to retained earnings.
  48 | Disposal proceeds > Carrying amount
  49 | Disposal proceeds < Carrying amount

   1 | Your Notes

   3 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
   4 | II. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT OF
   5 | INTANGIBLE ASSETS
   6 | 4. Amortisation (Additional reading)
   7 | An entity must assess whether the useful life of an intangible asset is finite or
   8 | indefinite.
   9 | Intangible non-current asset with
  10 | Infinite useful life
  11 | Finite useful life
  12 | should not be amortized but be
  13 | subject to an annual impairment
  14 | review
  15 | must be amortized on a systematic
  16 | basis over that life
  17 | Your Notes
  18 | 164
  19 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
  20 | II. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT OF
  21 | INTANGIBLE ASSETS
  22 | Additional reading
  23 | Infinite useful life
  24 | Finite useful life
  25 | •
  26 | The asset is not amortised.
  27 | •
  28 | Its
  29 | useful
  30 | life
  31 | should
  32 | be
  33 | reviewed each reporting period
  34 | to determine
  35 | whether
  36 | events
  37 | and circumstances continue to
  38 | support an indefinite useful life
  39 | assessment for that asset.
  40 | •
  41 | If they do not, the change in the
  42 | useful
  43 | life
  44 | assessment
  45 | from
  46 | indefinite to finite should be
  47 | accounted for as a change in an
  48 | accounting estimate.
  49 | •
  50 | The asset should be assessed
  51 | for impairment annually
  52 | • The cost less residual value of the
  53 | intangible asset is amortised over its
  54 | useful life
  55 | • The
  56 | amortisation
  57 | method
  58 | should
  59 | reflect the pattern in which the entity
  60 | will
  61 | consume
  62 | the
  63 | asset’s
  64 | future
  65 | economic benefits
  66 | • If the pattern cannot be identified
  67 | reliably,
  68 | the
  69 | straight
  70 | line
  71 | method
  72 | should be used
  73 | • The amortisation charge is recognised
  74 | in profit or loss unless another IFRS
  75 | requires it to be included in the cost of
  76 | another asset
  77 | • The amortisation period should be
  78 | reviewed at least annually and any
  79 | change accounted for as a change in
  80 | accounting estimate
  81 | • The asset should also be assessed for
  82 | impairment in accordance with IAS 36

   6 | Introduction
   7 | 1.1
   8 | Research
   9 | • Activities aimed at obtaining

  12 | search
  13 | for
  14 | evaluation,
  15 | final
  16 | selection
  17 | of
  18 | research
  19 | findings or other knowledge;
  20 | for alternatives of materials,
  21 | devices, products, processes,
  22 | systems or services
  23 | • The
  24 | formulation,
  25 | design
  26 | evaluation and final selection
  27 | of possible alternatives for
  28 | new or improved materials,
  29 | devices, products, systems or
  30 | services
  31 | Development
  32 | • The design, construction and
  33 | testing
  34 | of
  35 | pre-production,
  36 | chosen
  37 | alternatives
  38 | for
  39 | new/improved
  40 | materials

  57 | Your Notes
  58 | 166
  59 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
  60 | III. FUNDAMENTAL INTANGIBLE ASSETS
  61 | 1. Research and development
  62 | Accounting treatment
  63 | 1.2
  64 | Research expenditure: write off as incurred to the statement of profit or loss
  65 | (expense off).
  66 | Development expenditure: recognise as an ITA if, and only if, an entity can
  67 | demonstrate all of following criteria (PIRATE):
  68 | •
  69 | Probable future economic benefits
  70 | •
  71 | Intention to complete and use or sell the asset
  72 | •
  73 | Resources adequate
  74 | •
  75 | Ability to use or sell
  76 | •
  77 | Technical feasibility
  78 | •
  79 | Expenditure can be measured reliably
  80 | Note: All expenditure related to an intangible which does not meet the criteria for
  81 | recognition either as an identifiable intangible asset or as goodwill arising on an
  82 | acquisition should be expensed as incurred. For example:
  83 | •
  84 | Start-up costs
  85 | •
  86 | Training costs
  87 | •
  88 | Advertising costs
  89 | •
  90 | Business relocation costs

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 167
   3 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
   4 | III. FUNDAMENTAL INTANGIBLE ASSETS
   5 | 1. Research and development
   6 | Accounting treatment
   7 | 1.2
   8 | Intention
   9 | Its intention to complete the intangible asset and use or sell it
  10 | which is showed through plan, press release, …
  11 | Resource
  12 | Resources
  13 | (technical,
  14 | financial
  15 | and
  16 | other
  17 | resources)
  18 | are
  19 | adequate and available to complete and use the asset.
  20 | Ability to use or sell
  21 | Intangible assets should be useful and serve for some purpose
  22 | of entity or demand of other parties.
  23 | Technical feasibility
  24 | Technical feasibility of completing the intangible asset (so that
  25 | it will be available for use or sale).
  26 | Expenditure measurement
  27 | Expenditure can be measured reliably.
  28 | Probable future economic benefits
  29 | The existence of a market for the output of the intangible asset
  30 | or the usefulness of the intangible asset to the business.
  31 | P
  32 | E

  34 | R
  35 | A
  36 | T
  37 | Your Notes
  38 | 168
  39 | INTANGIBLE ASSET
  40 | III. FUNDAMENTAL INTANGIBLE ASSETS
  41 | 1. Research and development
  42 | Example
  43 | Dempsey Co's year end is 30 September 20X5. Dempsey Co commenced a new
  44 | project to produce a new pharmaceutical drug and the research stage of the
  45 | project lasted from 1 October 20X4 to 31 December 20X4 and incurred $60,000
  46 | of costs. The development stage of the project was commenced on 1 January
  47 | 20X5. Expenditure of $40,000 per month was incurred until the project was
  48 | completed on 30 June 20X5 when the drug went into immediate production.
  49 | The directors became confident of the project's success on 1 March 20X5. The
  50 | drug is expected to generate benefits for five years.
  51 | Required:
  52 | What is the carrying amount of any intangible asset recognized in respect of
  53 | the project and what is the total amount Dempsey Co will charge to profit or
  54 | loss for the year ended 30 September 20X5?

   7 | • Expenses for October to December 20X4 (Research stage) = $60,000

   9 | $80,000

  11 | 4 = $160,000

  13 | • Carrying amount of the asset at 30 September 20X5 = $160,000 – $8,000 =
  14 | $152,000
  15 | • Amortization expense for the period = $8,000
  16 | • Expenses taken to the statement of profit or loss = $60,000 + $80,000 +
  17 | $8,000 = $148,000

  57 | Answer
  58 | Question 3:
  59 | The correct answer is $5.5m
  60 | •
  61 | The project costs incurred between 1 April 20X6 and 31 December 20X6 cover
  62 | 9 months. In addition, the project did not meet the capitalization criteria until
  63 | 30 June, meaning that the first three months of the year were research
  64 | expenditures which were charged to the P&L is $15m x 3/9 months = $5m.
  65 | •
  66 | The development expenditure would be capitalized: $15m x 6/9 months =
  67 | $10m.
  68 | •
  69 | As the project was completed and began to generate revenue from 1 January
  70 | 20X7, the capitalized development expenditure should be amortised in 5
  71 | years.
  72 | •
  73 | Therefore, the amortization expense for three months which should be
  74 | charged to the P&L is $10m / 5 years x 3/12 months = $0.5m.
  75 | The total charge to the P&L in respect of project 324 for the year ended 31
  76 | March 20X7 is $5m + $0.5m = $5.5m

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 181
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | I. DEFINITION AND TERMINOLOGIES
   5 | 1. Definition of Impairment loss
   6 | An impairment loss is the amount by which the carrying amount of an asset or a
   7 | cash – generating unit exceeds its recoverable amount.
   8 | 2. Some relevant terminologies
   9 | Terminologies
  10 | Definition
  11 | Carrying
  12 | amount
  13 | Carrying amount is the amount at which the asset is recognized
  14 | after deducting accumulated depreciation and any impairment
  15 | losses in the statement of financial position.
  16 | Recoverable
  17 | amount
  18 | Recoverable amount is the higher of an asset's fair value less
  19 | costs of selling price and its value in use
  20 | Fair value
  21 | Fair value is the price that would be received to sell an asset or
  22 | paid to transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between
  23 | market participants at the measurement date
  24 | Value in use
  25 | The present value of the future cash flows expected to be
  26 | derived from an asset or cash-generating unit
  27 | Cash
  28 | generating unit
  29 | A cash generating unit Is the smallest identifiable group of
  30 | assets that generates cash inflows that are largely independent
  31 | of the cash inflows from other assets or groups of assets
  32 | Your Notes
  33 | 182
  34 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  35 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  36 | 1. Scope of IAS 36
  37 | IAS 36 applied for all assets except following items:
  38 | Exception of
  39 | IAS 36

================================================================================
PAGE 92
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 183
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   5 | 2. Impairment assessment
   6 | An enterprise should assess at each reporting date
   7 | Whether there is any
   8 | indication that an asset
   9 | may be impaired
  10 | Irrespective of any indication
  11 | of impairment, an entity shall
  12 | also:
  13 | Test in case of intangible
  14 | assets
  15 | having
  16 | indefinite
  17 | life or under development
  18 | Test goodwill acquired in
  19 | a
  20 | business
  21 | combination
  22 | for impairment annually
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 184
  25 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  26 | 2. Impairment assessment
  27 | IAS 36 suggests how indications of a possible impairment of assets might be
  28 | recognized. The suggestions are based largely on common sense.
  29 | Indications of impairment
  30 | External sources
  31 | Decline in asset’s market value
  32 | Adverse effect in the technological market, economic or
  33 | legal environment in which the enterprise operates
  34 | Increase in market interest rates
  35 | Internal sources
  36 | Obsolescence or physical damage
  37 | Significant changes with an adverse effect in the extent to
  38 | which, or manner in which, an asset is used or is expected to
  39 | be used
  40 | Economic performance and expected net cash flows of an
  41 | asset are worse than expected
  42 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS

PAGE 93
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 185
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | 2. Impairment assessment
   5 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   6 | Example 1:
   7 | Company X owns a fleet of 400 articulated diesel buses. Environmentalists are
   8 | pressing the government to require public transit companies to switch to hybrid
   9 | buses. You expect to earn $14 million from the buses each year for next 6 years.
  10 | There is a 51% chance that the new laws will be passed which will reduce your
  11 | revenues from the fleet by 35%. In this case, an adverse effect in the legal
  12 | environment in which the enterprise operates is occurred, this is an indicator of
  13 | impairment of assets relating to diesel buses.
  14 | Note: Impairment review is compulsory for:
  15 | •
  16 | Intangible assets with an indefinite useful life
  17 | •
  18 | Intangible assets not yet available for use
  19 | •
  20 | CGUs to which goodwill has been allocated
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 186
  23 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  24 | 3. Recognition of impairment
  25 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  26 | An impairment loss is recognized whenever recoverable amount is below carrying
  27 | amount.
  28 | An impairment loss should be
  29 | recognized
  30 | as
  31 | an
  32 | expense
  33 | in
  34 | SOPL
  35 | immediately,
  36 | unless
  37 | the
  38 | asset
  39 | is
  40 | carried
  41 | at
  42 | revalued
  43 | amount
  44 | (recognized
  45 | directly
  46 | against any revaluation surplus.
  47 | Any over and above amount as
  48 | expense in SOPL).
  49 | Adjust depreciation for future
  50 | periods.
  51 | Recoverable amount < Carrying amount

================================================================================
PAGE 94
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 187
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | 4. Measurement of impairment
   5 | Measuring recoverable amount
   6 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   7 | Exam focus: Impairment loss
   8 | Example 2:
   9 | A company that extracts natural gas and oil has a drilling platform in the
  10 | Caspian Sea. It is required by legislation of the country concerned to remove
  11 | and dismantle the platform at the end of its useful life.
  12 | Accordingly, the company has included an amount in its accounts for removal
  13 | and dismantling costs and is depreciating this amount over the platform's
  14 | expected life.
  15 | The company is carrying out an exercise to establish whether there has been an
  16 | impairment of the platform.
  17 | (a)
  18 | Its carrying amount in the statement of financial position is $12m.
  19 | (b)
  20 | The company has received an offer of $11.2m for the platform from
  21 | another oil company. The bidder would take over the responsibility (and
  22 | costs) for dismantling and removing the platform at the end of its life.
  23 | (c)
  24 | The present value of the estimated cash flows from the platform's
  25 | continued use is $13.2m (before adjusting for dismantling costs).
  26 | (d)
  27 | The carrying amount in the statement of financial position for the
  28 | provision for dismantling and removal is currently $2.4m.
  29 | Required: What should be the value of the drilling platform in the statement of
  30 | financial position, and what, if anything, is the impairment loss?
  31 | Your Notes
  32 | 188
  33 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  34 | 4. Measurement of impairment
  35 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  36 | Example 2 (Answer):
  37 | Step 1: identify the carrying amount
  38 | From the given information, the carrying amount of the platform is $12m
  39 | Step 2: Determine the recoverable amount.
  40 | •
  41 | Fair value less costs of disposal = $11.2m
  42 | •
  43 | Value in use: PV of cash flows from use less the carrying amount of the
  44 | provision /liability = $13.2m – $2.4m = $10.8m
  45 | Recoverable amount = Higher of these two amounts = $11.2m
  46 | Step 3: Compare the calculated recoverable amount with the carrying amount
  47 | Recoverable amount = $11.2m
  48 | Higher amount of
  49 | Carrying value = $12m
  50 | →
  51 | Impairment loss = recoverable amount – carrying amount
  52 | = $12m - $11.2m
  53 | = $0.8m (be recognised an expense in SOPL)
  54 | Step 4: Identify the carrying amount after impairment review:
  55 | Lower of the recoverable amount and carrying amount (before impairment
  56 | review): $11.2m

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 189
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | 5. Reversal of an impairment loss
   5 | In some cases, the recoverable amount of an asset that has previously been
   6 | impaired could be higher than the asset's current carrying amount. Therefore, there
   7 | might have been a reversal of some of the previous impairment loss.
   8 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   9 | Indications of impairment
  10 | Increases in the asset’s market value
  11 | Favorable changes in the technological, market, economic
  12 | or legal environment
  13 | Decreases in interest rates
  14 | Favorable changes in the use of the asset
  15 | Improvements in the asset’s economic performance.
  16 | Expected net cash flows of an asset are better than
  17 | expected
  18 | Indicators of impairment reversal
  19 | 5.1
  20 | External
  21 | sources
  22 | Internal
  23 | sources
  24 | Your Notes
  25 | 190
  26 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  27 | 5. Reversal of an impairment loss
  28 | Accounting treatment
  29 | 5.2
  30 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  31 | Note: Goodwill shall not be reversed in a subsequent period
  32 | Impaired assets should be reviewed at each reporting date to see whether there
  33 | are indications that the impairment has reversed.
  34 | A reversal of an
  35 | impairment loss is
  36 | recognised immediately
  37 | as income in profit or
  38 | loss.
  39 | If the original impairment
  40 | was charged against the
  41 | revaluation surplus, it is
  42 | recognised as SOCI to the
  43 | revaluation reserve.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 191
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   5 | Example 3:
   6 | Tusco company purchased a building on 1 January 20X4 at a cost of $60m. At
   7 | that date, the asset had an estimated useful life of ten years. Tusco accounts
   8 | for building on the basis of depreciated historical cost. At 31 December 20X5,
   9 | the asset was subject to an impairment review and had a recoverable amount
  10 | of $32m. At 31 December 20X8, the circumstances which caused the original
  11 | impairment to be recognized have reversed with the result that the recoverable
  12 | amount is now $80m.
  13 | Required: Describe the impact on the financial statements of the two
  14 | impairment reviews.
  15 | 5. Reversal of an impairment loss
  16 | Accounting treatment
  17 | 5.2
  18 | Your Notes
  19 | 192
  20 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  21 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  22 | Example 3 (Answer):
  23 | Step 1: Calculate impairment loss in the past
  24 | Impairment review at 31 December 20X5
  25 | $mil
  26 | Asset cost at 1 January 20X5
  27 | 60
  28 | Depreciation to 31 December 20X5 ($60m × 2/10)
  29 | (12)
  30 | Carrying amount at 31 December 20X5
  31 | 48
  32 | Recoverable amount = $32m => Impairment loss = $16m ($48m – $32m)
  33 | After impairment review, the asset is written down to $32m and the
  34 | impairment loss of $16m is charged to profit or loss.
  35 | The depreciation charge per annum in future periods will be $4m ($32m × 1/8).
  36 | Step 2: Identify whether a reversal is occurred
  37 | Impairment review at 31 December 20X8
  38 | $mil
  39 | Asset cost at 31 December 20X5
  40 | 32
  41 | Depreciation to 31 December 20X8 ($4m x 3)
  42 | (12)
  43 | Carrying amount at 31 December 20X8
  44 | 20
  45 | Recoverable amount = $80m
  46 | => There has been no impairment loss (carrying amount < recoverable
  47 | amount). In fact, there has been a reversal of the first impairment loss.
  48 | 5. Reversal of an impairment loss
  49 | Accounting treatment
  50 | 5.2
