   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 193
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | II. IAS 36 – IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   5 | Example 3 (Answer):
   6 | Step 3: Determine reversal amount
   7 | $mil
   8 | Asset historical cost at 1 January 20X4
   9 | 60
  10 | Historical cost depreciation to 31 December 20X8
  11 | (30)
  12 | (60×5/10)
  13 | Historical carrying amount at 31 December 20X8
  14 | 30
  15 | Impaired carrying amount (step 2)
  16 | (20)
  17 | Reversal of impairment loss
  18 | 10
  19 | •
  20 | The reversal of the loss is recognized by increasing the asset by $10m and
  21 | recognizing a gain of $10m as an income on SOPL. The building written
  22 | back to $30m.
  23 | •
  24 | The remaining impairment loss ($6m) has still included in SOPL of previous
  25 | year. As the carrying amount has been reduced since 1st impairment, the
  26 | depreciation expenses for the year following has also been reduced.
  27 | Original depreciation expenses = 60 : 10 = $6m
  28 | Depreciation expenses since 1st impairment = 32 : 8 = $4m
  29 | Net impact reduced in expenses each year = 6 -4 = $2m
  30 | Net impact for 3 years (from 20X5 to 20X8) = 3 x 2 = $6m
  31 | 5. Reversal of an impairment loss
  32 | Accounting treatment
  33 | 5.2
  34 | Your Notes
  35 | 194
  36 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  37 | 1. When do we use cash generating unit?
  38 | A cash generating unit Is the smallest identifiable group of assets that generates
  39 | cash inflows that are largely independent of the cash inflows from other assets or
  40 | groups of assets.
  41 | III. CASH GENERATING UNIT
  42 | 1
  43 | 2
  44 | 3
  45 | When assessing the impairment of
  46 | assets, it will not always be possible
  47 | to base the impairment review on
  48 | individual assets.
  49 | The value-in-use calculation may be
  50 | impossible on a single asset because
  51 | the individual asset does not generate
  52 | distinguishable cash flows.
  53 | Need using cash generating units
  54 | (CGU)

================================================================================
PAGE 98
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 195
   3 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
   4 | III. CASH GENERATING UNIT
   5 | Impairment calculation of total impairment calculation is done by:
   6 | Comparing the carrying amount of the CGU
   7 | to the recoverable amount of the CGU
   8 | Assuming the cash generating unit is one
   9 | asset
  10 | As goodwill acquired in a business combination does not generate cash flows
  11 | independently of the other assets, it must be allocated to each of the
  12 | acquirer’s cash generating units
  13 | 2. Accounting treatment for impairment of cash generating units
  14 | Your Notes
  15 | 196
  16 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  17 | III. CASH GENERATING UNIT
  18 | Impairment of a CGU
  19 | The impairment loss on a CGU is allocated in the following order:
  20 | Total
  21 | impairment
  22 | of CGU
  23 | Step 01
  24 | Step
  25 | 02
  26 | Step 03
  27 | Any asset that is impaired
  28 | (e.g.
  29 | if
  30 | an
  31 | asset
  32 | was
  33 | specifically damaged)
  34 | Goodwill
  35 | in
  36 | the
  37 | cash
  38 | generating unit
  39 | All other assets in the CGU
  40 | on a pro rata basis on
  41 | carrying value
  42 | Note:
  43 | •
  44 | Considerations on reversal of an impairment loss for a CGU. First is asset
  45 | other than goodwill on a pro-rata basis based on the carrying amount of each
  46 | asset in the units.
  47 | •
  48 | An impairment loss recognized for goodwill shall not be reversed in a
  49 | subsequent periods
  50 | 2. Accounting treatment for impairment of cash generating units

  19 | Following a recession, an impairment review has estimated the recoverable

  25 | 2. Accounting treatment for impairment of cash generating units

  27 | 198
  28 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  29 | III. CASH GENERATING UNIT
  30 | Example 4 (Answer):
  31 | Total impairment of CGU:
  32 | Recoverable amount of the cash – generating unit – Carrying amount
  33 | = $106m – $70m = $36m.
  34 | Company should allocate in the following order
  35 | Step 1: Determine any asset that is impaired (e.g. if an asset was specifically
  36 | damaged) In this case, the building is identified as an impaired asset
  37 | Carrying amount: $52m
  38 | Recoverable amount (higher of Fair value less cost and value in use): $40m
  39 | => Specific impairment of building: $12m
  40 | Step 2: Calculate impairment of goodwill in the cash generating unit
  41 | Total impairment – specific impairment = $36m – $12m = $24m
  42 | => Goodwill is impaired by $20m and reduced to zero
  43 | Step 3: Identify impairment of other assets in unit on pro-rata basis.
  44 | Impairment remaining = $24m – $20m = $4m
  45 | Impaired value of plant and equipment = $16m – $4m = $12m
  46 | As current assets are out of scope IAS 36, no impairment should be allocated to
  47 | these.
  48 | 2. Accounting treatment for impairment of cash generating units

   4 | Chapter 7 Quiz
   5 | Answer
   6 | Question 1:
   7 | The correct answer is $7,687.
   8 | • Fair value less costs of disposal: (78,000– 2,500) = $75,500
   9 | • Value in use: 30,000 x 1/1.08 + 30,000 x 1/1.082 + 30,000 x 1/1.083 =
  10 | 77,313
  11 | • Under IAS 36 Impairment of Assets, the recoverable amount of an asset is
  12 | the higher of fair value less costs of disposal and value in use. Thus,
  13 | recoverable amount is $77,313.
  14 | • If the recoverable amount of an asset is lower than the carrying amount,
  15 | the carrying amount should be reduced by the difference (ie the
  16 | impairment loss).
  17 | • The carrying amount is $85,000, which is higher than the recoverable
  18 | amount, so impairment: $85,000 - $77,313 = $7,687.
  19 | Your Notes
  20 | 204
  21 | IMPAIRMENT OF ASSETS
  22 | Chapter 7 Quiz
  23 | Answer
  24 | Question 2:
  25 | The correct answer is $350,000.
  26 | • The value in use of the plant is $2.6 million, which is determined by the
  27 | value of the offer Bazaar Co has received from a company in Japan
  28 | interest in buying the plant.
  29 | • Costs of disposal is $50,000, which is the estimated cost of shipping
  30 | the plant to Japan .
  31 | The fair value less costs of disposal: $2,700,000 - $50,000 = $2,650,000
  32 | • This amount is lower than the value in use of the plant ($2.6 million), so
  33 | the recoverable amount is $2.65 million (is the higher of fair value less
  34 | costs of disposal and value in use)
  35 | • The carrying amount of the plant given in the question is $3 million.
  36 | The impairment loss: $3,000,000 - $2,650,000 = $350,000

  40 | • Step 1: There is no specific impaired asset.

  43 | • Step 3: Except net current assets that are not impaired, the remaining
  44 | impairment loss is allocated for all other assets in the CGU on a pro rata
  45 | basis on carrying value.

   3 | INVESTMENT PROPERTY AND NON-CURRENT ASSET
   4 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
   5 | I. IAS 40 INVESTMENT PROPERTY
   6 | 1. Scope of investment property
   7 | IAS 40 Investment property relates to property (land or a building) held (by the
   8 | owner or by the lessee as
   9 | right-of-use asset) to earn rentals or for capital
  10 | appreciation or both.
  11 | • Property held for use in the production or supply of goods or services or for
  12 | administrative purposes (IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment)
  13 | • Property held for sale in the ordinary course of business or in the process of
  14 | construction of development for such sale (IAS 2 Inventories)
  15 | • Property being constructed or developed on behalf of third parties (IAS 11
  16 | Construction Contracts)
  17 | • Property leased to another entity under a finance lease (IFRS 16 Lease)
  18 | • Owner-occupied property (IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment)
  19 | • Land held for capital appreciation;
  20 | • Land held for undecided future use;
  21 | • Buildings leased out under an operating lease;
  22 | • Vacant buildings held to be leased out under an operating lease;…
  23 | Investment property
  24 | NOT Investment property
  25 | Your Notes
  26 | 210
  27 | INVESTMENT PROPERTY AND NON-CURRENT ASSET
  28 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
  29 | I. IAS 40 INVESTMENT PROPERTY
  30 | 2. Recognition of investment property
  31 | Investment property should be recognized as an asset when two conditions are
  32 | met:
  33 | Example: A company pays $500,000 for a building for leasing out purpose,
  34 | and expects its useful life of twenty five years. Based on this information,
  35 | building is recognized as an investment under IAS 40 because:
  36 | •
  37 | It can bring future economic benefits from rental income for company
  38 | •
  39 | The cost of building can be measured reliably (at $500,000)
  40 | Future economic
  41 | benefits associated
  42 | with the item will
  43 | flow to the entity
  44 | The cost of the
  45 | investment property
  46 | can be measured
  47 | reliably

  32 | Cost
  33 | model
  34 | (Similar
  35 | to PPE)

  37 | model

  44 | if this results in a more consistent presentation.

  46 | Investment property using revaluation value model without depreciation
  47 | charged (so this model is called fair value model) This is the difference
  48 | between Investment property under IAS 40 and PPE under IAS 16 using
  49 | revaluation model.
  50 | •
  51 | Fair value at each reporting date with gains/losses in P&L

  53 | No depreciation charged in this model

  14 | 40
  15 | = $75,000 (Statement of profit or loss)

  18 | financial position)

  21 | financial position)

  23 | adjustment (Statement of profit or loss)

   3 | 1. Introduction of non-current asset held for sale
   4 | Scope of non-current asset held for sale
   5 | 1.2
   6 | Criteria of non-current assets held for sale
   7 | Management must be committed to a plan to sell the
   8 | asset
   9 | There must be an active program to locate a buyer
  10 | Sale at a price that is reasonable in relation to its
  11 | current FV
  12 | The sale should be expected to take place within one
  13 | year from the date of classification
  14 | It is unlikely that significant changes to the plan will
  15 | be made or that the plan will be withdrawn
  16 | Available for immediate sale in its present condition
  17 | Highly
  18 | probable
  19 | for sale
  20 | (within 12
  21 | months)
  22 | A non-current asset (or disposal group) should be classified as held for sale if its
  23 | carrying amount will be recovered principally through a sale transaction rather than
  24 | through continuing use.

   1 | Your Notes

   3 | 1. Introduction of non-current asset held for sale
   4 | INVESTMENT PROPERTY AND NON-CURRENT ASSET
   5 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
   6 | II. IFRS 5 NON-CURRENT ASSET
   7 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
   8 | Measurement
   9 | 1.3
  10 | Example 2:
  11 | Tusco purchases a bus for $200,000 on 1 January 20X4 for its business, and
  12 | the bus is expected to have a useful life of 10 years, with none residual value.
  13 | At 31 December 20X5, Tusco has no longer business demand for the bus, and
  14 | decides to sell it. The market price of the same bus at that time is $180,000,
  15 | and the selling expense is $1000.
  16 | Required:
  17 | (a) Show how the bus would be presented in the financial statements at 31
  18 | December 20X5.
  19 | (b) At 30 June 20X6, due to the unexpected transportation demand of Tusco’s
  20 | staffs, it decides to use the bus. Tusco estimates the recoverable amount of
  21 | the bus at 30 June 20X6 is $170,000. Show how the bus would be presented
  22 | in the financial statements at 30 June 20X6
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 222
  25 | 1. Introduction of non-current asset held for sale
  26 | INVESTMENT PROPERTY AND NON-CURRENT ASSET
  27 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
  28 | II. IFRS 5 NON-CURRENT ASSET
  29 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
  30 | Measurement
  31 | 1.3
  32 | Answer:
  33 | (a) At 31 December 20X5
  34 | Step 1: Calculate depreciation of the bus at 31 December 20X5:
  35 | Depreciation = $200,000
  36 | 10
  37 | x 2 = $40,000
  38 | Step 2: Calculate carrying amount = $200,000 - $40,000 = $ 160,000
  39 | Step 3: Calculate fair value less cost of disposal = $180,000 - $1,000 = $179,000
  40 | Step 4: Compare the value between step 2 and 3:
  41 | The lower amount is $160,000 (Statement of financial position)
  42 | (b) At 30 June 20X6
  43 | Step 1: The depreciation at 30 June 20X6 when the bus is not classified as held for
  44 | sale:
  45 | Depreciation = $200,000
  46 | 10
  47 | x 2.5 = $50,000
  48 | Step 2: Calculate carrying amount = $200,000 - $50,000 = $ 150,000
  49 | Step 3: The recoverable amount: $170,000
  50 | Step 4: Compare the value between step 2 and 3:
  51 | The lower amount is $150,000 (Statement of financial position)

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 223
   3 | INVESTMENT PROPERTY AND NON-CURRENT ASSET
   4 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
   5 | II. IFRS 5 NON-CURRENT ASSET
   6 | HELD FOR SALE & DISCONTINUED OPERATIONS
   7 | 2. Disposal groups
   8 | A group of assets to be disposed of, by sale or otherwise, together as a group in
   9 | a single transaction, and liabilities directly associated with those assets that will
  10 | be transferred in the transaction.
  11 | The disposal group may be a group of CGUs (cash-generating units), a single CGU,
  12 | or part of a CGU
  13 | 3. Discontinued operation
  14 | Discontinued operation: A component of an entity that either has been disposed of
  15 | or is classified as held for sale and:
  16 | (a) Represents a separate major line of business or geographical area of
  17 | operations;
  18 | (b) Is part of a single coordinated plan to dispose of separate major lines of
  19 | business or geographical areas of operations; or
  20 | (c)
  21 | Is a subsidiary acquired exclusively with a view to resale.
  22 | Component of an entity: A part that has operations and cash flows that can be
  23 | clearly distinguished, operationally and for financial reporting purposes, from the
  24 | rest of the entity.
  25 | KEY POINT

   3 | 1. Terminologies
   4 | Terminologies
   5 | Definition
   6 | Inventories
   7 | ▪Assets are held for sale in the ordinary course of business
   8 | ▪Assets are in the process of production for such sale; or
   9 | ▪Assets are in the form of materials or supplies to be
  10 | consumed in the production process or in the rendering of
  11 | services
  12 | Net realizable value
  13 | The estimated selling price
  14 | in the
  15 | ordinary course
  16 | of
  17 | business less the estimated costs of completion and the
  18 | estimated costs necessary to make the sale.
  19 | Fair value
  20 | The price that would be received to sell an asset or paid to
  21 | transfer a liability in orderly transaction between market
  22 | participants at the measurement date.
  23 | Fixed production
  24 | overheads
  25 | Indirect costs of production that remain relatively constant
  26 | regardless of the volume of production.
  27 | Ex: The cost of factory management and administration.
  28 | Variable production
  29 | overheads
  30 | Indirect costs of production that vary directly, or nearly
  31 | directly, with the volume of production
  32 | Ex: Indirect materials and labor.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 251
   3 | 2. Measurement of inventory
   4 | I. IAS 2 – INVENTORY
   5 | (*) Other costs directly attributable: Transport, handling and any other cost
   6 | directly attributable to the acquisition of finished goods, services and materials
   7 | The standard lists types of cost which would not be included in cost of
   8 | inventories should be recognized as an expense:
   9 | • 'Abnormal amounts of wasted materials, labor or other production costs;
  10 | • Storage costs, unless those costs are necessary in the production process
  11 | before a further production stage;
  12 | • Administrative overheads that do not contribute to bringing inventories to
  13 | their present location and condition; and
  14 | • Selling costs.'

   1 | Your Notes

   3 | INVENTORY AND
   4 | AGRICULTURE
   5 | 4. Measurement of IAS 41
   6 | II. IAS 41 – AGRICULTURE
   7 | Example 1:
   8 | A herd of five 4-year-old pigs was held on 1 January 20X6. On 1 July 20X6 a 4.5-
   9 | year-old pig was purchased for $440. The fair values less estimated point of sale
  10 | costs was:
  11 | •
  12 | 4-year-old pig at 1 January 20X6 $400
  13 | •
  14 | 4.5-year-old pig at 1 July 20X6 $440
  15 | •
  16 | 5-year-old pig at 31 December 20X6 $500
  17 | Required: Calculate the amount that will be taken to the statement of profit or loss
  18 | for the year ended 31 December 20X6
  19 | Answer:
  20 | Step 1: Calculate initial cost of pigs at the beginning of the year Initial cost of pigs
  21 | = 5 × $400 = $2,000
  22 | Step 2: Calculate cost for buying pigs in the year Cost for buying pigs in the year =
  23 | $440
  24 | Step 3: Calculate cost of the pigs at the end of the year
  25 | Cost of the pigs at the end of the year = 6 × $500 = $3,000
  26 | Step 4: Calculate gain/loss from the change in fair value of the pigs = Step 3 –
  27 | (Step 1+ Step 2)
  28 | Gain/loss from the change in fair value of the pigs = $3,000 – ($2,000 + $440) =
  29 | $560 (gain)
  30 | Your Notes
  31 | 260
  32 | INVENTORY AND
  33 | AGRICULTURE
  34 | 4. Measurement of IAS 41
  35 | II. IAS 41 – AGRICULTURE
  36 | Example 2:
  37 | Company Vinas Milk owns a dairy farm. At 1 January 20X8, Company owned
  38 | 10,000 cows worth $2,000 each (rely on market price). At 31 December 20X8,
  39 | Company had 11,000 cows worth $2,100 each. During 20X1 Company sold 20
  40 | million liter of milk at an average price of $2 per liter. When cows are sold at the
  41 | local market, the auctioneer charges a commission of 5%.
  42 | Required: Show extract from the financial statements for 20X1 for these
  43 | activities, assuming that no cows were purchased or sold during the year
  44 | • Revenue from sales of milk (W1)
  45 | • Value of the cows at the end of the year (W2)
  46 | • Gain from increase in the cows (W3)

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 261
   3 | INVENTORY AND
   4 | AGRICULTURE
   5 | 4. Measurement of IAS 41
   6 | II. IAS 41 – AGRICULTURE
   7 | Example 2: Answer
   8 | (W1) Calculate revenue from sales of milk (SOPL)
   9 | Revenue from sales of milk = Selling price of each liter of milk × Total of milk liter
  10 | ⇒Revenue from sales of milk = $2 × 20,000,000 = $40,000,000
  11 | (W2) Calculate the value of the cows at the end of the year (SOFP)
  12 | Because the cows are classified as biological assets ⇒Value of the cows will
  13 | recorded in SOFP
  14 | Value of the cows at the end = Value of each cow × Total number of the cows ×
  15 | (1 – commission)
  16 | ⇒Value of the cows at the end = $2,100 × 11,000 × (1 – 5%) = $21,945,000
  17 | (W3) Calculate gain from an increase in the value of the cows (SOPL)
  18 | Gains and losses arising from changes in the fair value of biological assets are
  19 | recognized in SOPL for the period in which they arise.
  20 | ⇒Gain form increase in value of the cows = Value of the cows at the end – Value
  21 | of the cows at the beginning
  22 | Value of the cows at the beginning of the year = Value of each cow × Total
  23 | number of the cows × (1 – commission)
  24 | ⇒Value of the cows at the begin = $2,000 × 10,000 × (1 – 5%) = $19,000,000
  25 | ⇒Gain from increase in value of the cows = $21,945,000 – $19,000,000 =
  26 | $2,945,000

  18 | operating lease, as the lessee controls the leased asset during the lease term, and

  20 | standard's finance lease method)

   4 | III. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT

   6 | Recognition and measurement
   7 | 1.1
   8 | Initial measurement
   9 | Note:
  10 | Lease term (IFRS 16, Appendix A)
  11 | To calculate the initial value of the liability and right-of-use asset, the lessee must
  12 | consider the length of the lease term. The lease term comprises:
  13 | •
  14 | Non-cancellable periods.
  15 | •
  16 | Periods covered by an option to extend the lease if reasonably certain to be
  17 | exercised.
  18 | •
  19 | Periods covered by an option to terminate the lease if these are reasonably
  20 | certain not to be exercised.
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 280
  23 | LEASE
  24 | III. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT

  26 | Recognition and measurement
  27 | 1.1
  28 | Example 2:
  29 | At the beginning of the year 20X6, Tusco signed into a three-year contract to
  30 | lease a lorry. The contract contains an option to extend for one more year.
  31 | Tusco believes that they will use this option. Lorries have a useful economic
  32 | life of 8 years. Lease payments are $16,000 per year for the initial term and
  33 | $20,000 per year for the option. All payments are due at the end of the year. To
  34 | obtain the lease, Tusco paid initial payment of $4,000. Tusco’s incremental rate
  35 | of borrowing is 5%. Calculate the initial carrying amount of the lease liability
  36 | and the right-of use asset of Tusco for this contract.
  37 | Guidance:
  38 | Step 1: Identify exactly lease term
  39 | Step 2: Calculate lease liability
  40 | Lease liability = Total value of annual payment (in each term of lease) ×
  41 | Discount rate
  42 | Discount rate = 1/(1+r)^y
  43 | •
  44 | r: implicitly rate
  45 | •
  46 | y: time period from year 1 to the year-end of the lease term
  47 | Step 3: Calculate right of use asset (according to above theory).
  48 | Step 4: Record lease liability and right of use asset.

   7 | Step 1: Identify exact lease term

  13 | commence date

  23 | Annual payment

  51 | Right of use asset

  53 | losses
  54 | Recognition and measurement
  55 | 1.1
  56 | Subsequent measurement (After commencement date)
  57 | Note 1: After initial recognition
  58 | • A right-of-use asset will normally be measured on the cost model.
  59 | • It can be measured on the revaluation model if that belongs to an asset group
  60 | that should applies revaluation model under IAS 16, or it is an investment
  61 | property under IAS 40.
  62 | Note 2: When using cost model to measure a right-of-use asset, we must
  63 | calculate depreciation as follows:
  64 | • If ownership of the asset transfers to the lessee at the end of the lease term
  65 | then depreciation should be charged over the asset's useful life.
  66 | • Otherwise, depreciation is charged over the shorter of the useful life and the
  67 | lease term.
  68 | •
  69 | Measured at amortized cost.
  70 | •
  71 | Increase due to interest at constant rate on
  72 | the outstanding obligation.
  73 | •
  74 | Decrease to reflect payment made.

  22 | LEASE
  23 | III. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT
  24 | 2. Lessor accounting
  25 | Notes:
  26 | Examples of single or combined situations that often lead to leasing of property
  27 | that are classified as finance leases are as follows:
  28 | •
  29 | The lease transfers ownership of the asset to the lessee by the end of the
  30 | lease term.
  31 | •
  32 | The lessee has the option to purchase the asset at a price that is expected
  33 | to be sufficiently lower than the fair value at the date of the option
  34 | exercisability.
  35 | •
  36 | The lease term is for the major part of the economic life of the asset even if
  37 | the title is not transferred.
  38 | •
  39 | At the inception of the lease the present value of the lease payments
  40 | amounts to at least substantially all of the fair value of the leased asset.
  41 | •
  42 | The leased assets are of such a specialized nature that only the lessee can
  43 | use them without major modifications.

  15 | 02
  16 | Calculate gain relates to right transferred

  18 | = Total gain – Gain relates to right retained
  19 | Step
  20 | 03
  21 | Record gain relates to right transferred:
  22 | Debit Cash (Sell price)
  23 | Debit Right of use asset
  24 | (carrying amount × discounted lease payment / fair value)
  25 | Credit PPE (Carrying amount)
  26 | Credit Lease Liability (PV of annual Lease payment)
  27 | Credit P&L (Gain relates to right transferred)

  29 | 04

   7 | Step 1: Calculate total gain from sell of asset
   8 | Total gain = $1,300,000 – $900,000 = $400,000
   9 | Step 2: Calculate gain relates to right retained
  10 | Gain relates to right retained = $400,000 × $1,200,000/$1,300,000 = $369,231
  11 | Step 3: Calculate gain relates to right transferred
  12 | Gain relates to right transferred = $400,000 – $369,231 = $30,769

  16 | Credit PPE $900,000 (scenario)
  17 | Credit Lease Liability $1,200,000 (scenario)
  18 | Credit P&L $30,769 (Step 3)

   1 | 301
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | I. INTRODUCTION
   4 | 1. Introduction of IFRS 13
   5 | Introduction and Scope of IFRS 13
   6 | 1.1
   7 | IAS 16 Property, Plant and
   8 | Equipment
   9 | Allows
  10 | entities
  11 | to
  12 | measure
  13 | property, plant and equipment
  14 | at fair value
  15 | Requires
  16 | the
  17 | identifiable
  18 | net
  19 | assets of a subsidiary to be
  20 | measured at fair value at the
  21 | acquisition date
  22 | IFRS 3 Business
  23 | Combinations
  24 | The objective of IFRS 13 is to provide a single source of guidance for fair value
  25 | measurement where it is required by a reporting standard, rather than it being
  26 | spread throughout several reporting standards.
  27 | Many accounting standards require or allow items to be measured at fair value.
  28 | Some examples include:
  29 | The scope of IFRS 13 does not apply to:
  30 | •
  31 | Share-based payment transactions (IFRS 2 Share-based Payments)
  32 | •
  33 | Leases (IFRS 16 Leases)
  34 | •
  35 | Inventories (IAS 2) and Value in use (IAS 36 Impairment of Assets)
  36 | FAIR VALUE
  37 | MEASUREMENT
  38 | 302
  39 | Your Notes
  40 | II. FAIR VALUE
  41 | 1. The definition of fair value
  42 | Market participants are knowledgeable, third parties. When pricing an asset or a
  43 | liability, they would take into account:
  44 | It should be assumed that market participants are not forced into transactions (i.e
  45 | they are not suffering from cash flow shortages)
  46 | IFRS 13 notes that there are various approaches to determining the fair value of an
  47 | asset or liability:
  48 | Condition
  49 | Location
  50 | Fair value is defined as “the price that would be received to sell an asset or paid
  51 | to transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between market participants at
  52 | the measurement date”
  53 | Restrictions in use
  54 | Market approaches (valuation based on recent sales prices)
  55 | Cost approaches (valuations based on replacement cost)
  56 | Income approaches (valuation based on financial forecasts)
  57 | FAIR VALUE
  58 | MEASUREMENT

================================================================================
PAGE 152
================================================================================
   1 | 303
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. The price
   4 | II. FAIR VALUE
   5 | Fair value is a market-based measurement, not one that is entity-specific. As such,
   6 | when determining the price at which an asset would be sold (or the price paid to
   7 | transfer a liability), observable data from active markets should be used where
   8 | possible
   9 | An active market is a market where transactions for the asset or liability occur
  10 | frequently.
  11 | IFRS 13 classifies inputs into valuation techniques into three levels.
  12 | Level 1 inputs are quoted for identical assets in active markets
  13 | 01
  14 | Level 2 inputs are observable prices that are not level 1 inputs.
  15 | This may include:
  16 | •
  17 | Quoted prices for similar assets in active markets
  18 | •
  19 | Quotes prices for identical assets in less active markets
  20 | •
  21 | Observable inputs that are not prices (such as interest rates)
  22 | 02
  23 | Level 3 inputs are unobservable. This could include cash or profit
  24 | forecasts using an entity’s own data
  25 | A significant adjustment to a level 2 input would lead to it being
  26 | categorized as a level 3 input
  27 | 03
  28 | FAIR VALUE
  29 | MEASUREMENT
  30 | 304
  31 | Your Notes
  32 | 2. The price
  33 | II. FAIR VALUE
  34 | Priority is given to level 1 inputs. The lowest priority is given to level 3 inputs
  35 | Inputs to determine fair value
  36 | IFRS 13 gives the following examples of inputs used to determine fair value:
  37 | Asset
  38 | Example
  39 | Level 1
  40 | Equity shares in a
  41 | listed entity
  42 | Unadjusted quoted prices in an
  43 | active market
  44 | Level 2
  45 | Building held and
  46 | used
  47 | Price per square meter for the
  48 | building from observable market
  49 | data, such as observed transactions
  50 | for similar buildings in similar
  51 | locations
  52 | Level 3
  53 | Cash-generating unit
  54 | Profit or cash flow forecast using
  55 | own data
  56 | FAIR VALUE
  57 | MEASUREMENT

================================================================================
   1 | 305
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. The price
   4 | II. FAIR VALUE
   5 | Example 1:
   6 | Bak has an investment property that is measured at fair value. This property is
   7 | rented out on short-term leases
   8 | The directors wish to fair value the property by estimating the present value of
   9 | the net cash flows that the property will generate for Bak. They argue that this
  10 | best reflects the way in which the building will generate economic benefits for
  11 | Bak.
  12 | The building is unique, although there have been many sales of similar buildings
  13 | in the local area.
  14 | Required:
  15 | Discuss whether the valuation technique suggested by the directors complies
  16 | with International Reporting Standards
  17 | FAIR VALUE
  18 | MEASUREMENT
  19 | 306
  20 | Your Notes
  21 | 2. The price
  22 | II. FAIR VALUE
  23 | Example 1: (Answer)
  24 | The directors’ estimate of the future net cash flows that the building will
  25 | generate is a level 3 input (e.g. Level 3 inputs are unobservable. This could
  26 | include cash or profit forecasts using an entity’s own data).
  27 | IFRS 13 gives the lowest priority to level 3 inputs. These should not be used if
  28 | level 1 or level 2 inputs exist.
  29 | Observable data about the recent sales prices of similar properties is a level 2
  30 | input. The fair value of the building should therefore be based on these prices,
  31 | with adjustments made as necessary to reflect the specific location and
  32 | condition of Baklava’s building
  33 | FAIR VALUE
  34 | MEASUREMENT

================================================================================
PAGE 154
================================================================================
   1 | 307
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 3. Markets
   4 | II. FAIR VALUE
   5 | The price received when an asset is sold (or paid when a liability is transferred)
   6 | may differ depending on the specific market where the transaction occurs.
   7 | Title
   8 | Principal
   9 | market
  10 | The entity must be able to access the principal
  11 | market at the measurement date. This means that
  12 | the principal market for the same asset can differ
  13 | between entities
  14 | The principal market is the market with the
  15 | greatest activity for the asset or liability being
  16 | measured
  17 | IFRS 13 says that fair value should be measured
  18 | by reference to the principal market
  19 | FAIR VALUE
  20 | MEASUREMENT
  21 | 308
  22 | Your Notes
  23 | 3. Markets
  24 | II. FAIR VALUE
  25 | Most advantageous market
  26 | If there is no principal market, then fair value is measured by reference to
  27 | prices in the most advantageous market
  28 | The most advantageous market is the one that maximises the net amount
  29 | received from selling an asset (or minimizes the amount paid to transfer a
  30 | liability)
  31 | Transaction costs (such as legal and broker fees) will play a role in deciding
  32 | which market is most advantageous. However, fair value is not adjusted for
  33 | transaction costs because they are a characteristic of the market, rather than
  34 | the asset
  35 | FAIR VALUE
  36 | MEASUREMENT

================================================================================
PAGE 155
================================================================================
   1 | 309
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 3. Markets
   4 | II. FAIR VALUE
   5 | Example 2:
   6 | An asset is sold in two different active markets at different prices. An entity
   7 | enters into transactions in both markets and can access the price in those
   8 | markets for the asset at the measurement date as follows:
   9 | Required:
  10 | What is the fair value of the asset if:
  11 | (a)
  12 | Market 1 the principal market for the asset?
  13 | (b)
  14 | No principal market can be determined?
  15 | Market 1
  16 | $
  17 | Market 2
  18 | $
  19 | Price
  20 | 26
  21 | 25
  22 | Transaction costs
  23 | (3)
  24 | (1)
  25 | Transport costs
  26 | (2)
  27 | (2)
  28 | Net price received
  29 | 21
  30 | 22
  31 | FAIR VALUE
  32 | MEASUREMENT
  33 | 310
  34 | Your Notes
  35 | 3. Markets
  36 | II. FAIR VALUE
  37 | Example 2: (Answer)
  38 | (a) If Market 1 is the principal market then the fair value would be measured
  39 | using the price that would be received in that market less transport costs. The
  40 | fair value would therefore be $24 ($26 - $2). Transaction costs are ignored as
  41 | they are not a characteristic of the asset
  42 | (b) If neither market is the principal market for the asset then the fair value
  43 | would be measured in the most advantageous market. The most advantageous
  44 | market that maximises the net amount received from the sale.
  45 | The net amount received in Market 2 ($22) is higher than the net amount
  46 | received in Market 1 ($21). Market 2 is therefore the most advantageous
  47 | market. This results in a fair value measurement of $23 ($25 - $2). IFRS 13
  48 | specifies that transaction costs play a role when determining which market is
  49 | most advantageous but that they are not factored into the fair value
  50 | measurement itself
  51 | FAIR VALUE
  52 | MEASUREMENT

================================================================================
   1 | 311
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 1. Non-financial assets
   4 | III. NON-FINANCIAL ASSETS
   5 | Financial assets
   6 | Contractual rights to receive cash
   7 | (such as receivables)
   8 | Investment in equity shares
   9 | Non-financial assets
  10 | Property, plant and equipment
  11 | Intangible assets
  12 | FAIR VALUE
  13 | MEASUREMENT
  14 | 312
  15 | Your Notes
  16 | 2. The fair value of a non-financial asset
  17 | III. NON-FINANCIAL ASSETS
  18 | IFRS 13 says that the fair value of a non-financial asset should be based on its
  19 | highest and best use. The highest and best use of an asset is the use that a
  20 | market participant would adopt in order to maximise its value.
  21 | The current use of a non-financial asset can be assumed to be the highest and
  22 | best use unless evidence exists to the contrary.
  23 | The highest and best use should take into account uses that are:
  24 | IFRS 13 says a use can be legally permissible even if it is not legally approved
  25 | Physical possible
  26 | Legally permissible
  27 | Financially feasible
  28 | FAIR VALUE
  29 | MEASUREMENT

================================================================================
PAGE 157
================================================================================
   1 | 313
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | 2. The fair value of a non-financial asset
   4 | III. NON-FINANCIAL ASSETS
   5 | Example 3:
   6 | Five Quarters has purchased 100% of the ordinary shares of Three Halves and
   7 | is trying to determine the fair value of the net assets at the acquisition date.
   8 | Three Halves owns land that is currently developed for industrial use. The fair
   9 | value of the land if used in a manufacturing operation is $5 million.
  10 | Many nearby plots of land have been developed for residential use (as high-rise
  11 | apartment buildings). The land owned by Three Halves does not have planning
  12 | permission for residential use, although permission has been granted for
  13 | similar plots of land. The fair value of Three Halves’ land as a vacant site for
  14 | residential development is $6 million. However, transformation costs of $0.3
  15 | million would need to be incurred to get the land into this condition.
  16 | Required:
  17 | How should the fair value of the land be determined?
  18 | FAIR VALUE
  19 | MEASUREMENT
  20 | 314
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 2. The fair value of a non-financial asset
  23 | III. NON-FINANCIAL ASSETS
  24 | Example 3: (Answer)
  25 | Land is a non-financial asset. IFRS 13 says that the fair value of a non-financial
  26 | asset should be based on its highest and best use. This is presumed to be its
  27 | current use unless evidence exists to the contrary
  28 | The current use of the asset would suggest a fair value of $5 million.
  29 | However, there is evidence that market participants would be interested in
  30 | developing the land for residential use.
  31 | Residential use of the land is not legally prohibited. Similar plots of land have
  32 | been granted planning permission, so it is likely that this particular plot of land
  33 | will also be granted planning permission.
  34 | If used for residential purposes, the fair value of the land would be $5.7 million
  35 | ($6m - $0.3m)
  36 | It would seem that the land’s highest and best use is for residential
  37 | development. Its fair value is therefore $5.7 million.
  38 | FAIR VALUE
  39 | MEASUREMENT

  47 | that results in the entity reclassifying financial assets.

  49 | interest are not restated.

  51 | versa)

   3 | 3. Objective of Hedge Accounting
   4 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
   5 | The objective of hedge accounting is to represent in the financial statements the
   6 | effect of the risk management activities which seek to address this type of risk.
   7 | Hedge accounting is a choice; where it is applied it will eliminate some of the
   8 | volatility that would otherwise be reported in profit or loss.
   9 | KEY POINT
  10 | If
  11 | hedge
  12 | accounting
  13 | is
  14 | NOT
  15 | used,
  16 | hedged
  17 | items
  18 | and
  19 | hedging
  20 | instruments are accounted for in the same manner as other financial
  21 | assets and liabilities.
  22 | I. IFRS 9 – FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 332
  25 | 4. Terminology in Hedge Accounting
  26 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  27 | Example 2: Forward Contract
  28 | On 1 March 20X9 Abacus Engine Co sold aircraft engines to a customer in
  29 | South Africa, pricing the sale in Rand and providing credit terms of 60 days. In
  30 | order to protect against movements in the Dollar-Rand exchange rate, Abacus
  31 | entered into a forward contract to sell Rand on 29 April 20X9 at a fixed rate.
  32 | •
  33 | The hedged item is the foreign currency receivable.
  34 | •
  35 | The hedging instrument is the forward contract to sell Rand.
  36 | In accounting terms a hedge involves designating one (or more) hedging
  37 | instruments so that the change in their fair value offsets, in whole or in part, the
  38 | change in fair value or the cash flows associated with a hedged item.
  39 | I. IFRS 9 – FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS

   5 | Hedged item
   6 | 4.1
   7 | A hedged item is an item which is exposed to the risk of changes in fair value or
   8 | changes in future cash flows and designated as being hedged.
   9 | Hedged item
  10 | A recognized
  11 | asset or liability
  12 | A net investment in a
  13 | foreign operation
  14 | A highly probable
  15 | forecast transaction
  16 | An unrecognized
  17 | firm commitment
  18 | It can be:

  22 | 4. Terminology in Hedge Accounting
  23 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  24 | Hedging instrument
  25 | 4.2
  26 | A hedging instrument is a designated derivative, or a non-derivative financial
  27 | asset or financial liability, whose fair value or cash flows are expected to offset
  28 | changes in fair value or future cash flows of the hedged item.
  29 | Qualifying instruments include:
  30 | 01
  31 | 03
  32 | 02
  33 | Non-derivative financial assets or liabilities measured at FVTPL
  34 | (with the exception of financial liabilities for which changes in
  35 | fair value due to credit risk are presented in OCI)
  36 | For a hedge of foreign currency risk, the foreign currency risk
  37 | component of a non-derivative financial asset or liability
  38 | (other than an equity investment classified as FVTOCI)
  39 | Derivatives measured at FVTPL (with the exception of some
  40 | written options)

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 335
   3 | 4. Terminology in Hedge Accounting
   4 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
   5 | Hedging relationships
   6 | 4.3
   7 | There are 3 types of hedging relationships:
   8 | A hedge of the exposure to changes in the fair value of a recognized
   9 | asset or liability or an unrecognized firm commitment (or an
  10 | identified portion of such an asset or liability) which:
  11 | •
  12 | is attributable to a particular risk, and
  13 | •
  14 | could affect reported profit or loss
  15 | A hedge of the exposure to variability in cash flows which:
  16 | •
  17 | is attributable to a particular risk associated with a recognized
  18 | asset or liability or a highly probable forecast transaction, and
  19 | •
  20 | could affect reported profit or loss
  21 | Fair value hedges
  22 | Cash flow hedges
  23 | Hedge of a net investment in a foreign operation
  24 | The amount of the reporting entity’s interest in the net assets of the
  25 | foreign operation
  26 | I. IFRS 9 – FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  27 | Your Notes
  28 | 336
  29 | 4. Terminology in Hedge Accounting
  30 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  31 | Hedging relationships
  32 | 4.3
  33 | Example 3: Fair Value Hedge
  34 | Heley acquired 10,000 grams of gold for use in its business for $19.2 million
  35 | on 12 September 20X1. In order to hedge a potential fall in the market price of
  36 | gold Heley entered a future contract on the same date to sell 10,000 gram of
  37 | gold on 31 January 20X2 at a price of $1,920 per gram. This would allow Helley
  38 | to sell the gold at the fixed price and repurchase it at a lower market price in
  39 | the event of a fall in market prices.
  40 | This is a fair value hedge because Heley is hedging exposure to changed in the
  41 | fair value of a recognized asset (inventory) which is attributable to a particular
  42 | risk (changes in the value of gold).
  43 | I. IFRS 9 – FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS

PAGE 169
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 337
   3 | 5. Accounting for Hedge Relationships
   4 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
   5 | Qualifying criteria
   6 | 5.1
   7 | A hedge relationship qualifies for hedge accounting if all the following criteria are met:
   8 | The relationship consists only of eligible hedging instruments and hedged
   9 | items.
  10 | At inception, there is a formal designation and documentation of the
  11 | relationship and of the entity's risk management objective. Documentation must
  12 | identify:
  13 | •
  14 | The hedge item
  15 | •
  16 | The hedging instrument
  17 | •
  18 | The hedged risk
  19 | •
  20 | How the entity will assess that hedge effectiveness requirements are met
  21 | The relationship meets all the hedge effectiveness requirements:
  22 | •
  23 | There is an economic relationship between the hedged item and hedging
  24 | instrument (i.e. they are expected to have offsetting changes in fair value)
  25 | •
  26 | the effect of credit risk does not dominate the fair value changes that result
  27 | from the economic relationship of either the hedged item or hedging
  28 | instrument
  29 | •
  30 | the hedge ratio of the relationship is the same as the hedge ratio of
  31 | the actual quantity of the hedging instrument to the actual quantity of the
  32 | hedged item.
  33 | I. IFRS 9 – FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  34 | Your Notes

  23 | 3. Presentation of compound financial instrument
  24 | A compound instrument is a financial instrument that has characteristics of both
  25 | equity and liabilities, such as a convertible loan.
  26 | It is important to separate the value of the financial liability and equity instruments
  27 | to record them separately initially as well as arising thereafter:
  28 | Value of financial instruments
  29 | Fair value of
  30 | liability
  31 | instrument
  32 | A conversion
  33 | option
  34 | A
  35 | financial
  36 | liability
  37 | Equity
  38 | instruments
  39 | Future cash flows are
  40 | discounted back to the present
  41 | by the prevailing market interest
  42 | rate of similar debt

PAGE 172
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 343
   3 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
   4 | Example:
   5 | Company X issues 8,000 convertible bonds at the start of 20X9. The bonds have a
   6 | four-year term, and are issued at par with a face value of $1,000 per bond. Interest is
   7 | payable annually in arrears at a nominal annual interest rate of 5%. When the bonds
   8 | are issued, the prevailing market interest rate for similar debt without conversion
   9 | options is 8%. What is the value of the equity component in the bond?
  10 | Guidance:
  11 | Step 1: Calculate total proceeds
  12 | Total proceeds = Number of convertible bonds × issued price of each bond
  13 | Step 2: Calculate annual interest payable
  14 | Annual interest payable = Nominal value × Nominal annual interest rate
  15 | Step 3: Calculate present value of interest cash flow and total proceeds
  16 | Present value of annual interest payable = Discount rate × Annual interest payable
  17 | Present value of total proceeds at the end of term = Discount rate × Total proceeds
  18 | Discount rate = 1/(1+r)y – with r: market interest rate for similar debt, y: payment year
  19 | Step 4: Calculate value of financial liability and equity instrument Value of financial
  20 | liability = Present value of (annual interest payable + total proceeds) Value of Equity
  21 | instrument = Total proceeds – Value of financial liability
  22 | III. IAS 32 - PRESENTATION
  23 | OF FINANCIAL INSTRUMENT
  24 | 3. Presentation of compound financial instrument
  25 | Your Notes
  26 | 344
  27 | FINANCIAL INSTRUMENTS
  28 | Example: Answer
  29 | Step 1: Calculate total proceeds
  30 | Total proceeds = 8,000 × $1,000 = $8,000,000
  31 | Step 2: Calculate annual interest payable
  32 | Annual interest payable = $8,000,000 × 5% = $400,000
  33 | Step 3: Present value of annual interest cash flow and total proceeds
  34 | III. IAS 32 - PRESENTATION
  35 | OF FINANCIAL INSTRUMENT
  36 | 3. Presentation of compound financial instrument
  37 | Time
  38 | Annual interest payable
  39 | Discount rate
  40 | [1/(1+r)^y]
  41 | Present value
  42 | Year 1
  43 | $400,000
  44 | 1/(1+8%)^1
  45 | $370,370
  46 | Year 2
  47 | $400,000
  48 | 1/(1+8%)^2
  49 | $342,935
  50 | Year 3
  51 | $400,000
  52 | 1/(1+8%)^3
  53 | $317,533
  54 | Year 4
  55 | $400,000
  56 | 1/(1+8%)^4
  57 | $294,011
  58 | Total
  59 | $1,324,849
  60 | Present value of total proceeds = $8,000,000 × 1/(1+8%)^4 = $5,880,239

   3 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS
   4 | AND CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
   5 | I. PROVISION
   6 | 1. Definition
   7 | A provision is defined as 'a liability of uncertain timing or amount'.
   8 | For example, a company may face a legal action for a breach of health and
   9 | safety law. Your lawyers estimate the probability of losing the case at 80%.
  10 | The likely consequence is that it may be fined if the court judgement is
  11 | made against them. The timing and potential fine of the fine is uncertain
  12 | because it will be decided by a court at some future date.
  13 | Your Notes
  14 | 356
  15 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS
  16 | AND CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
  17 | I. PROVISION
  18 | 2. Recognition
  19 | Entity has a present obligation
  20 | Arise
  21 | from
  22 | a
  23 | contract,
  24 | legislation,
  25 | other
  26 | operation of law
  27 | E.g. warranties required in sales contracts
  28 | Legal
  29 | obligation
  30 | Arise through past behaviours and actions
  31 | E.g. business which doesn't offer warranties has
  32 | a history of usually carrying out free small
  33 | repairs on its products, so that customers have
  34 | come to expect this benefit when they make a
  35 | purchase.
  36 | Constructive
  37 | obligation
  38 | A transfer of economic benefits is probable (> 50% likely)
  39 | A reliable estimate can be made
  40 | Provision should be recognised when:

  22 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS
  23 | AND CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
  24 | I. PROVISION
  25 | Example 1: Damages provision (Single obligation)
  26 | 3. Measurement
  27 | A customer has brought a lawsuit against Stark Co and is claiming $1,000,000
  28 | in damages. Stark Co’s legal advisors have assessed the probability of Stark Co
  29 | losing and having to pay the damages at 60%.
  30 | Required: What should the provision in Stark Co’s financial statements be?
  31 | Solution:
  32 | There is single obligation and provision is measured at the single obligation
  33 | most likely outcome $1,000,000.
  34 | The provision is NOT measured at 60% × $1,000,000 = $600,000 because there
  35 | is not a large population of items.

   1 | Your Notes

   3 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS
   4 | AND CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
   5 | I. PROVISION
   6 | Example 2: Warranty provision (A large population of items)
   7 | 3. Measurement
   8 | Stone Co sells goods with a warranty under which customers are covered for the
   9 | cost of repairs of any manufacturing defect that becomes apparent within the
  10 | first six months of purchase. The company's past experience and future
  11 | expectations indicate the following pattern of likely repairs.
  12 | Note:
  13 | When the effect of the time value of money is material, the provision uses an
  14 | appropriate discount to reflect the present value of the expense to obligation
  15 | settlement.
  16 | % of goods sold
  17 | Defects
  18 | Cost of repair ($m)
  19 | 75
  20 | None
  21 | -
  22 | 20
  23 | Minor
  24 | 3
  25 | 5
  26 | Major
  27 | 12
  28 | Required: What should the warranty provision in Stone Co's financial statements
  29 | be?
  30 | Solution:
  31 | Stone Co should provide on the basis of the expected cost of the repairs under
  32 | warranty.
  33 | The expected cost is calculated as (75% × $nil) + (20% × $3m) + (5% × $12m) =
  34 | $1,200,000.
  35 | Stone Co should include a provision of $1,200,000 in the financial statements.
  36 | Your Notes
  37 | 360
  38 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS
  39 | AND CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
  40 | I. PROVISION
  41 | 3. Measurement
  42 | Example 3: Time value of money
  43 | A company knows that when it ceases a certain operation in 5 year-time it will
  44 | have to pay environmental cleanup costs of $5m. The provision to be made
  45 | now will be the present value of $5m in 5 year-time. The relevant discount rate
  46 | in this case is 10%.
  47 | Required: Determines the current provision for the cleanup cost after year 5?
  48 | Solution:
  49 | Applying formula about time value of money:
  50 | Value of money after year “y” = Current value of money × 1/(1 + discount rate)^y
  51 | The current provision for the cleanup cost after year 5 = $5m × 1/(1 + 10%)^5
  52 | = $3.1m

  32 | Example 4:
  33 | D Co might face a lawsuit, but D Co’s lawyers estimate the probability of losing the case
  34 | at 30% – in this case, it’s not probable that D Co will have to incur any expenditures to
  35 | settle the claim and they should not book a provision. It’s typical contingent liability.
  36 | If D Co identify they have a contingent liability, they do NOT recognize it – no journal
  37 | entry. D Co should only make appropriate disclosures in the notes to the financial
  38 | statements.

  40 | Provision

  43 | the probability of more

  25 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS

  28 | CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
  29 | 1. Contingent liabilities
  30 | Example 5: Answer
  31 | (a) At 31 December 20X0
  32 | On the basis of evidence available when the financial statements were
  33 | approved, there is no obligation as a result of past events.
  34 | No provision is recognized. The matter is disclosed as a contingent liability
  35 | unless the probability of any transfer is regarded as remote.

  38 | economic benefits in settlement is probable.
  39 | A provision is Recognized for the best estimate of the amount needed to settle
  40 | the present obligation.

   5 | Chapter 14 Quiz
   6 | Answer
   7 | Question 1: The correct answer is D
   8 | According to IAS 37, a restructuring provision should include only the direct
   9 | expenditures arising from the restructuring, which are those that are both
  10 | necessarily entailed by the restructuring and not associated with the ongoing
  11 | activities of the entity. One of the costs should not be classified within the
  12 | restructuring provision including retraining or relocating continuing staff.
  13 | Question 2: The correct answer is Contingent liability
  14 | The occurrence is probable but the amount cannot be reliably measured.
  15 | Question 3: The correct answer is B
  16 | • One-year warranty has the rate of returns of 12%. Rainbird Co sold 525,000
  17 | units so the total return is: 525,000 x 12% = 63,000
  18 | • 5% of the returned items can be repaired at a cost of $5 and the other 95%
  19 | are scrapped and a full refund of $30 is given. This question is provision
  20 | for a large population of items, so the provision is estimated based on the
  21 | expected value.
  22 | Expected cost: 63,000 x 95% x $30 + 63,000 x 5% x $5 = $1,811,250
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 372
  25 | PROVISIONS AND CONTINGENT ASSETS
  26 | AND CONTINGENT LIABILITIES
  27 | Chapter 14 Quiz
  28 | Answer
  29 | Question 4:
  30 | The correct answer is D
  31 | • The opening balance on the provision at 1 January 20X1 was $2.63 million
  32 | with the cost of capital of 8%. This is the unwinding of the discount.
  33 | The amount of the provision at 31 December 20X1:
  34 | $2,630,000 x 108% = $2,840,400

   1 | Your Notes

   3 | EVENTS AFTER REPORTING PERIOD
   4 | I. DEFINITION AND TYPES OF EVENTS
   5 | 1. Definition
   6 | IAS 10 Events after the reporting period defines events after the reporting period
   7 | as ‘those events which could be favourable or unfavourable, that occurs between
   8 | the reporting period and the date that the financial statements are authorised
   9 | for issue.
  10 | Start of the
  11 | reporting period
  12 | End of the
  13 | reporting period
  14 | FSs are
  15 | authorized
  16 | for issue
  17 | Information
  18 | made
  19 | public
  20 | Shareholder
  21 | meeting
  22 | Events after the
  23 | reporting period

  63 | EVENTS AFTER REPORTING PERIOD
  64 | Company X has an accounting period from January 1 to December 31, the date
  65 | of issuing financial statements for the fiscal year 2022 is February 28, 2023. On
  66 | February 15, X’s accountants listed some following subsequent events:
  67 | 1. On January 15, X sold goods A with the price of $98 and selling expenses
  68 | were $2. Knowing that the goods A are recognized at costs of $100 on
  69 | December 31, 2022.
  70 | Solution: The goods A was sold at the price of $98 and selling expenses were
  71 | $2  NRV = $98 - $2 = $96
  72 | Sale of inventory held at the end of the reporting period for less than cost.
  73 | Therefore, the inventory need writing down.

   3 | EVENTS AFTER REPORTING PERIOD
   4 | Company X has an accounting period from January 1 to December 31, the date
   5 | of issue financial statements for the fiscal year 2022 is February 28, 2023. On
   6 | February 15, X’s accountants listed some following subsequent events:
   7 | 8. On January 15, 2023, the Company made a declaration of dividends paid out
   8 | to the shareholders.
   9 | Solution: This is an event occurring after the reporting date that does not need
  10 | to be adjusted, because the obligation to pay dividends is only recognized with
  11 | the approval of the general meeting of shareholders and an official declaration.
  12 | Example 1:
  13 | I. DEFINITION AND TYPES OF EVENTS
  14 | 2. Types of events
  15 | Your Notes
  16 | 386
  17 | EVENTS AFTER REPORTING PERIOD
  18 | II. EVENTS AFTER THE REPORTING PERIOD
  19 | 1. Dividends
  20 | Dividends proposed or declared after the end of the reporting period
  21 | must not be recognised as a liability. The amount of dividends proposed
  22 | or declared after the end of the reporting period, but before the financial
  23 | statements were authorised for issue, is disclosed in the notes (IAS 1).
  24 | 2. Going concern
  25 | If the going concern assumption is no longer appropriate IAS 1 requires that
  26 | the going concern basis of accounting be changed (e.g. to a break-up basis).
  27 | KEY POINT
  28 | An entity should not prepare its financial statements on a going concern
  29 | basis if management determines after the end of the reporting period:
  30 | •
  31 | It intends to liquidate the entity or to cease trading, or
  32 | •
  33 | It has no realistic alternative but to do so
  34 | Deterioration in operating results and financial position after the end of the
  35 | reporting period may indicate a need to consider whether the going
  36 | concern assumption is still appropriate.

  14 | dispose of the plant. The loss on

  28 | REQUIRE

  32 | 20X1 to cover possible damages was

  50 | Chapter 15 Quiz
  51 | Answer
  52 | Question 1: The correct answer is B and C
  53 | • According to IAS 10 Events after the reporting period, while adjusting
  54 | events provide the information on conditions that existed at the
  55 | reporting
  56 | date,
  57 | the
  58 | non-adjusting
  59 | events
  60 | provide
  61 | information
  62 | on
  63 | conditions that arose after the reporting date.
  64 | • Option B and C are correct because they provide the evidence of
  65 | conditions that existed at the end of the reporting period.
  66 | • Option A and D are incorrect because they are indicatives of condition
  67 | that arose after the reporting period.
  68 | Question 2: The correct answer is A and C
  69 | • Option A is correct because it gives the information about the reduction
  70 | in the value of receivables that will not be recovered due to customer’s
  71 | insolvency.
  72 | • Option B is incorrect because the factory accident happened after the
  73 | year end so it is non-adjusting event.
  74 | • Option C is correct because it is related to the value of inventory, which
  75 | is required to be held at the lower of cost or net realizable value.
  76 | • Option D is incorrect because the sale of the division is announcement
  77 | after the 31 March despite the agreement on 25 March 20X5.

  10 | Post-employment Benefits
  11 | Your Notes
  12 | 394
  13 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  14 | Employee benefits are all forms of consideration given by an entity in exchange
  15 | for service rendered by employees or for the termination of employment.
  16 | 1. Introduction
  17 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  18 | The objective of IAS 19 Employee Benefits is to prescribe the accounting and
  19 | disclosure for employee benefits. An entity must recognise:
  20 | an expense when the entity consumes the economic benefit
  21 | arising from service provided by an employee in exchange for
  22 | employee benefits.
  23 | a liability when an employee has provided service in exchange for
  24 | employee benefits to be paid in the future; and

================================================================================
PAGE 198
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 395
   3 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   4 | 2. Short-term Employee Benefits
   5 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   6 | Short-term employee benefits are employee benefits (other than termination
   7 | benefits) that are expected to be settled wholly within 12 months after the end of
   8 | the reporting period in which the employees render the related service.
   9 | Types
  10 | 2.1
  11 | Wages, salaries and social security contributions.
  12 | Profit sharing and bonuses payable within 12 months after the end of the
  13 | period in which the employees render the related service.
  14 | Short-term compensated absences (e.g. paid annual leave, paid sick leave,
  15 | maternity leave) where the absences are expected to occur within 12 months
  16 | after the end of the period in which the employees render the related
  17 | employee service.
  18 | Non-monetary benefits
  19 | (e.g. medical care, housing,
  20 | cars and
  21 | free or
  22 | subsidised goods or services) for current employees.
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 396
  25 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  26 | 2. Short-term Employee Benefits
  27 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  28 | Accounting for Short-term Benefits
  29 | 2.2
  30 | When an employee has rendered service during an accounting period, the entity
  31 | recognises the amount of short-term employee benefits expected to be paid in
  32 | exchange for that service:
  33 | as an expense, unless another IFRS Standard requires
  34 | or permits the inclusion of the benefits in the cost of
  35 | an asset (e.g. IAS 2 Inventories).
  36 | as a liability (accrued expense), after deducting any
  37 | amount already paid;
  38 | IAS 19 clarifies how this guidance is applied to paid absences and profit-
  39 | sharing/bonus plans.

  54 | The entity has a legal or constructive obligation as a result of a

   2 | 399
   3 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   4 | 3. Long-term Employee Benefits
   5 | Other long-term employee benefits are all employee benefits other than short-
   6 | term employee benefits, post-employment benefits and termination benefits.
   7 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   8 | Long-term employee benefits are those other than post-employment benefits
   9 | (pensions) which are not expected to be settled in full before 12 months after the
  10 | end of the reporting period in which the service was given by the employee.
  11 | Types
  12 | 3.1
  13 | Long-term paid absences (e.g. long-service and sabbatical leave)
  14 | Jubilee awards
  15 | Long-term disability benefits
  16 | Profit sharing and bonuses
  17 | Deferred remuneration
  18 | Types of long-term employee benefits:
  19 | Your Notes
  20 | 400
  21 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  22 | 3. Long-term Employee Benefits
  23 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  24 | Accounting for Long-term Benefits
  25 | 3.2
  26 | The principle applied in accounting for long-term benefits is similar to the
  27 | accounting model for post-retirement benefits in that a net deficit or surplus is
  28 | recognised in the statement of financial position. The calculation of this net
  29 | amount is the same as for a defined benefit plan.
  30 | As there is not as much uncertainty about the measurement of other long-term
  31 | employee benefits as there is with defined benefit entitlements, the standard
  32 | requires a much simpler method of accounting for changes in the net
  33 | deficit/surplus, with no items recognised in OCI.
  34 | Service cost
  35 | Net interest on the defined benefit asset or
  36 | liability
  37 | Any remeasurements of the defined benefit
  38 | asset or liability
  39 | Recognised in
  40 | profit or loss

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 401
   3 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   4 | 3. Long-term Employee Benefits
   5 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   6 | Accounting for Long-term Benefits
   7 | 3.2
   8 | If the amount of a long-
   9 | term
  10 | disability
  11 | benefit
  12 | depends on the length of
  13 | service
  14 | •
  15 | The obligation arises when the
  16 | service is rendered.
  17 | •
  18 | The
  19 | amount
  20 | recognised
  21 | should
  22 | reflect the probability of payment
  23 | and the length of time for which
  24 | payments will be made.
  25 | If
  26 | the
  27 | benefit
  28 | is
  29 | fixed
  30 | irrespective of the period of
  31 | service
  32 | The amount is recognised when an
  33 | obligating event arises (i.e. an event
  34 | that causes a long-term disability).
  35 | Your Notes
  36 | 402
  37 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  38 | 4. Termination Benefits
  39 | Termination benefits are employee benefits provided in exchange for the
  40 | termination of an employee’s employment as a result of either:
  41 | •
  42 | an entity’s decision to terminate an employee’s employment before the
  43 | normal retirement date; or
  44 | •
  45 | an employee’s decision to accept an offer of benefits in exchange for the
  46 | termination of employment.
  47 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  48 | This term does not cover any benefits if the employee terminates the employment
  49 | other than in response to an offer of termination.
  50 | Employee’s working life
  51 | Retirement time
  52 | Termination date
  53 | Retirement date
  54 | Working
  55 | Death
  56 | `

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 403
   3 | I. EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   4 | 4. Termination Benefits
   5 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   6 | Recognition and Measurement
   7 | 4.2
   8 | A liability and
   9 | expense is
  10 | recognised
  11 | Earlier of
  12 | Salary to the end of a notice period during which the
  13 | employee renders no service (i.e."gardening leave")
  14 | Enhanced post-employment benefits (e.g. pensions)
  15 | Types
  16 | 4.1
  17 | Termination benefits are usually lump sum payments but may also include:
  18 | •
  19 | A termination benefit to be settled wholly within 12 months after the end of the
  20 | reporting period is treated as a short-term benefit.
  21 | •
  22 | Otherwise it is treated as a long-term benefit (i.e. with remeasurement at the
  23 | end of each reporting period).
  24 | When the entity recognises any related restructuring
  25 | costs (IAS 37)
  26 | When the entity cannot withdraw the offer of benefits
  27 | (e.g. because the employee accepted it)
  28 | Your Notes
  29 | 404
  30 | II. POST-EMPLOYMENT BENEFITS
  31 | 1. Definitions
  32 | Post-employment benefits are employee benefits (other than termination
  33 | benefits and short-term employee benefits) which are payable after the
  34 | completion of employment.
  35 | Post-employment benefit plans are formal or informal arrangements under
  36 | which an entity provides post-employment benefits for one or more employees.
  37 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  38 | A benefit plan will usually be a formal arrangement but is not necessarily.
  39 | An entity may, or may not, establish a separate entity to receive
  40 | contributions and to pay benefits, although it is convenient to think of the
  41 | plan as a separate entity.
  42 | 2. Benefit Plans
  43 | Payment of
  44 | The Entity
  45 | Retired
  46 | Employee
  47 | The Plan
  48 | Transfer of Cash
  49 | (Funding)
  50 | Asset (bond, stock)
  51 | Post-retirement
  52 | Benefits

PAGE 203
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 405
   3 | II. POST-EMPLOYMENT BENEFITS
   4 | 3. Classification
   5 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   6 | KEY POINT
   7 | Post-employment benefit plans are classified as either defined contribution
   8 | plans or defined benefit plans, according to the economic substance of the
   9 | plan.
  10 | Defined contribution plans are post-employment benefit plans under which an
  11 | entity pays fixed contributions into a separate entity (a fund) and will have no
  12 | legal or constructive obligation to pay further contributions if the fund does not
  13 | hold sufficient assets to pay all employee benefits relating to employee service in
  14 | the current and prior periods.
  15 | Defined benefit plans are post-employment benefit plans other than defined
  16 | contribution plans.
  17 | Your Notes
  18 | 406
  19 | II. POST-EMPLOYMENT BENEFITS
  20 | 3. Classification
  21 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  22 | In a defined contribution plan the entity pays a predetermined amount
  23 | annually into the plan, which is a separate entity. This contribution is usually
  24 | defined as a percentage of an employee's salary. The plan invests the funds
  25 | and the level of benefits paid to a retired employee depends on the
  26 | performance of the plan’s investments, with no guarantee of a minimum
  27 | amount.
  28 | Therefore risks relating to the plan lie with the employee/retiree.
  29 | Predetermined
  30 | contributions
  31 | Variable
  32 | benefits
  33 | Employer
  34 | Plan
  35 | Retiree

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 407
   3 | II. POST-EMPLOYMENT BENEFITS
   4 | 3. Classification
   5 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
   6 | In a defined benefit plan the level of benefits paid to a retired employee is
   7 | guaranteed. It is usually defined as a percentage of the employee's salary,
   8 | with the percentage depending on the number of years’ service provided by
   9 | the employee. Again the entity (and possibly the employee) pay contributions
  10 | to the plan while the employee provides service; however, these contributions
  11 | will
  12 | vary
  13 | depending
  14 | on
  15 | the
  16 | performance
  17 | of
  18 | the
  19 | fund.
  20 | Where
  21 | under-
  22 | performance means that the value of the fund is less than the future amount
  23 | payable, contributions must increase.
  24 | Therefore risks relating to the pension plan lie with the employer.
  25 | Variable
  26 | contributions
  27 | Guaranteed
  28 | predetermined
  29 | benefits

  47 | Chapter 16 Quiz
  48 | EMPLOYEE BENEFITS
  49 | Question 2:
  50 | Which of the following does NOT hold on paid absences?
  51 | A.
  52 | They may affect computation under defined benefit plans
  53 | B.
  54 | They affect other long-term employee benefits
  55 | C.
  56 | They may affect the computation of termination benefits
  57 | D.
  58 | Their effect is mostly on short-term employee benefits
  59 | E.
  60 | They apply only to short-term employee benefits

================================================================================
PAGE 205
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 409
   3 | Chapter 16 Quiz
   4 | Answer
   5 | Question 1: The correct answer is B
   6 | According to IAS 19 Employee Benefits, short-term employee benefits are
   7 | defined as benefits that are expected to be settled within 12 months after the
   8 | end of the reporting period in which the employees render the related
   9 | services.
  10 | For short-term employee benefits, IAS 19 requires that they be measured at
  11 | an undiscounted amount. This is because these benefits are typically due to
  12 | being settled shortly after the reporting period, so there is no need to
  13 | discount them for the time value of money, unlike long-term benefits, which
  14 | may need to be discounted.
  15 | Question 2: The correct answer is E
  16 | Paid absences can impact various categories of employee benefits, not just
  17 | short-term benefits. Under IAS 19 Employee Benefits:
  18 | 1. Paid absences can affect short-term employee benefits if they are
  19 | expected to be settled within a short period
  20 | 2. They can also influence other long-term employee benefits and defined
  21 | benefit plans if they accumulate over a longer period or are vested
  22 | 3. They may even impact termination benefits if they are part of the
  23 | conditions for an employee’s termination package.

  14 | II. Deferred Taxation – Basics
  15 | Your Notes
  16 | 412
  17 | I. INCOME TAXES
  18 | 1. Current tax
  19 | Current tax: the amount of income taxes payable (recoverable) in respect of the
  20 | taxable profit (tax loss) for a period.
  21 | Calculation
  22 | Current
  23 | tax
  24 | on
  25 | profits
  26 | is
  27 | calculated
  28 | in
  29 | accordance with tax legislation and charged,
  30 | or credited, to profit or loss for the period.
  31 | Recognition:
  32 | INCOME TAXES
  33 | Any unpaid amounts are recognised as a liability. If the
  34 | amount already paid in respect of current and prior
  35 | periods exceeds the amounts due for those periods,
  36 | the excess should be recognised as an asset.
  37 | The benefit relating to a tax loss that can be carried
  38 | back to recover current tax of a previous period should
  39 | be recognised as an asset.

PAGE 207
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 413
   3 | I. INCOME TAXES
   4 | 1. Current tax
   5 | The income tax charged in the statement of profit or loss is an estimate. Any
   6 | over/under provisions are cleared in the following period's statement of
   7 | profit or loss and do not give rise to a prior period adjustment.
   8 | KEY POINT
   9 | INCOME TAXES
  10 | Example
  11 | On 31 December 20X8, Company X estimates that the current tax liability for
  12 | 20X8 is $400,000 and will pay for tax authority at 20 January 20X9. At 31 May
  13 | 20X9, Company X finalizes with the tax authority the amount of tax liability is
  14 | $500,000. At 31 December 20X9, Company X estimates current tax liability for
  15 | 20X9 is $1,000,000
  16 | Answer
  17 | Company X will record the following entries:
  18 | At 31 December 20X8: Dr Current tax expense
  19 | $400,000
  20 | Cr Current tax liability
  21 | $400,000
  22 | At 20 January 20X9: Dr Current tax liability
  23 | $400,000
  24 | Cr Cash
  25 | $400,000
  26 | Your Notes
  27 | 414
  28 | I. INCOME TAXES
  29 | 1. Current tax
  30 | INCOME TAXES
  31 | Answer
  32 | At 31 May 20X9: Dr Current tax expense
  33 | $100,000
  34 | Cr Current tax liability
  35 | $100,000
  36 | After that, company X will pay under the provision and record
  37 | Dr Current tax liability
  38 | $100,000
  39 | Cr Cash
  40 | $100,000
  41 | At 31 December 20X9: Dr Current tax expense $1,000,000
  42 | Cr Current tax liability
  43 | $1,000,000
  44 | In the statement of financial position: Current tax liability 1,000,000 (provision
  45 | for 20X9 only).
  46 | In the statement of profit or loss: Current tax expense 1,100,000 (provision for
  47 | 20X9 and additional amount from under provision for 20X8).

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 415
   3 | I. INCOME TAXES
   4 | 2. Deferred taxation
   5 | Deferred tax: an accounting measure that recognises the future tax effect of
   6 | assets and liabilities currently held in the statement of financial position
   7 | determined by applying
   8 | the principles of IFRS
   9 | determined by applying
  10 | the tax rules established
  11 | by the tax authorities
  12 | In most jurisdictions, accounting profit and taxable profit differ
  13 | meaning that the tax charge may bear little relation to profits in a
  14 | period.
  15 | Transactions which are recognised in the financial statements in
  16 | a particular period may not have their tax effect recognised until a
  17 | later period i.e. it is deferred.
  18 | Accounting profit
  19 | Taxable profit
  20 | INCOME TAXES
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 416
  23 | 1. Tax base
  24 | II. DEFERRED TAXATION - BASICS
  25 | INCOME TAXES
  26 | Its carrying amount, less any amount that will be deductible for tax purposes in respect of
  27 | that liability in future periods.
  28 | TAX BASE OF A LIABILITY
  29 | The amount that will be deductible for tax purposes against any taxable economic benefit
  30 | that will flow as and when the carrying amount of the asset is recovered
  31 | TAX BASE OF AN ASSET
  32 | Example 3: Tax bases
  33 | A company’s financial statements include the following:
  34 | • A machine costing $10,000 that is used in the business to generate revenues. Tax
  35 | allowances of $4,000 were given in the current, first year of ownership. Thereafter,
  36 | allowances are available at 20% per annum.
  37 | • Trade receivables with a carrying amount of $2,000; the related revenue has already
  38 | been included in taxable profit.
  39 | • A loan payable of $1 million; there are no tax consequences of repaying the loan.
  40 | • Revenue received in advance with a carrying amount of $3,000. The revenue was
  41 | taxed on an accrual basis.
  42 | Required: Explain the tax base of each item.
  43 | Tax base of an asset or liability: the amount attributed to that asset or liability for tax
  44 | purposes.

================================================================================
PAGE 209
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 417
   3 | 1. Tax base
   4 | II. DEFERRED TAXATION - BASICS
   5 | Example 3: Answer
   6 | • The tax base of the machine is $6,000. This is the remaining amount of cost
   7 | that will be deductible from taxable profits as the machine is used to
   8 | generate taxable revenues.
   9 | • The tax base of the receivables is $2,000. The economic benefits that will
  10 | flow from the asset are not taxable in the future and therefore tax base is
  11 | equal to carrying amount.
  12 | • The tax base of the loan is $1 million, being its carrying amount, because no
  13 | amount is deductible for tax purposes in future periods.
  14 | • The tax base of the revenue received in advance is $3,000, being its carrying
  15 | amount, because no amount is deductible for tax purposes in future periods.
  16 | INCOME TAXES
  17 | Your Notes
  18 | 418
  19 | 2. Temporary differences
  20 | II. DEFERRED TAXATION - BASICS
  21 | INCOME TAXES
  22 | Temporary differences – differences between the carrying amount of an
  23 | asset or liability and its tax base. Temporary differences may be
  24 | either taxable or deductible.
  25 | Taxable temporary differences – temporary differences that will result in
  26 | taxable amounts in determining taxable profit (tax loss) of future periods
  27 | when the carrying amount of the asset or liability is recovered or settled,
  28 | leading to a deferred tax liability
  29 | Deductible temporary differences – temporary differences that will result
  30 | in amounts that are deductible in determining taxable profit (tax loss) of
  31 | future periods when the carrying amount of the asset or liability is
  32 | recovered or settled, leading to a deferred tax asset

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 419
   3 | 2. Temporary differences
   4 | II. DEFERRED TAXATION - BASICS
   5 | INCOME TAXES
   6 | Taxable differences
   7 | Temporary differences
   8 | Taxable profit < Accounting profit
   9 | before tax
  10 | Taxable profit > Accounting profit
  11 | before tax
  12 | Tax savings in the future
  13 | (Deferred tax asset)

  15 | Income approach

  17 | profit) × Tax rate
  18 | (Carrying amount – Tax base)

  21 | of deferred
