  24 | (Deferred tax liability)
  25 | Deductible differences
  26 | Your Notes
  27 | 420
  28 | 2. Temporary differences
  29 | II. DEFERRED TAXATION - BASICS
  30 | INCOME TAXES
  31 | Balance sheet approach
  32 | Deferred tax asset/liability = (Carrying amount – Tax base) ×
  33 | Tax rate
  34 | Tax base = Carrying amount according to tax law
  35 | If Carrying amount > Tax base ⇨Deferred tax liability
  36 | Carrying amount < Tax base ⇨Deferred tax asset
  37 | Deferred tax incurred on the asset items
  38 | Deferred tax incurred on the liability items
  39 | Deferred tax asset/liability = (Carrying amount – Tax base) ×
  40 | Tax rate
  41 | Tax base = Carrying amount – Future deductible expense
  42 | If Carrying amount > Tax base ⇨Deferred tax asset
  43 | Carrying amount < Tax base ⇨Deferred tax liability

   3 | INCOME TAXES
   4 | Chapter 17 Quiz
   5 | Question 3:
   6 | The trial balance of Highwood Co at 31 March 20X6 showed credit
   7 | balances of $800,000 on current tax and $2.6 million on deferred tax. A
   8 | property was revalued during the year giving rise to deferred tax of $3.75
   9 | million. This has been included in the deferred tax provision of $6.75
  10 | million at 31 March 20X6.
  11 | The income tax liability for the year ended 31 March 20X6 is estimated at
  12 | $19.4 million.
  13 | What is the amount of the income tax charge in the statement of profit or
  14 | loss of Highwood at 31 March 20X6?
  15 | $
  16 | Your Notes
  17 | 424
  18 | INCOME TAXES
  19 | Chapter 17 Quiz
  20 | Answer
  21 | Question 1:
  22 | The correct answer is D
  23 | We have a formula to calculate Deferred tax assets and Deferred tax liability
  24 | as follows:
  25 | • The carrying amount of non-current assets exceeded their tax written-
  26 | down value by $850,000. Thus, temporary difference related to the carrying
  27 | amount of non-current asset: DTL = $850,000 x 30% = $255,000
  28 | • The entity claimed depreciation for tax purposes of $500,000 and charged
  29 | depreciation of $450,000 in the financial statements. Thus, temporary
  30 | difference related to depreciation: DTL = ($500,000 - $450,000) x 30% =
  31 | $15,000
  32 | • The revaluation surplus was $250,000. Therefore, the temporary difference
  33 | related to the revaluation surplus: DTL = $250,000 x 30% = $75,000
  34 | • We can calculate the provision for deferred tax:
  35 | $255,000 + $75,000 + $15,000 = $345,000
  36 | Deferred tax asset/liability = Temporary difference × Tax rate
  37 | Temporary difference (=Profit before tax - Taxable profit) > 0
  38 | => Deferred tax liability
  39 | Temporary difference (=Profit before tax - Taxable profit) < 0
  40 | => Deferred tax asset

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 425
   3 | INCOME TAXES
   4 | Chapter 17 Quiz
   5 | Answer
   6 | Question 2:
   7 | The correct answer is $130 million
   8 | We have a formula to calculate the tax paid as follows:
   9 | Tax paid = Total tax expense + Opening tax balance - Closing tax balance
  10 | In which:
  11 | • Opening tax balance = opening current tax liability + opening deferred
  12 | tax liability
  13 | • Closing tax balance = closing current tax liability + closing deferred
  14 | tax liability
  15 | Apply to this question, tax paid is:
  16 | $270m + ($140m + $160m) - ($310m + $130m) = $130m
  17 | Your Notes
  18 | 426
  19 | INCOME TAXES
  20 | Chapter 17 Quiz
  21 | Answer
  22 | Question 3:
  23 | The correct answer is $19 million
  24 | • The trial balance showed credit balances of $800,000 on current tax
  25 | showing the prior year over provision is $800,000
  26 | • The income tax liability for there is estimated at $19.4 million so the
  27 | provision for income tax is $19.4 million
  28 | • Deferred tax on the trial balance is $2.6 million, a revalued property
  29 | gives rise to deferred tax of $3.75 million which is included in the
  30 | deferred tax provision of $6.75 million at 31 March 20X6.
  31 | Deferred tax expense:
  32 | $6.75m - $3.75m - $2.6m = $0.4m
  33 | Income tax expense is:
  34 | $19.4m - $0.8m + $0.4m = $19m

   3 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
   4 | Key issues
   5 | I. OVERVIEW
   6 | Recognition of the charge for
   7 | share-based payments must
   8 | reflect accrual accounting in
   9 | keeping with the Framework.
  10 | Two key
  11 | issues
  12 | Recognition
  13 | Measurement
  14 | How
  15 | to
  16 | measure
  17 | the
  18 | share-based transaction.
  19 | Objective of IFRS 2
  20 | The objective of IFRS 2 is to specify the financial reporting of share-
  21 | based payment transactions and, in particular, to show the effects of
  22 | such transactions (including associated expenses) on profit or loss and
  23 | financial position.
  24 | In summary, IFRS 2 requires the recognition of all share-based payment
  25 | transactions measured at fair value.
  26 | KEY POINT
  27 | Your Notes
  28 | 430
  29 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
  30 | II. SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
  31 | 1. Definition
  32 | Own equity instruments
  33 | Payment in cash or other assets based on
  34 | price (or Value) of own equity instrument
  35 | Goods or services
  36 | Share-based payment transaction: a transaction in a share-based payment
  37 | arrangement in which the entity:
  38 | • receives goods or services from a supplier (including an employee); or
  39 | • incurs an obligation (to the supplier) when another group entity receives
  40 | those goods or services.
  41 | Reporting
  42 | entity
  43 | Supplier/
  44 | employee

   5 | 2. Types of Transactions
   6 | Equity-settled
   7 | • Share option – a contract that gives the holder the
   8 | right, but not the obligation, to subscribe to the
   9 | entity's shares at a fixed (or determinable) price for
  10 | a specified period of time.
  11 | • The entity receives goods
  12 | or services from a
  13 | supplier or employee and in return it provides
  14 | consideration
  15 | in
  16 | the
  17 | form
  18 | of
  19 | its
  20 | own
  21 | equity
  22 | instruments, (e.g. shares or share options).
  23 | Cash alternatives
  24 | • In some arrangements either the counterparty or the
  25 | entity may have a choice whether to settle a share-
  26 | based payment in equity instruments or cash.
  27 | Cash-settled
  28 | • The entity promises to pay a cash amount based on
  29 | the price (or value) of its equity instruments (or
  30 | those of another group entity) in return for the
  31 | receipt of goods or services.
  32 | Your Notes
  33 | 432

  38 | services received when it obtains the goods or when the
  39 | services are provided:
  40 | Debit Expense/Asset

  43 | X

  49 | The key issues are how to measure each type of transaction and
  50 | when to recognise it.

  50 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
  51 | Example
  52 | A company grants three directors 200 share options on 1 January 20X6, and
  53 | these vest (ie the director becomes entitled to them) after two years, providing
  54 | that the director still works for the company. This is expected to be the case.
  55 | Each option has a fair value of $3 at the grant date.
  56 | The total expense to be recognised is $1,800 (3 directors x 200 options x $3).
  57 | This is spread over the two year vesting period giving an expense of $900 in
  58 | each year.
  59 | At the end of year 1 the balance in equity is $900; at the end of year two it is
  60 | $1,800. Assuming that the options are exercised, the equity balance is
  61 | transferred to the share capital account.
  62 | III. EQUITY-SETTLED TRANSACTIONS
  63 | Example (additional reading)

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 437
   3 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
   4 | III. EQUITY-SETTLED TRANSACTIONS
   5 | 2. Vesting Conditions and Recognition of Transactions
   6 | • Vest: to become an entitlement. A party's right to shares of an entity may be
   7 | free or at a prearranged exercise price.
   8 | • Vesting conditions: the conditions that must be satisfied for a person to
   9 | become entitled to receive cash, other assets or equity instruments under a
  10 | share-based payment arrangement.
  11 | Definition
  12 | 2.1
  13 | The counterparty
  14 | becomes entitled
  15 | to their award
  16 | when it vests
  17 | Immediate in the
  18 | case of a supplier
  19 | At some time in
  20 | the future in the
  21 | case of employees
  22 | Grant date
  23 | Vesting date
  24 | Vesting
  25 | period:
  26 | the
  27 | total
  28 | share-based
  29 | payment
  30 | is
  31 | recognised
  32 | in
  33 | the
  34 | financial
  35 | statements.
  36 | Time
  37 | Your Notes
  38 | 438
  39 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
  40 | IV. CASH-SETTLED TRANSACTIONS
  41 | 1. Definition
  42 | Cash-settled share-based payment transaction: one in which the entity acquires
  43 | goods or services by incurring a liability to transfer cash or other assets to the
  44 | supplier of those goods or services for amounts that are based on the price (or
  45 | value) of equity instruments (including shares or share options) of the entity or
  46 | another group entity.
  47 | Common example of a cash-settled share-based payment transaction
  48 | • Share
  49 | appreciation
  50 | rights
  51 | (SARs):
  52 | These
  53 | are
  54 | granted
  55 | to
  56 | employees and give the employee an entitlement to a future
  57 | cash payment based on the increase in the entity's share price
  58 | from a determined level and for a specific period of time.
  59 | 2. Measurement and Recognition
  60 | For cash-settled share-based payment transactions, the goods or services
  61 | acquired and the liability incurred are measured at the fair value of the
  62 | liability (i.e. the amount that an entity expects to pay).
  63 | KEY POINT

================================================================================
PAGE 220
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 439
   3 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
   4 | Example
   5 | On 1 January 20X4 a company grants a director share appreciation rights
   6 | whereby she is entitled to cash equivalent to 1,000 shares on 31 December
   7 | 20X5, assuming she remains in employment.
   8 | The share price is $3.40 on 31 December 20X4 and $4.05 on 31 December
   9 | 20X5.
  10 | At 31 December 20X4 a liability and expense are recognised of $1,700 (1,000 x
  11 | $3.40 x 1/2years). At 31 December the total liability is $4,050 (1,000 x $4.05).
  12 | Therefore the year 2 expense is $2,350 ($4,050 - $1,700).
  13 | IV. CASH-SETTLED TRANSACTIONS
  14 | Example (additional reading)

   8 | On 1 July 20X1 Smeaton grants an employee a right that allows them to choose
   9 | to receive either 20,000 shares on 30 June 20X4 or cash to the value (on that
  10 | date) of 18,000 shares. The employee must remain employed by Smeaton on 30

  12 | The market price of Smeaton’s shares is $5 at 1 July 20X1, $5.35 at 30 June
  13 | 20X2, $5.80 at 30 June 20X3 and $6.10 at 30 June 20X4. The fair value of the
  14 | share route is estimated to be $4.90.
  15 | The employee chooses to take the shares at the vesting date.
  16 | Required:
  17 | Explain the accounting treatment applicable to the awards.
  18 | Liability component

  20 | settled share-based payment

  23 | settled share-based payment

  27 | issue of a

  29 | instrument
  30 | 442
  31 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT

  35 | This is an arrangement in which the counterparty has the choice of settlement. At the grant date:
  36 | • The fair value of the cash route is 18,000 × $5 = $90,000
  37 | • The fair value of the share route is 20,000 × $4.90 = $98,000
  38 | • The fair value of the equity component is therefore $8,000 ($98,000 −$90,000)
  39 | The share based payment is therefore recognised as:
  40 | The employee elects to receive shares rather than cash and therefore the balance on the liability account is transferred to equity at the
  41 | vesting date.

   4 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
   5 | Question 1:
   6 | What are the different types of share-based payment transactions?
   7 | Your answer:
   8 | Question 2:
   9 | How should an entity account for cash-settled share-based payment
  10 | transactions?
  11 | Your answer:
  12 | Your Notes
  13 | 444
  14 | Chapter 18 Quiz
  15 | SHARE-BASED PAYMENT
  16 | Answer
  17 | Question 1:
  18 | The two main types of share-based payment transactions are:
  19 | •
  20 | Equity-settled share-based payment transactions, where the entity
  21 | receives goods or services as consideration for equity instruments of
  22 | the entity.
  23 | •
  24 | Cash-settled
  25 | share-based
  26 | payment
  27 | transactions,
  28 | where
  29 | the
  30 | entity
  31 | receives goods or services as consideration for amounts of cash or
  32 | other assets of the entity that are based on the price of those equity
  33 | instruments.
  34 | Question 2:
  35 | An entity should recognise the goods or services received as an expense,
  36 | with a corresponding increase in liabilities. The expense should be measured
  37 | at the fair value of the cash or other assets that will be paid, which is typically
  38 | the fair value of the equity instruments that the cash or other assets are
  39 | based on.

   4 | FOR AND EVALUATION OF
   5 | MINERAL RESOURCES
   6 | (ADDITIONAL READING)
   7 | 446
   8 | EXPLORATION FOR AND EVALUATION
   9 | OF MINERAL RESOURCES
  10 | IFRS 6 imposes few requirements on companies that
  11 | are engaged in the exploration for and evaluation of
  12 | mineral resources
  13 | IFRS 5 requires entities to develop a policy for the extent to which such expenditure should be capitalised and to disclose
  14 | that policy clearly in the financial statements. It does not, however, specify the capitalisation policy.
  15 | (IFRS 6 paragraph 6, 7)
  16 | The standard also requires entities recognising exploration and evaluation assets to perform an impairment test on those
  17 | assets when facts and circumstances suggest that the carrying amount of the assets may exceed their recoverable amount.
  18 | (IFRS 6 paragraph 18)
  19 | IFRS 6 requires disclosure of information that identifies and explains the amounts recognised in its financial statements
  20 | arising from the exploration for and evaluation of mineral resources, including:
  21 | •
  22 | Its accounting policies for exploration and evaluation expenditures including the recognition of exploration and evaluation
  23 | assets
  24 | •
  25 | The amounts of assets, liabilities, income and expense and operating and investing cash flows arising from those assets
  26 | (IFRS 6 paragraph 23, 24)
  27 | For further useful information
  28 | please scan this QR code

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 449
   3 | Chapter 19 Quiz
   4 | EXPLORATION FOR AND EVALUATION
   5 | OF MINERAL RESOURCES
   6 | Answer
   7 | Question 1:
   8 | An entity should recognize exploration and evaluation expenditures as an
   9 | asset when it is probable that future economic benefits will flow to the entity
  10 | and the expenditures can be measured reliably. The asset should be
  11 | measured at cost, and subsequently, the entity should test the asset for
  12 | impairment.
  13 | Question 2:
  14 | An entity should test exploration and evaluation assets for impairment when
  15 | there are indicators of impairment. If the asset is impaired, the entity should
  16 | recognize an impairment loss.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 453
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | I. INTRODUCTION
   6 | 1. Business Combinations and IFRS Accounting Standards
   7 | IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements applies when preparing the financial
   8 | statements of the parent;
   9 | IFRS 3 Business Combinations is applied to determine whether a business
  10 | combination has taken place and it provides the accounting requirements for the
  11 | acquisition;
  12 | IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements is applied with IFRS 3 to determine
  13 | whether a business combination has taken place and it provides the accounting
  14 | requirements for the period after the acquisition when consolidated financial
  15 | statements are prepared for subsequent reporting dates.
  16 | Three IFRS Standards are relevant:
  17 | The parent must recognise the acquisition of a subsidiary in its
  18 | separate financial statements and is also required to prepare
  19 | consolidated financial statements that represent all group
  20 | companies (i.e. the parent and all subsidiaries) as a single entity.
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 454
  23 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  24 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  25 | I. INTRODUCTION
  26 | 2. IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements
  27 | In the separate financial statements of the investor (parent) an investment in a
  28 | subsidiary (or associate or joint venture) is accounted for:
  29 | At cost
  30 | In accordance with IFRS 9 Financial Instruments
  31 | KEY POINT
  32 | The same method of accounting should be applied consistently to each
  33 | category of investment.
  34 | When an investment becomes classified as held for sale:
  35 | If it was carried at cost or using the equity method – IFRS 5 Non-
  36 | current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations is applied
  37 | If it was carried at fair value – IFRS 9 continues to apply.
  38 | Using equity method of IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures

================================================================================
PAGE 228
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 455
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | I. INTRODUCTION
   6 | 2. IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements
   7 | Any dividends received from the investment will be recognised when the investor's
   8 | right to receive the dividend is established. If an investment is measured:
   9 | At cost or in accordance with IFRS 9, dividends are recognised in profit or
  10 | loss.
  11 | Using equity accounting, the dividend is deducted from the carrying
  12 | amount of the investment.
  13 | Your Notes
  14 | 456
  15 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  16 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  17 | Control exists when the investor is exposed, or has rights, to variable returns
  18 | from its involvement with the investee and has the ability to affect those returns
  19 | through its power over the investee.
  20 | There are three elements to control arising from the definition:
  21 | Power over the investee
  22 | Exposure/rights to variable returns
  23 | The ability to affect those returns through the power held
  24 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION
  25 | 1. Control

================================================================================
PAGE 229
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 457
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | Power
   6 | 1.1
   7 | KEY POINT
   8 | The investor has power over the investee if the investor has rights giving it
   9 | the current ability to direct relevant activities (activities that significantly
  10 | affect returns from the investee).
  11 | Relevant activities include selling and purchasing goods or services,
  12 | managing financial assets, selecting, acquiring or disposing of assets,
  13 | researching and developing new products or processes and determining a
  14 | funding structure and obtaining funding.
  15 | In many straightforward situations, power is gained by holding more than
  16 | 50% of the voting rights in the investee (subsidiary).
  17 | The assessment of voting rights should take into account any potential voting
  18 | rights that the investor holds in the investee (e.g. share options or convertible
  19 | instruments). In order to contribute to control these must be exercisable or
  20 | convertible at the current time. If this is not the case they are ignored.
  21 | 1. Control
  22 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION
  23 | Your Notes
  24 | 458
  25 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  26 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  27 | 1. Control
  28 | Power
  29 | 1.1
  30 | Example 1 (Question): Voting rights
  31 | Mojave has acquired 45% of the voting rights in Sahara. The remaining 55% of
  32 | shares are owned by hundreds of other unrelated investors, all of whom own a
  33 | maximum of 1% of shares. There are no arrangements for shareholders to
  34 | consult one another or act together. Past experience indicates that few other
  35 | investors vote at all.
  36 | Required: Discuss whether Mojave has power over Sahara.
  37 | Power may be achieved where less than
  38 | a majority of votes is held.
  39 | This is a subjective area but the larger the
  40 | size of an investor’s (minority) voting
  41 | rights
  42 | is
  43 | compared
  44 | to
  45 | that
  46 | of
  47 | other
  48 | investors and the higher the number of
  49 | other investors that would have to act
  50 | together to outvote the investor, the more
  51 | likely it is that the investor has control.
  52 | An
  53 | assessment
  54 | of
  55 | voting rights should
  56 | also
  57 | consider
  58 | the
  59 | size of the investor’s
  60 | voting rights relative
  61 | to other vote holders
  62 | and the number of
  63 | other
  64 | parties
  65 | that
  66 | would have to act to
  67 | outvote the investor.
  68 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION

================================================================================
PAGE 230
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 459
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | Power
   6 | 1.1
   7 | Example 1 (Answer): Voting rights
   8 | Although Mojave does not hold a majority of voting rights, it is the largest
   9 | shareholder. The other investors all hold 1% or less of shares, there are no
  10 | arrangements for them to vote together and many of them do not vote. Mojave
  11 | therefore has the practical ability to have power over Sahara.
  12 | Power can also result from one or more contractual arrangements, without holding
  13 | a majority of voting rights. For example, the right:
  14 | to direct the investee to enter into (or veto) transactions for the benefit of
  15 | the investor.
  16 | to appoint or remove key management personnel or another entity that can
  17 | direct relevant activities;
  18 | 1
  19 | 2
  20 | 1. Control
  21 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION
  22 | Your Notes
  23 | 460
  24 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  25 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  26 | Power
  27 | 1.1
  28 | An investor can have power over an investee even if other entities have rights
  29 | allowing them to participate in the activities of the investee (e.g. if another investor
  30 | has significant influence).
  31 | A right is substantive if the investor has the practical
  32 | ability to exercise the right when decisions about
  33 | relevant activities need to be made.
  34 | Factors that may stop a right being substantive are
  35 | financial penalties or legal obstacles. Equally a right
  36 | may not be substantive if the party that holds the
  37 | right would not benefit from exercising them.
  38 | Only substantive
  39 | rights
  40 | are
  41 | considered
  42 | when assessing
  43 | control.
  44 | Example 2 (Question): Control
  45 | Entity A holds 40% of the voting right in entity B. It also holds share options
  46 | which, if it were to exercise them, would take its share-holding in entity B to
  47 | 80%. The share options can be exercised at any time and are in the money
  48 | because the exercise price is less than the fair value of a share.
  49 | Required: Discuss whether Entity A controls Entity B.
  50 | 1. Control
  51 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION

================================================================================
PAGE 231
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 461
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | 1. Control
   6 | Power
   7 | 1.1
   8 | Example 2 (Answer): Control
   9 | The share options are in the money and therefore entity A would benefit
  10 | economically from exercising them. Ignoring any other issues, entity A’s share
  11 | option rights therefore appear substantive and it is probable that Entity A has
  12 | control over entity B through both its current share-holding and its potential
  13 | future shares. Entity B should be recognised as a subsidiary of entity A.
  14 | In this situation, recognising entity B as a subsidiary means that the non-
  15 | controlling interest would be based on a 60% holding (i.e. the actual percentage).
  16 | Returns
  17 | 1.2
  18 | An investor is exposed to variable returns if those returns have the potential to
  19 | vary as a result of performance from the investee.
  20 | Returns may include
  21 | dividends, fees or returns as a result of achieving
  22 | synergies/economies of scale.
  23 | KEY POINT
  24 | There is no fixed right to a specific return.
  25 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION
  26 | Your Notes
  27 | 462
  28 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  29 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  30 | 1. Control
  31 | Link between power and returns
  32 | 1.3
  33 | KEY POINT
  34 | To have control over the investee, the investor will have power and exposure
  35 | to variable returns but must also have the ability to use that power to affect
  36 | the returns from the investee.
  37 | An investor with decision-making rights determines if it is acting as either a
  38 | principal or an agent.
  39 | Principal
  40 | The investor will only control its
  41 | investee if it is acting as a
  42 | principal.
  43 | Agent
  44 | An agent would need powers
  45 | delegated to it by its controlling
  46 | body.
  47 | II. BUSINESS COMBINATION

PAGE 232
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 463
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | Subsidiary is defined as an entity controlled by another entity (the parent).
   6 | Non-controlling interests are the equity in a subsidiary not attributable, directly or
   7 | indirectly, to a parent. For example, if a parent owns 60% of a subsidiary, then the
   8 | non-controlling interest percentage is 40%
   9 | Note: An entity could have power over the investee without holding a majority of
  10 | the voting rights. Returns could be either positive or negative and could include
  11 | dividends, change in the value of the investment, management or service fees etc.
  12 | Additional reading
  13 | III. IFRS 10 CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
  14 | Your Notes
  15 | 464
  16 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  17 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  18 | A parent prepares consolidated financial statements applying uniform
  19 | accounting policies throughout .
  20 | A parent should start to consolidate from the date control is obtained
  21 | and cease when control is lost. There is just one exemption available to
  22 | this under IFRS 5. Consolidation is not required where temporary control
  23 | is acquired because the subsidiary is held exclusively with a view to its
  24 | subsequent disposal in the near future.
  25 | A partial disposal of an interest in a subsidiary in which the parent
  26 | retains control, does not result in a gain or loss but an increase or
  27 | decrease in equity. Purchase of some or all of the non-controlling interest
  28 | is treated as a treasury share-type transaction and accounted for in
  29 | equity.
  30 | Additional Reading - Principles of consolidation
  31 | III. IFRS 10 CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

================================================================================
PAGE 233
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 465
   3 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   4 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   5 | Once an investment ceases to fall within the definition of a subsidiary,
   6 | the parent company should derecognise the assets and liabilities of the
   7 | subsidiary, derecognise the carrying amount of any non controlling
   8 | interest and recognise the consideration received. Any investment
   9 | retained in the subsidiary should be recognised at fair value, and treated
  10 | as an associate under IAS 28, as a joint arrangement under IFRS 11 or as
  11 | an investment under IFRS 9 as appropriate
  12 | A
  13 | parent
  14 | is
  15 | exempted
  16 | from
  17 | the
  18 | preparation
  19 | of
  20 | consolidated accounts if it is itself a wholly or partially owned
  21 | subsidiary and the ultimate or any intermediate parent company
  22 | produces consolidated financial statements that comply with IFRS
  23 | Any difference between the reporting date of the parent and the
  24 | reporting date of a subsidiary should not exceed three months.
  25 | III. IFRS 10 CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
  26 | Additional Reading - Principles of consolidation

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 467
   3 | Chapter 20A Quiz
   4 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
   5 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
   6 | Question 1:
   7 | What are the key differences between consolidated financial statements
   8 | and separate financial statements?
   9 | Your answer:
  10 | Question 2:
  11 | How does an entity account for changes in ownership interest in a
  12 | subsidiary, such as a change in control or a disposal?
  13 | Your answer:
  14 | Your Notes
  15 | 468
  16 | Chapter 20A Quiz
  17 | CONCEPTUAL PRINCIPLES
  18 | OF GROUP ACCOUNTING
  19 | Answer
  20 | Question 1:
  21 | Consolidation: Consolidated financial statements combine the financial
  22 | information of the parent and its subsidiaries, while separate financial
  23 | statements present the financial information of an individual entity.
  24 | Control: Consolidated financial statements require control over subsidiaries,
  25 | while separate financial statements do not require control over investments.
  26 | Question 2:
  27 | An entity should account for changes in ownership interest in a subsidiary
  28 | based on the nature of the change. For example, a change in control would
  29 | trigger the loss of control and the disposal of the subsidiary, while partial
  30 | disposal would result in the parent retaining control but adjusting the
  31 | carrying amount of the investment.

   3 | I. BUSINESS COMBINATION
   4 | 1. Definition of a Business
   5 | A business combination arises when an acquirer obtains control of a business. A
   6 | business is an acquired set of activities and assets that includes, as a minimum,
   7 | an input and a substantive process that together significantly contribute to the
   8 | ability to create outputs
   9 | KEY POINT
  10 | A combination that is an acquisition should be accounted for using the
  11 | acquisition method
  12 | IFRS 3 requires that the acquisition method is applied to a business combination.
  13 | As at the date of the acquisition the acquirer should:
  14 | Identify the acquirer. One entity must be the acquirer
  15 | Determine the acquisition date (i.e the date on which control is obtained)
  16 | Recognise and measure the identifiable assets acquired liabilities assumed
  17 | and non-controlling interest in the acquiree
  18 | Recognise and measure goodwill or a gain on a bargain purchase
  19 | 2. Acquisition Method
  20 | GOODWILL
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 472
  23 | I. BUSINESS COMBINATION
  24 | 3. Goodwill
  25 | Calculation
  26 | 3.1
  27 | Goodwill – an asset representing the future economic benefits arising from other
  28 | assets acquired in a business combination that are individually identified and
  29 | separately recognised
  30 | Goodwill is calculated as the difference between the cost of the acquisition
  31 | (consideration transferred) plus any non-controlling interest and the fair value of
  32 | the acquire’s identifiable assets and liabilities at the date of the exchange
  33 | transaction:

  39 | Less: Acquisition date fair value of identifiable assets acquired

   3 | I. BUSINESS COMBINATION
   4 | 3. Goodwill
   5 | Non-controlling Interest (NCI)
   6 | 3.2
   7 | IFRS 3 permits a choice of measurement for the NCI. It may be measured, on an
   8 | acquisition-by-acquisition basis, at:
   9 | As a proportion of the fair value of identifiable net assets of
  10 | the acquiree
  11 | Fair value at the acquisition date
  12 | OR
  13 | At the acquisition date, the amount of NCI included in the goodwill
  14 | calculation is recognized in the consolidated financial statements
  15 | within equity.
  16 | The NCI is subsequently allocated its share of the subsidiary’s post-
  17 | acquisition total comprehensive income and this is added to the initial
  18 | measurement of the NCI in the equity.
  19 | EXAM ADVICE
  20 | Non-controlling interests should be measured using the method specified in an
  21 | exam question and the information provided
  22 | GOODWILL

  36 | as

   4 | 1. Basic Principle
   5 | The amount of cash or cash equivalents paid; and
   6 | 2
   7 | 1
   8 | The fair value of any other purchase consideration given
   9 | IFRS 3 requires that purchase consideration is measured at its fair value. This is:
  10 | GOODWILL
  11 | 2. Deferred Consideration
  12 | KEY POINT
  13 | Any deferred consideration is measured at its present value taking into
  14 | account any premium or discount likely to be incurred in settlement (and not
  15 | the nominal value of the payable)
  16 | If cash is to be paid sometime in the future, the amount will need to be
  17 | discounted to present value and that amount included as the fair value in the
  18 | cost calculation
  19 | The increase in present value, through the passage of time, will be a finance cost
  20 | charged to profit or loss
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 476
  23 | II. CONSIDERATION TRANSFERRED
  24 | 3. Transaction Costs
  25 | KEY POINT
  26 | IFRS 3 requires that transaction costs (e.g. legal fees, advisory costs or
  27 | valuation fees) incurred on the acquisition of a subsidiary are expensed in
  28 | the period they are incurred.
  29 | Acquisition costs do not form part of the fair value of consideration
  30 | transferred and are not included in the goodwill calculation:
  31 | •
  32 | Costs related to the issue of shares or debt are treated in
  33 | accordance with IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation or IFRS
  34 | 9 Financial Instruments.
  35 | This will generally mean that they are recognised within equity.

  23 | IV. ACCOUNTING FOR GOODWILL
  24 | 1. “Positive” Goodwill
  25 | 1.1
  26 | Measurement
  27 | Goodwill reflects the future economic benefits arising from assets that cannot be
  28 | identified individually or recognized separately
  29 | Initially, it is measured at cost and recognized as an asset
  30 | Subsequently, it is not amortised but tested for impairment annually
  31 | 1.2
  32 | Consolidation adjustment to recognise goodwill
  33 | Consolidation involves adding together the assets, liabilities, income and expenses
  34 | of a parent and subsidiary on a line-by-line basis and then posting consolidation
  35 | adjustments (i.e. consolidation journals).
  36 | The consolidation
  37 | adjustment to
  38 | recognise goodwill
  39 | also ensures that:
  40 | The
  41 | non-controlling
  42 | interest
  43 | and
  44 | any
  45 | fair
  46 | value
  47 | adjustments are recognised
  48 | The cost of the investment in the subsidiary and the
  49 | subsidiary’s equity and reserves at the acquisition date
  50 | are eliminated
  51 | GOODWILL

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 481
   3 | IV. ACCOUNTING FOR GOODWILL
   4 | 2. A Gain on a Bargain Purchase (“Negative” Goodwill)
   5 | The fair value of net assets acquired
   6 | All relevant assets and liabilities have been identified
   7 | Access to an exchange to buy goods or services
   8 | If on initial measurement the fair value of the acquiree’s net assets exceeds the
   9 | cost of acquisition plus non-controlling interest (an “excess”), the acquirer
  10 | reassesses:
  11 | Any excess remaining after the reassessment is recognized immediately in profit
  12 | or loss and attributed entirely to the parent. IFRS 3 refers to this as a gain on a
  13 | bargain purchase. This excess (gain) could have arisen due to:
  14 | Future
  15 | costs that have not been
  16 | reflected in the
  17 | acquisition process
  18 | Measurement of items not at fair value, if required by another
  19 | standard (e.g. deferred tax liabilities cannot be discounted)
  20 | A forced sale at a low price

  21 | $
  22 | Consideration transferred

  34 | = $350,000 + $300,000 – $500,000 = $150,000

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 485
   3 | Chapter 20B Quiz
   4 | Answer
   5 | Question 3:
   6 | Negative goodwill, also known as a bargain purchase, arises when the
   7 | purchase price of an acquired company is less than the net fair value of the
   8 | identifiable assets and liabilities acquired. In other words, the acquiring
   9 | company obtains a "bargain" in the acquisition.
  10 | Under
  11 | IFRS
  12 | 3,
  13 | negative
  14 | goodwill
  15 | should
  16 | be
  17 | recognized
  18 | as
  19 | income
  20 | immediately in profit or loss in the period in which the acquisition occurs.
  21 | This is because negative goodwill represents an excess of the fair value of
  22 | the net assets acquired over the cost of the acquisition, which results in an
  23 | immediate gain for the acquiring company.
  24 | It is important to note that negative goodwill can only be recognized as
  25 | income if the acquiring company can identify all of the assets and liabilities
  26 | of the acquired company at fair value and can demonstrate that the
  27 | purchase price is less than the net fair value of those assets and liabilities. If
  28 | there are any unidentifiable assets or liabilities, the acquiring company must
  29 | adjust the negative goodwill until the total fair value of the net assets
  30 | acquired equals the purchase price.
  31 | GOODWILL

   5 | Associate – an entity over which the investor has significant influence

   3 | Significant influence – the power to participate in the financial and operating
   4 | policy decisions of the investee but is not control or joint control of those policies
   5 | An associate is an entity over which the investor has significant influence
   6 | Significant influence means that an investor has the power to participate in the
   7 | financial and operating policy decisions of the investee.
   8 | The existence of significant influence by an investor is usually evidenced by one or
   9 | more of the following:
  10 | Representation on the board of directors or equivalent governing body
  11 | Participation in policymaking
  12 | Material transactions between the investor and the investee
  13 | Interchange of managerial personnel
  14 | Provision of essential technical information

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 493
   3 | Joint control – the contractually agreed sharing of control of an arrangement,
   4 | which exists only when the decisions about the relevant activities require the
   5 | unanimous consent of the parties sharing control.
   6 | 01
   7 | The sharing of control must be agreed in a contract, and
   8 | 02
   9 | Decisions about relevant activities must require the unanimous
  10 | consent of the parties sharing control
  11 | An entity that is subject to joint control by more than one party is a joint
  12 | arrangement. In order for joint control to exist:
  13 | KEY POINT
  14 | If either of these features does not exist there is no joint control
  15 | ASSOCIATES AND
  16 | JOINT ARRANGEMENTS
  17 | II. SIGNIFICANT INFLUENCE AND
  18 | JOINT CONTROL
  19 | 2. Joint Control
  20 | Your Notes
  21 | 494
  22 | Example 1: Unanimous Consent
  23 | James Co is owned by A (50%), B (25%) and C (25%). The contractual agreement
  24 | between the investors states that decisions about relevant activities of James Co
  25 | require 75% support by investors
  26 | Required:
  27 | Discuss whether joint control exists over James Co
  28 | Answer
  29 | In order for a decision about the relevant activities of James Co to be made, as a
  30 | minimum either:
  31 | •
  32 | A and B have to vote for the decision; or
  33 | •
  34 | A and C have to vote for the decision
  35 | Therefore there is no unanimous consent because all three parties to the
  36 | contractual arrangement do not need to agree the decision. In that case A and B
  37 | would joint control James Co. C would be a participant but not have joint control.
  38 | If the ownership proportions were A (50%), B (30%) and C (20%), James Co would
  39 | be a joint arrangement and A and B would have joint control (because they must
  40 | agree to any decision in order to reach the 75% threshold)
  41 | ASSOCIATES AND
  42 | JOINT ARRANGEMENTS
  43 | II. SIGNIFICANT INFLUENCE AND
  44 | JOINT CONTROL
  45 | 2. Joint Control

   8 | have rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities

  11 | A joint operation if the parties have rights to the assets and

  17 | A group may sell to an associate/joint venture or purchase goods and services

  34 | An associate/joint venture is not part of the group. It is, therefore,
  35 | appropriate to report amounts owed to the group by the investee as
  36 | assets and amounts owed to the investee by the group as liabilities.

  33 | Example 4: Eliminate Unrealised Profit
  34 | The parent has a 40% associate. The parent sells the goods to the associate for
  35 | $150, the goods originally cost the parent $100. The goods are still in the
  36 | associate’s inventory at year-end

  40 | The unrealized profit is $20 ( 40% x $50)
  41 | This increases the cost of sales in the consolidated statement of profit or loss
  42 | which, in turn, reduces consolidated retained earnings

   6 | thereafter for the post-acquisition change in the investor’s share of the investee’s

  14 | share of the profit or loss and OCI of the investee after the date of
  15 | acquisition

  35 | Chapter 21 Quiz
  36 | ASSOCIATES AND
  37 | JOINT ARRANGEMENTS
  38 | Answer
  39 | Question 3: The correct answer is
  40 | Associate 1: Acquisition of 50% of Castur Co. Zuckal Co has appointed two
  41 | of the five board members.
  42 | Associate 2: Acquisition of 15% of Furnitt Co. Zuckal Co can appoint one of
  43 | Furnitt Co's five board members. No party can appoint more than two.
  44 | According to IAS 28, An investor has significant influence over the investee
  45 | when it holds around 20% - 50% of the investee with the following features:
  46 | • Hold 20% - 50% of the investee: A holding of 20% or more of the voting
  47 | power. If the holding is less than 20%, the investor will be presumed not to
  48 | have
  49 | significant
  50 | influence
  51 | unless
  52 | such
  53 | influence
  54 | can
  55 | be
  56 | clearly
  57 | demonstrated.
  58 | • Existence of significant influence: Representation on the board of directors
  59 | or equivalent governing body of the investee; Participation in the policy-
  60 | making process; Material transactions between the investor and the
  61 | investee ; Interchange of managerial personnel and Provision of essential
  62 | technical information.
  63 | Apply in this question, we have:
  64 | •
  65 | In pull down list 1, statement (3) is correct because owning 50% of
  66 | Castur Co gives Zuckal significant influence.
  67 | •
  68 | In pull down list 2, statement (2) is correct because 15% of Furnitt Co
  69 | with the right to appoint one of Furnitt Co's five board members will also
  70 | give significant influence.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 513
   3 | Chapter 21 Quiz
   4 | ASSOCIATES AND
   5 | JOINT ARRANGEMENTS
   6 | Answer
   7 | Question 4: The correct answer is C
   8 | •
   9 | Each of Sneezy Co, Sleepy Co, and Dopey Co holds one-third of the
  10 | shares and voting rights in Snow White Co, which means that no
  11 | single company has control.
  12 | •
  13 | However, if an agreement is in place that requires unanimous consent
  14 | of all three companies for decision-making, then each company has
  15 | the power to veto decisions, which means they have joint control.
  16 | •
  17 | In this scenario, Sneezy Co, Sleepy Co, and Dopey Co are collectively
  18 | responsible for making decisions, and no single company can make
  19 | decisions without the consent of the others.
  20 | The other options are incorrect because:
  21 | A. If an agreement requires at least 60% of the voting rights, Sneezy Co
  22 | would not have joint control because it only holds one-third of the voting
  23 | rights.
  24 | B. If an agreement requires unanimous agreement by Sleepy Co and
  25 | Dopey Co, Sneezy Co would not have joint control because it is not a party
  26 | to that agreement.

  45 | I. INTRODUCTION
  46 | 1. Key issues
  47 | A company may
  48 | engage in foreign
  49 | currency operations
  50 | in two ways:
  51 | Entering directly
  52 | into transactions
  53 | which are
  54 | denominated in
  55 | foreign currencies.
  56 | Resultant transactions and balances must be translated into the
  57 | presentation currency of the entity for inclusion in financial statements.
  58 | The key
  59 | issues
  60 | What exchange rate should be used to translate
  61 | foreign currency amounts?
  62 | How are exchange differences resulting from the
  63 | movement in exchange rates recognised?
  64 | Conducting foreign
  65 | operations through
  66 | a
  67 | foreign
  68 | entity
  69 | (subsidiary
  70 | or
  71 | associate).

   1 | 517
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
   4 | I. INTRODUCTION
   5 | 2. Scope
   6 | IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates is applied:
   7 | To
   8 | account
   9 | for
  10 | transactions
  11 | in
  12 | foreign currencies.
  13 | To translate the financial statements of
  14 | foreign operations that are included in
  15 | the financial statements of the entity by
  16 | consolidation or by the equity method.
  17 | To translate financial statements into
  18 | a different presentation currency.
  19 | This standard does not deal with hedge accounting for foreign currency.
  20 | 518
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
  23 | I. INTRODUCTION
  24 | 3. Foreign Currency
  25 | • Foreign currency: a currency other than the functional currency of the entity.
  26 | • Functional currency: the currency of the primary economic environment in
  27 | which the entity operates.
  28 | • Presentation currency: the currency in which the financial statements are
  29 | presented.
  30 | IAS 21 identifies two types of foreign currency: functional currency and
  31 | presentation currency.
  32 | An entity can select any currency to be its presentation currency
  33 | However, its functional currency must be determined by applying the
  34 | guidance provided in IAS 21.
  35 | KEY POINT
  36 | You must be able to discuss the requirements of IAS 21 and apply them to a
  37 | given scenario in order to identify the functional currency of an entity.

================================================================================

================================================================================

   2 | Your Notes

   4 | I. INTRODUCTION
   5 | 4. Functional Currency
   6 | The functional currency is dictated by the primary economic environment in
   7 | which the entity operates (i.e. the currency in which it spends and receives cash).

  64 | II. INDIVIDUAL COMPANY TRANSACTIONS
  65 | 1. Exchange Rates
  66 | Spot exchange rate: the exchange rate for immediate delivery.
  67 | Closing rate: the spot exchange rate at the end of the reporting period.
  68 | An average rate for the period may be used as a
  69 | practical
  70 | expedient
  71 | providing
  72 | that
  73 | rates
  74 | do
  75 | not
  76 | fluctuate significantly.
  77 | A foreign currency transaction (e.g. a purchase or
  78 | sale of goods) is initially recorded at the spot
  79 | exchange rate (of the functional currency) on the date
  80 | of the transaction.

  43 | Your Notes
  44 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
  45 | II. INDIVIDUAL COMPANY TRANSACTIONS
  46 | 4. Remeasurement at Reporting Date
  47 | When a foreign currency amount is not settled at the reporting end it may require
  48 | remeasurement using up-to-date exchange rates for presentation in the financial
  49 | statements. IAS 21 requires that foreign currency amounts are classified as
  50 | monetary or non-monetary and that classification drives their treatment:
  51 | Monetary Items
  52 | Non-monetary Items
  53 | Cash and amounts
  54 | that will be settled
  55 | in cash
  56 | Balances that do not involve a transfer of
  57 | cash (e.g. inventories and property, plant,
  58 | and equipment)
  59 | Measured using a
  60 | cost model
  61 | Measured using a
  62 | fair value model
  63 | Do not remeasure
  64 | at reporting date
  65 | Translate
  66 | at
  67 | spot
  68 | exchange
  69 | rate
  70 | at
  71 | remeasurement date
  72 | Remeasure
  73 | at
  74 | reporting date using
  75 | closing rate
  76 | Exchange difference arises

   1 | 523
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
   4 | II. INDIVIDUAL COMPANY TRANSACTIONS
   5 | 5. Exchange Differences
   6 | Exchange differences arising on the retranslation of non-monetary items
   7 | are recognised in the same way as the gain or loss on the remeasurement
   8 | to fair value of the underlying item (e.g. in profit or loss for investment
   9 | properties and in OCI for owner-occupied properties).
  10 | Exchange
  11 | differences
  12 | arising
  13 | on
  14 | settlement
  15 | and
  16 | the
  17 | retranslation of monetary items
  18 | are recognised in profit or loss
  19 | as they arise:
  20 | Amounts relating to trading items are
  21 | usually recognised in operating costs;
  22 | Amounts relating to financing items are
  23 | usually recognised within finance costs.
  24 | Example 1: Remeasurement at Reporting Date
  25 | York, whose functional and presentation currency is the dollar ($) acquired a
  26 | financial asset at a cost of €10 million on 6 May 20X5 and classified it as
  27 | measured at fair value through other comprehensive income in accordance with
  28 | IFRS 9 at that date. The fair value of the financial asset at the year end of 31
  29 | December 20X5 was €12 million.
  30 | Relevant exchange rates are as follows:
  31 | 6 May 20X5
  32 | $1: €1.20
  33 | 31 December 20X5
  34 | $1: €1.25
  35 | Required: Calculate the amounts to be recognised in York’s financial statements
  36 | for the year ended 31 December 20X5.
  37 | 524
  38 | Your Notes
  39 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
  40 | II. INDIVIDUAL COMPANY TRANSACTIONS
  41 | 5. Exchange Differences
  42 | Example 1: Answer
  43 | At 6 May 20X5 the financial asset is recognised at $8,333,333 (€10m
  44 | 1.20 ).
  45 | At 31 December 20X5 the financial asset is remeasured to fair value and so
  46 | must be retranslated using the year end exchange rate of $1 : €1.25.
  47 | $
  48 | Initial recognised
  49 | asset
  50 | €10m
  51 | 1.20
  52 | 8,333,333
  53 | Asset at year end
  54 | €12m
  55 | 1.25
  56 | 9,600,000
  57 | Total gain
  58 | 1,266,667
  59 | The total gain includes two elements:
  60 | Exchange loss
  61 | €10m
  62 | 1.25 −$8,333,333
  63 | (333,333)
  64 | Profit or loss
  65 | Fair value gain
  66 | €12m
  67 | 1.25 −€10m
  68 | 1.25
  69 | 1,600,000
  70 | 1,266,667

================================================================================
PAGE 263
================================================================================
   1 | 525
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
   4 | II. INDIVIDUAL COMPANY TRANSACTIONS
   5 | 5. Exchange Differences
   6 | Example 1: Answer
   7 | The asset is a monetary item and therefore the exchange loss is recognised in
   8 | profit or loss (IAS 21). The fair value gain is recognised in OCI (IFRS 9).
   9 | Note, however:
  10 | •
  11 | If the asset had been a financial asset classified as FVTPL, both exchange
  12 | loss and fair value gain would have been recognised in profit or loss.
  13 | •
  14 | If the asset had been owner-occupied property (a non-monetary item)
  15 | under the revaluation model, both exchange loss and the fair value gain
  16 | would have been recognised in OCI.
  17 | •
  18 | If the asset had been investment property (a non-monetary item), both
  19 | exchange loss and the fair value gain would have been recognised in profit
  20 | or loss.
  21 | 526
  22 | Your Notes
  23 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
  24 | 1. Definition
  25 | III. FOREIGN OPERATIONS AND
  26 | CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
  27 | Foreign operation: a subsidiary, associate, joint arrangement or branch of the
  28 | reporting entity, the activities of which are based or conducted in a country or
  29 | currency other than the country of the reporting entity.
  30 | 2. Translation to Presentation Currency
  31 | The financial statements of a foreign operation are translated
  32 | into the presentation currency of the parent (in order to
  33 | prepare the consolidated financial statements).
  34 | Translation gives rise to an exchange difference which is:
  35 | Reported as OCI of the
  36 | foreign subsidiary.
  37 | Accumulated
  38 | in
  39 | a
  40 | foreign
  41 | exchange
  42 | reserve
  43 | in
  44 | its
  45 | statement of financial position.

   1 | 527
   2 | Your Notes

   6 | CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

  23 | the
  24 | post-

  27 | translated
  28 | at
  29 | the
  30 | average rate for that year.
  31 | Foreign exchange reserve
  32 | Arises
  33 | only
  34 | in
  35 | the
  36 | translated
  37 | financial
  38 | statements. The balance is the cumulative
  39 | exchange difference on translation.
  40 | Statement of profit
  41 | or loss and OCI
  42 | Income and expenses
  43 | Spot rate on
  44 | date
  45 | of transaction
  46 | (or
  47 | average rate for the year as a practical
  48 | expedient).
  49 | Exchange
  50 | difference
  51 | for the year
  52 | Arises only in the translated financial
  53 | statements. The exchange difference is
  54 | reported in OCI.
  55 | 528
  56 | Your Notes
  57 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
  58 | IV. DISPOSAL OF FOREIGN OPERATION
  59 | 1. Cumulative Exchange Differences
  60 | On the disposal of a foreign operation, the cumulative amount of the
  61 | exchange differences which have been recognised as OCI and allocated
  62 | to the parent such that they are accumulated in a foreign exchange
  63 | reserve are reclassified to profit or loss to form part of the profit or loss
  64 | on disposal.
  65 | 2. Partial Disposal
  66 | In the case of a partial disposal, only the proportionate
  67 | share of the related accumulated exchange differences
  68 | is included in the gain or loss.

================================================================================
PAGE 265
================================================================================
   1 | 529
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
   4 | V. MONETARY ITEMS IN A NET INVESTMENT
   5 | 1. Introduction
   6 | Net investment in a foreign entity: the amount of the reporting entity's interest in
   7 | the net assets of that operation.
   8 | Where these items are long term in
   9 | nature and settlement is neither
  10 | planned nor likely to occur in the
  11 | foreseeable
  12 | future,
  13 | they
  14 | are,
  15 | in
  16 | substance,
  17 | part
  18 | of
  19 | the
  20 | "net
  21 | investment in the operation".
  22 | A parent (or another member of its group) may have a monetary item that is
  23 | receivable from (or payable to) a foreign subsidiary (or other foreign
  24 | operation), such as a long-term loan.
  25 | Trade
  26 | receivables
  27 | or
  28 | payables
  29 | are not part of the net investment in
  30 | a foreign operation (i.e. they are
  31 | excluded
  32 | from
  33 | the
  34 | accounting
  35 | treatment described below).
  36 | 530
  37 | Your Notes
  38 | FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTION
  39 | V. MONETARY ITEMS IN A NET INVESTMENT
  40 | 2. Separate financial statements
  41 | In the separate financial statements of the parties to the transaction,
  42 | exchange differences on the monetary item that is part of the net
  43 | investment in the foreign operation are included in profit or loss:
  44 | If the transaction is denominated
  45 | in the functional
  46 | currency of the parent, an exchange difference arises in
  47 | the foreign subsidiary’s separate financial statements;
  48 | If the transaction is denominated
  49 | in the functional
  50 | currency
  51 | of
  52 | the
  53 | foreign
  54 | subsidiary,
  55 | an
  56 | exchange
  57 | difference
  58 | arises
  59 | in
  60 | the
  61 | parent’s
  62 | separate
  63 | financial
  64 | statements;
  65 | If the transaction is denominated in a currency that is not
  66 | the functional current of either party to the transaction,
  67 | exchange differences are reported in both companies’
  68 | financial statements.

   4 | V. MONETARY ITEMS IN A NET INVESTMENT
   5 | 3. Consolidated financial statements
   6 | In the consolidated financial statements, exchange differences
   7 | arising on the monetary item that is part of the net investment in the
   8 | foreign operation are recognised in other comprehensive income
   9 | and reclassified through profit or loss on disposal of the investment.

   4 | 1. Scope
   5 | IAS 29 Financial Reporting in Hyperinflationary Economies
   6 | should be applied by any entity that reports in the currency of
   7 | a hyperinflationary economy.
   8 | 2. Accounting for Hyperinflation
   9 | Hyperinflation is not specifically defined,
  10 | but an indication would be where there
  11 | is a cumulative inflation rate of one
  12 | hundred percent over three years
  13 | For most countries this would not apply
  14 | at present.
  15 | Groups might have a subsidiary in such
  16 | a country, which is why this standard
  17 | has been included here in this module
  18 | on group accounting.

   5 | Question 1: The correct answer is A
   6 | This Standard shall be applied to the financial statements, including the
   7 | consolidated
   8 | financial
   9 | statements,
  10 | of
  11 | any
  12 | entity
  13 | whose
  14 | functional
  15 | currency is the currency of a hyperinflationary economy.
  16 | Question 2: The correct answer is B
  17 | In a hyperinflationary economy, reporting of operating results and
  18 | financial position in the local currency without restatement is not useful.
  19 | Money loses purchasing power at such a rate that comparison of
  20 | amounts from transactions and other events that have occurred at
  21 | different times, even within the same accounting period, is misleading
  22 | Question 3: The correct answer is A
  23 | In a period of inflation, an entity holding an excess of monetary assets
  24 | over monetary liabilities loses purchasing power and an entity with an
  25 | excess of monetary liabilities over monetary assets gains purchasing
  26 | power to the extent the assets and liabilities are not linked to a price level.
  27 | Question 4: The correct answer is C

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 545
   3 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   4 | I. OVERVIEW OF IAS 7 STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   5 | 1. Definition
   6 | Statement of
   7 | cash flows (SOCF)
   8 | The principal revenue-producing activities of the entity and
   9 | other activities that are not investing or financing activities.
  10 | Inflows and outflows of cash and cash equivalents.
  11 | Short-term, highly liquid investments that are readily
  12 | convertible to known amounts of cash and which are
  13 | subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value.
  14 | Comprises cash on hand and demand deposits.
  15 | Financing
  16 | activities
  17 | Cash flows
  18 | Cash
  19 | equivalents
  20 | Cash
  21 | The acquisition and disposal of long-term assets and other
  22 | investments not included in cash equivalents.
  23 | Investing
  24 | activities
  25 | Operating
  26 | activities
  27 | Activities that result in changes in the size and composition
  28 | of the contributed equity capital and borrowings of the
  29 | entity.
  30 | A useful addition to financial statements:
  31 | •
  32 | Concentrates on the sources and uses of cash;
  33 | •
  34 | A useful indicator of a company’s liquidity and solvency.
  35 | Your Notes
  36 | 546
  37 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
  38 | 2. Objectives
  39 | I. OVERVIEW OF IAS 7 STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
  40 | 3. Scope
  41 | An entity shall prepare a statement of cash flows and shall present
  42 | it as an integral part of its financial statements for each period for
  43 | which financial statements are presented. All entities are required
  44 | by the standard to produce a statement of cash flows.
  45 | Provide
  46 | users
  47 | of
  48 | financial statements
  49 | with
  50 | a
  51 | basis
  52 | to
  53 | assess the ability of
  54 | the
  55 | entity
  56 | to
  57 | generate cash
  58 | and
  59 | cash
  60 | equivalents
  61 | and the needs of the
  62 | entity
  63 | to
  64 | utilize
  65 | those cash flows.
  66 | The
  67 | economic
  68 | decisions
  69 | that
  70 | are
  71 | taken
  72 | by
  73 | users
  74 | require an evaluation
  75 | of the ability of an
  76 | entity
  77 | to
  78 | generate
  79 | cash
  80 | and
  81 | cash
  82 | equivalents and the
  83 | timing and certainty
  84 | of their generation.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 547
   3 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   4 | 4. The importance of SOCF
   5 | I. OVERVIEW OF IAS 7 STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   6 | It has been argued that 'profit' does not always give a
   7 | useful
   8 | or
   9 | meaningful
  10 | picture
  11 | of
  12 | a
  13 | company's
  14 | operations. For example:
  15 | • It is probable that a profitable business has insufficient cash to pay
  16 | its suppliers and shareholders. Because profit is not the same as
  17 | cash flow, it includes both cash and non-cash sale and expense
  18 | items.
  19 | • Employees might believe that if a company makes profits, it can
  20 | afford to pay higher wages next year. This opinion may not be
  21 | correct: the ability to pay wages depends on the availability of
  22 | cash, not profit.
  23 | It seems that a company's performance and prospects
  24 | depend not so much on the 'profits' earned in a period,
  25 | but more realistically on liquidity or cash flows.
  26 | Your Notes
  27 | 548
  28 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
  29 | 5. Advantages & Disadvantages
  30 | I. OVERVIEW OF IAS 7 STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
  31 | Survival in business depends on the
  32 | ability to generate cash. Cash flow
  33 | accounting directs attention toward
  34 | this critical issue.
  35 | Cash flow is more comprehensive
  36 | than 'profit‘.
  37 | Cash
  38 | flow
  39 | reporting
  40 | provides
  41 | a
  42 | better
  43 | means
  44 | of
  45 | comparing
  46 | the
  47 | results of different companies than
  48 | traditional profit reporting.
  49 | Cash flow reporting satisfies the
  50 | needs of all users better.
  51 | Cash flow forecasts are easier to
  52 | prepare, as well as more useful.
  53 | In some respects, be audited more
  54 | easily than accounts based on the
  55 | accruals concept.
  56 | Cash
  57 | flows
  58 | are
  59 | more
  60 | easily
  61 | understood.
  62 | Better basis for decision making.
  63 | The disadvantages of cash flow
  64 | accounting
  65 | are
  66 | basically
  67 | the
  68 | opposite
  69 | of
  70 | the
  71 | advantages
  72 | of
  73 | accruals accounting.
  74 | Accrual
  75 | accounting
  76 | generally
  77 | makes the relationships between
  78 | revenue
  79 | and
  80 | expenses
  81 | clearer,
  82 | providing
  83 | better
  84 | insight
  85 | into
  86 | profitability.
  87 | Since cash flow accounting shows
  88 | only cash
  89 | position,
  90 | it does
  91 | not
  92 | reflex the accurate performance of
  93 | the entity.
  94 | Accrual accounting is done in real-
  95 | time, while cash accounting is an
  96 | “after the fact” accounting style.
  97 | When looking at the statement of
  98 | cash
  99 | flows,
 100 | you
 101 | are
 102 | essentially
 103 | looking at information from past
 104 | business operations. So, it does not
 105 | take into consideration any future
 106 | growth.
 107 | Advantages
 108 | Disadvantages

   5 | 1. Classification of activities
   6 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   7 | Example:
   8 | •
   9 | Cash receipts from the sale of goods and the rendering of services.
  10 | •
  11 | Cash receipts from royalties, fees, commissions, and other revenue.
  12 | •
  13 | Cash payments to suppliers for goods and services.
  14 | •
  15 | Cash payments to and on behalf of employees.
  16 | Example:
  17 | •
  18 | Cash proceeds from issuing shares.
  19 | •
  20 | Cash payments to owners to acquire or redeem the entity’s shares.
  21 | •
  22 | Cash proceeds from short- or long-term borrowings.
  23 | •
  24 | Cash repayments of amounts borrowed.
  25 | •
  26 | Cash payments to reduce a lease obligation.
  27 | The main revenue–producing activities of the entity and other
  28 | activities that are not investing or financing activities.
  29 | The acquisition and disposal of long-term assets and other
  30 | investments not included in cash equivalents.
  31 | Activities that result in changes in the size and composition of
  32 | contributed equity capital and borrowings of the entity
  33 | Example:
  34 | •
  35 | Cash payments to acquire non-current assets.
  36 | •
  37 | Cash receipts from sales of non-current assets.
  38 | •
  39 | Cash payments to acquire shares or loan notes of other entities.
  40 | •
  41 | Cash receipts from sales of shares or loan notes of other entities.
  42 | •
  43 | Cash advances and loans made to other parties.
  44 | •
  45 | Cash receipts from the repayment of advances and loans made to other parties.
  46 | Your Notes

  48 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS

  70 | be
  71 | classified
  72 | as
  73 | an

  81 | classified
  82 | as

  88 | may
  89 | be
  90 | classified
  91 | as

  99 | Tax on income

   6 | Profit before tax (PBT)
   7 | Adjust for non-cash items
   8 | e.g. depreciation/amortization, provisions/ allowance,
   9 | gain or loss on disposal assets; investment income,
  10 | interest expense, etc.
  11 | Operating profit before working changes
  12 | Adjust for working changes
  13 | e.g. changes during the period in inventories,
  14 | operating receivables, and payables, etc.
  15 | Cash generated/used from operating activities

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 555
   3 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   4 | Adjustments for non-cash items, financing and investing items
   5 | 2.1
   6 | 2. Format of SOCF using indirect method
   7 | III. PREPARATION OF STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
   8 | Non – cash items
   9 | Adjustment
  10 | Proceeds
  11 | A loss on a disposal of a non-current
  12 | asset (arising through under-provision
  13 | of depreciation) needs to be added
  14 | back and a profit deducted.
  15 | Add
  16 | A gain on a disposal of a non-current
  17 | asset needs to be subtracted back by
  18 | the same logic.
  19 | Subtract
  20 | Expenses
  21 | Depreciation is not a cash expense
  22 | but is deducted in arriving at a profit. It
  23 | makes sense, therefore, to eliminate it
  24 | by adding it back.
  25 | Add
  26 | Your Notes
  27 | 556
  28 | STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
  29 | Adjustments for working changes
  30 | 2.2
  31 | 2. Format of SOCF using indirect method
  32 | III. PREPARATION OF STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS
  33 | Items
  34 | Increases
  35 | Decreases
  36 | Current Assets
  37 | Accounts Receivable (money from customers)
  38 | Reason:
  39 | • A decrease in receivables is added to profit before tax
  40 | as it represents a cash inflow as more cash has been
  41 | collected from receivables.
  42 | • An
  43 | increase
  44 | in
  45 | trade
  46 | receivables
  47 | is
  48 | therefore
  49 | subtracted from profit before tax.
  50 | Subtract
  51 | Add
  52 | Inventory (purchase or payment for inventory)
  53 | Reason:
  54 | • A decrease in inventory is added to profit before tax as
  55 | it represents a cash inflow from disposing of inventory.
  56 | • An increase in inventory is subtracted from profit
  57 | before tax as it represents a cash outflow to pay for
  58 | the additional inventory.
  59 | Subtract
  60 | Add
  61 | Current Liabilities
  62 | Accounts Payable (pay your bills)
  63 | Reason:
  64 | • A decrease in trade payables is subtracted from profit
  65 | before tax as it represents a cash outflow as more
  66 | payables have been paid off.
  67 | • An increase in trade payables is added to profit before
  68 | tax as company is holding money from customers.
  69 | Add
  70 | Subtract
  71 | It is very important to understand why certain items are added and others subtracted.

   3 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
   4 | OVERVIEW
   5 | What will you learn?
   6 | IFRS 8 OPERATING SEGMENTS
   7 | Reportable Segments
   8 | Overview of IFRS 8
   9 | Disclosure
  10 | Scope
  11 | Criteria
  12 | Aggregation criteria
  13 | General information
  14 | Profit or loss, assets
  15 | and liabilities
  16 | Reconciliations
  17 | Entity-wide
  18 | Disclosures
  19 | Your Notes
  20 | 570
  21 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
  22 | 1. Scope
  23 | I. OVERVIEW OF IFRS 8
  24 | KEY POINT
  25 | IFRS 8 Operating Segments applies to the separate financial statements of
  26 | entities whose debt or equity instruments are publicly traded (or are in the
  27 | process of being issued in a public market)
  28 | 01
  29 | 02
  30 | 03
  31 | IFRS 8 also applies to
  32 | the consolidated
  33 | financial statements
  34 | of a group whose
  35 | parent is required to
  36 | apply IFRS 8 to its
  37 | separate financial
  38 | statements
  39 | Where an entity is not
  40 | required to apply
  41 | IFRS 8, but chooses
  42 | to do so, it must not
  43 | describe information
  44 | about segments as
  45 | “segment
  46 | information” unless it
  47 | complies with IFRS 8
  48 | Where a parent’s separate
  49 | financial statements are
  50 | presented with
  51 | consolidated financial
  52 | statements, segment
  53 | information is required only
  54 | in the consolidated
  55 | financial statements

PAGE 286
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 571
   3 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
   4 | 2. Criteria
   5 | I. OVERVIEW OF IFRS 8
   6 | An operating segment is component that meets the following three criteria:
   7 | 01
   8 | 02
   9 | 03
  10 | It engages in business activities from which it may earn revenues
  11 | and incur expenses (including revenues and expenses arising
  12 | from transactions with other components of the same entity)
  13 | It operating results are regularly reviewed by the entity’s “chief
  14 | operating decision-maker” to make decisions (about resources to
  15 | be allocated) and to assess its performance
  16 | Discrete financial information is available. A component, by
  17 | definition, should meet this criterion (IFRS 5)
  18 | Chief operating decision-maker (CODM) – describes the function which
  19 | allocates resources to and assesses the performance of operating segments
  20 | (e.g. a CEO or board of directors)
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 572
  23 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
  24 | 1. Aggregation Criteria
  25 | II. REPORTABLE SEGMENTS
  26 | KEY POINT
  27 | Separate information must be reported for each operating segment that:
  28 | •
  29 | Meets the definition criteria or aggregation criteria for two or more
  30 | segments; and
  31 | •
  32 | Exceeds the quantitative thresholds
  33 | Two or more operating segments may be aggregated into a single operating
  34 | segment if:
  35 | 01
  36 | 02
  37 | 03
  38 | Aggregation is consistent with the cope principle of this
  39 | IFRS
  40 | The segments have similar economic characteristics
  41 | The segments are similar (e.g. in respect of products
  42 | and
  43 | services,
  44 | distribution
  45 | methods
  46 | or
  47 | regulatory
  48 | environment)

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 573
   3 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
   4 | 1. Aggregation Criteria
   5 | II. REPORTABLE SEGMENTS
   6 | KEY POINT
   7 | IFRS 8 is the only IFRS Accounting Standard with a “materiality rule”
   8 | At least 75% of revenue must be included in reportable segments.
   9 | Therefore,
  10 | operating
  11 | segments
  12 | that
  13 | fall
  14 | below
  15 | the
  16 | quantitative
  17 | thresholds may need to be identified as reportable
  18 | Information about other business activities and operating segments that
  19 | are not reportable are combined and disclosed in an “all other
  20 | segments” category
  21 | The standard suggests 10 as a practical limit to the number of
  22 | reportable segments separately disclosed, as segment information may
  23 | otherwise become too detailed
  24 | Your Notes
  25 | 574
  26 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
  27 | 1. Aggregation Criteria
  28 | II. REPORTABLE SEGMENTS
  29 | Example 1: Reportable Segments
  30 | Fireball group has:
  31 | •
  32 | three significant business lines which make up about 70% of combined
  33 | revenue, split equally between them; and
  34 | •
  35 | five small business lines, each of them contributing about 6% to the
  36 | combined revenue.
  37 | Required:
  38 | Explain how many segments the Fireball group should report

================================================================================
PAGE 288
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 575
   3 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
   4 | 1. Aggregation Criteria
   5 | II. REPORTABLE SEGMENTS
   6 | Example 1: Answer
   7 | The issue is whether or not it is acceptable to report the three major business lines
   8 | separately and to include the rest for reconciliation purposes as "all other
   9 | segments".
  10 | Since the reported revenues attributable to the three business lines that meet the
  11 | separate reporting criteria constitute less than 75% of the combined revenue,
  12 | additional segments must be identified as reportable, even though they are below
  13 | the 10% threshold, until at least 75% of the combined revenue is included in
  14 | reportable segments.
  15 | If at least one of the small segments meets the aggregation criteria to be
  16 | aggregated with one of the three reportable segments, there will be four
  17 | reportable segments (including "all other segments").
  18 | Alternatively, two or more of the smaller segments may be aggregated, if they
  19 | meet the aggregation criteria. In this case, there will be five reportable segments.
  20 | If none of the small segments meets the criteria to be aggregated with any of the
  21 | other business lines, one of them must be identified as reportable, though it does
  22 | not meet the 10% threshold. In this case, there will be five reportable segments
  23 | also.
  24 | Your Notes
  25 | 576
  26 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
  27 | 1. General Information
  28 | III. DISCLOSURE
  29 | The factors used to identify reportable segments must be disclosed, including:
  30 | the basis of organisation (e.g. around products and services,
  31 | geographical areas, regulatory environments, or a combination of
  32 | factors and whether segments have been aggregated)
  33 | Basis of Organisation
  34 | The reportable segments of Eparts are strategic business units that offer
  35 | different products and services. They are managed separately because each
  36 | business requires different technology and marketing strategies. Most of the
  37 | businesses were acquired as individual units and the management at the time
  38 | of the acquisition was retained
  39 | Judgements made by management in applying the aggregation
  40 | criteria

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 577
   3 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
   4 | 1. General Information
   5 | III. DISCLOSURE
   6 | The factors used to identify reportable segments must be disclosed, including:
   7 | Types of products and services from which each reportable
   8 | segment derives its revenues
   9 | Types of Products and Services
  10 | Eparts has five reportable segments: machine parts, laser, prototyping, water
  11 | jet and finance. The machine parts segment produces parts for sale to
  12 | aviation equipment manufacturers. The laser segment produces laser cutters
  13 | to serve the petrochemical industry. The prototyping segment produces
  14 | plastics for sale to the pharmaceutical industry. The water jet segment
  15 | produces cutting equipment for sale to car manufacturers and jewelers. The
  16 | finance segment is responsible for certain aspects of financial operations,
  17 | including financing customer purchases of products from other segments and
  18 | car leasing operations.

   4 | 2. Profit or Loss, Assets and Liabilities
   5 | III. DISCLOSURE
   6 | Segment cash flow information is voluntary (IAS 7) and therefore unlikely
   7 | to be produced, as it would provide information to an acquirer to value and
   8 | target for a takeover bid.
   9 | The following must also be disclosed if the specific amounts are regularly
  10 | provided to the chief operating decision maker (CODM) (even if not included in the
  11 | measure of segment assets):
  12 | The investment in associates/JVs accounted for by

  14 | Additions to non-current assets (other than financial
  15 | instruments, deferred tax assets and post-employment
  16 | benefit assets).

  19 | OPERATING SEGMENTS
  20 | 3. Reconciliations
  21 | III. DISCLOSURE
  22 | The reporting entity should provide reconciliations of the total of the following
  23 | amounts for reportable segments with the corresponding total for the entity as a
  24 | whole, reported in the financial statements:
  25 | Revenue
  26 | Profit or loss (before tax and discontinued operations)
  27 | Assets
  28 | Liabilities (if applicable)
  29 | Every other material item
  30 | All material reconciling items must be separately identified and described (e.g.
  31 | arising from different accounting policies)

   5 | III. DISCLOSURE
   6 | Major customers
   7 | 03
   8 | For this purpose, a group of entities known to be under common
   9 | control (including control of a government) is a single customer.
  10 | An entity discloses the extent of its reliance on major customers by
  11 | stating:
  12 | •
  13 | if revenues from a single external customer amount to 10% or
  14 | more of the total;
  15 | •
  16 | the total revenues from each such customer; and
  17 | •
  18 | the segment(s) reporting the revenues.
  19 | An entity need not disclose the identity of a major customer or the
  20 | amount of revenues that each segment reports from that customer.
