   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 593
   3 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
   4 | I. TERMINOLOGY
   5 | 1. Terminology
   6 | Related party: A person or entity that is related to the entity that is preparing its
   7 | financial statements.
   8 | Close member of family: a family member who may be expected to influence,
   9 | or be influenced by, that person in their dealings with the entity and include:
  10 | •
  11 | that person's children and spouse/domestic partner
  12 | •
  13 | children of that person's spouse/domestic partner
  14 | •
  15 | dependents of that person or their spouse/domestic partner
  16 | a. A person (or a close family member), is related to a reporting entity if that
  17 | person
  18 | Has significant influence over the reporting entity
  19 | Has control or joint control of the reporting entity
  20 | Is a member of the key management personnel or of a parent of the reporting
  21 | entity
  22 | i
  23 | ii
  24 | iii
  25 | Your Notes
  26 | 594
  27 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
  28 | I. TERMINOLOGY
  29 | 1. Terminology
  30 | b. An entity is a related party to a reporting entity if the following conditions
  31 | applies
  32 | One entity is an associate or joint venture of the other entity (or an associate or
  33 | joint venture of a member of a group of which the other entity is a member)
  34 | Is a member of the key management personnel or of a parent of the reporting
  35 | entity
  36 | i
  37 | ii
  38 | iii
  39 | One entity is a joint venture of a third party and the other entity is an associate of
  40 | the third entity.
  41 | The entity is controlled or jointly controlled by a person identified in (a)
  42 | iv
  43 | v
  44 | vi
  45 | The entity, or any member of a group of which it is a part, provides key
  46 | management personnel services to the reporting entity or its parent.
  47 | vii
  48 | viii
  49 | The entity and the reporting entity are members of the same group (which
  50 | means that each parent, subsidiary and fellow subsidiary is related to the
  51 | others)
  52 | The entity is a post-employment benefit plan for the employees of either the
  53 | reporting entity or an entity related to the reporting entity.
  54 | A person having control or joint control of the reporting entity has significant
  55 | influence over the entity or is a member of the key management personnel of
  56 | the entity (or parent of the entity).

  22 | A transfer of resources, services or obligations between a reporting
  23 | entity and a related party, regardless of whether a price is charged.
  24 | The substance of a relationship, not merely its legal form, should be
  25 | considered. One party has the ability to control the other party or exercise
  26 | significant influence over the other party in making financial and operating
  27 | decisions.

  34 | Two entities, simply because they have a director or other

  39 | significant influence over the other entity.

  43 | of action of an entity or participate in its decision-making process).

  46 | virtue of the resulting economic dependence.

================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 601
   3 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
   4 | II. EXAMPLES OF RELATED PARTY RELATIONSHIPS
   5 | Examples
   6 | Example 1: Answer
   7 | In Sefton’s financial statements:
   8 | •
   9 | Sefton is a joint venture of both Jolie and Trench and is therefore related to
  10 | both companies.
  11 | •
  12 | Sefton is a joint venture of the group that Green is a member of and
  13 | therefore Sefton and Green are related.
  14 | •
  15 | Sefton is a joint venture and Brown is an associate of Jolie and so Sefton
  16 | and Brown are related.
  17 | •
  18 | Mr Philip is key management personnel of Jolie which has joint control
  19 | over Sefton. As this does not meet the definition of a related party, again
  20 | the substance of the relationship should be examined in order to conclude.
  21 | In Jolie’s financial statements:
  22 | •
  23 | Jolie and Trench are joint venturers; however, this does not automatically
  24 | mean that Trench is related to Jolie; the substance of the relationship
  25 | should be examined to conclude.
  26 | •
  27 | Jolie is related to Green by virtue of being members of the same group.
  28 | Your Notes
  29 | 602
  30 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
  31 | II. EXAMPLES OF RELATED PARTY RELATIONSHIPS
  32 | Examples
  33 | Example 1: Answer
  34 | •
  35 | Brown is an associate of Jolie and so Brown and Jolie are related.
  36 | •
  37 | Sefton is a joint venture of Jolie and so Sefton and Jolie are related.
  38 | •
  39 | Mr Philip is key management personnel of Jolie and is therefore related to
  40 | Jolie
  41 | In Trench’s financial statements:
  42 | •
  43 | As above, Trench may be related to Jolie by virtue of them both being joint
  44 | venturers in Sefton; however, the substance of the relationship should be
  45 | examined to conclude.
  46 | •
  47 | Sefton is a joint venture of Trench and so Sefton and Trench are related.
  48 | In Green’s financial statements:
  49 | •
  50 | Green is related to Jolie because they are in the same group.
  51 | •
  52 | Green is a member of the group of which Sefton is a joint venture so Green
  53 | and Sefton are related.
  54 | •
  55 | Green is a member of the group of which Brown is an associate so Green
  56 | and Brown are related.

  26 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
  27 | III. DISCLOSURES
  28 | 1. Disclosure Required
  29 | KEY POINT
  30 | Relationships between a parent and its subsidiaries are disclosed even if
  31 | there have been no transactions between them.
  32 | Compensation to key management personnel must also be
  33 | disclosed, in total and for each of the following headings:
  34 | •
  35 | Short-term employee benefits
  36 | •
  37 | Post-employment benefits
  38 | •
  39 | Other long-term benefits
  40 | •
  41 | Termination benefits
  42 | •
  43 | Share-based payment.
  44 | The name of the entity's parent must be disclosed and the
  45 | ultimate controlling party, if different.
  46 | Related party relationships where control exists (irrespective
  47 | of whether there have been transactions between them)
  48 | must be disclosed so a reader can form a view about the
  49 | effects of related party relationships.

  11 | information (e.g. whether they are secured)
  12 | Any allowances for doubtful debts
  13 | Any expense recognised in the period in respect of irrecoverable
  14 | or doubtful debts due from related parties
  15 | The amount of the transaction (or aggregate of similar items unless
  16 | separate disclosure is necessary to understand their effects)

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 607
   3 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
   4 | IV. GOVERNMENT-RELATED ENTITIES
   5 | 1. Government-related entities
   6 | The requirements of IAS 24 are quite onerous for many government-related
   7 | entities.
   8 | In some countries, many thousands of government entities would be related
   9 | to each other, so IAS 24 has relaxed the disclosures for some related parties.
  10 | A government
  11 | A reporting entity is exempt from disclosure requirements in relation to related
  12 | party transactions and outstanding balances with:
  13 | which is a related party because the same government has control, joint
  14 | control or significant influence over both the reporting entity and the other
  15 | entity.
  16 | Another entity
  17 | which has control, joint control or significant influence over the reporting
  18 | entity
  19 | Your Notes
  20 | 608
  21 | Illustration 4 Government
  22 | RELATED PARTY DISCLOSURES
  23 | IV. GOVERNMENT-RELATED ENTITIES
  24 | 1. Government-related entities
  25 | •
  26 | Government body Z has control, either directly or indirectly, over entities A
  27 | to F.
  28 | •
  29 | Entity C would be able to claim the disclosure exemptions available under
  30 | IAS 24 in respect of transactions with Government Z and entities A, B, D, E
  31 | and F.
  32 | •
  33 | If entity C entered into a transaction with a person who was key
  34 | management personnel of entity A, the exemption would not apply.
  35 | Government Z
  36 | Entity A
  37 | Entity B
  38 | Entity C
  39 | Entity D
  40 | Entity E
  41 | Entity F

  38 | The objective of this Standard is to ensure that an entity’s financial
  39 | statements contain the disclosures necessary to draw attention to the
  40 | possibility that its financial position and profit or loss may have been
  41 | affected by the existence of related parties and by transactions and
  42 | outstanding balances, including commitments, with such parties.

  24 | Your Notes
  25 | 616
  26 | EARNINGS PER SHARE
  27 | Earnings per share (EPS) is a measure of profits earned by a company for each
  28 | ordinary share.
  29 | I. OVERVIEW OF IAS 33 EARNING PER SHARE
  30 | 1. Definition
  31 | Financial instrument
  32 | Any contract that gives rise to both a financial asset of one entity and a
  33 | financial liability or equity instrument of another entity.
  34 | Equity instrument
  35 | Any contract that evidences a residual interest in the
  36 | assets of an entity after deducting all of its liabilities.
  37 | Ordinary shares
  38 | An equity instrument that is subordinate to all other classes of equity
  39 | instruments.
  40 | Potential ordinary shares
  41 | A financial instrument or other contract that may entitle
  42 | its holder to ordinary shares
  43 | Dividends
  44 | Distributions of profits to holders of equity instruments in proportion to
  45 | their holdings of a particular class of capital.
  46 | 2. Some relevant terminologies

   5 | 2. Some relevant terminologies
   6 | Preference dividends
   7 | Distributions of profit to holders of equity instruments in proportion to
   8 | their holdings of preference shares.
   9 | Options, warrants and their equivalents
  10 | Financial instruments that give the holder the right to
  11 | purchase ordinary shares.
  12 | Convertible loan stock / Convertible preferred shares
  13 | Stock/preferred shares that include an option for shareholder to convert
  14 | the shares into a fixed number of ordinary shares after predetermined
  15 | date.
  16 | Exercise Price / Subscription price
  17 | The price at which the holder is capable of purchasing
  18 | the underlying ordinary shares.
  19 | Preference shares
  20 | Company shares which entitle the holder to a fixed rate
  21 | of dividend and priority of repayment ahead of ordinary
  22 | shareholders.

   5 | 4. Type of earnings per share
   6 | There are two (02) different types of EPS: basic EPS and diluted EPS.
   7 | IAS 33 requires companies’ statements of profit or loss to present both basic EPS
   8 | and diluted EPS with outstanding levels such as together.
   9 | 5. Presentation of EPS on financial statements
  10 | 6. The importance of EPS
  11 | EPS shows the number of earnings available to each ordinary shareholder so that it
  12 | indicates the potential return on individual investments.

   5 | 1. Calculation of basic EPS
   6 | Weighted average number of ordinary shares
   7 | 1.2
   8 | Example 2:
   9 | Chelsea Co is a start-up company with a called-up and paid-up capital of 400,000
  10 | ordinary shares of $2 each and 80,000 8% redeemable preference shares of $2 each
  11 | as at 1 January 20X1. The gross profit from trading activities was $1,000,000 and
  12 | trading expenses were $100,000. The tax charge for the year was estimated at
  13 | $180,000. Calculate basic EPS for the year ended 31 December 20X1
  14 | Guidance:
  15 | Step 1: Calculate preference dividends
  16 | Preference dividends = Number of preference shares × Par value of each share ×
  17 | Rate
  18 | => Preference dividends = 80,000 ×$2 × 8% = $12,800
  19 | Step 2: Calculate net profit after tax:
  20 | Net profit after tax = Gross profit – trading expenses – tax charge
  21 | ⇒
  22 | Net profit after tax = $1,000,000 – $100,000 – $180,000 = $720,000
  23 | Step 3: Calculate Net profit attributable to ordinary shareholders
  24 | Net profit attribute = Profit after tax – Non-controlling interest – Preference dividends
  25 | ⇒
  26 | Net profit attribute = $720,000 – 0 – $12,800 = $707,200
  27 | Step 4: Calculate basic EPS
  28 | => Basis EPS = $707,200 / 400,000 = $1.768

  55 | Right issue (Issuing

  67 | resources

  38 | Example 6:

  41 | the corresponding figure for 20X8 if total earnings were $300,000 in 20X9 and
  42 | EPS for 20X8 was $0.135. The company's accounting year runs from 1 January
  43 | to 31 December.
  44 | Guidance:
  45 | Step 1: Calculate total number of ordinary shares outstanding at 20X9
  46 | Total number of ordinary shares at 20X9 = 2,000,000 + 2,000,000 x 1/5
  47 | = 2,000,000 + 400,000 = 2,400,000
  48 | Step 2: Calculate EPS of 20X9
  49 | Basic EPS (20X9) = $300,000 / 2,400,000 = $0.125
  50 | Step 3: Calculate restated EPS 20X8
  51 | Restated EPS (20X8) = $0.135 x (2,000,000 / 2,400,000) = $0.1125
  52 | EPS in 20X8 is not as good as EPS in 20X9 due to restated EPS 20X8 < basic
  53 | EPS 20X9

   6 | Right issue
   7 | 2.3
   8 | The right issue is the right provided by the company for the existing shareholder to
   9 | purchase shares at prices lower than current market prices.
  10 | Example: Company X has 8 million $1 ordinary shares. After that, the company
  11 | decided to make a right issue 1 for 4 ordinary share with a price of $0.5, market
  12 | price of $2. The change after that event will be set out below:
  13 | Criteria
  14 | Before issue
  15 | After issue
  16 | Number of shares
  17 | 8 million
  18 | 10 million (8m + 8m /4)
  19 | Change in equity
  20 | Increase by $1 million
  21 | Your Notes
  22 | 632
  23 | EARNINGS PER SHARE
  24 | II. BASIC EPS
  25 | 2. Effect on EPS of changes in the number of shares
  26 | Right issue
  27 | 2.3
  28 | Guidance to calculate EPS in case of rights issue
  29 | Step 1: Calculate the weighted average value per share (theoretical ex-rights price
  30 | TERP)
  31 | Number of current shares x Pre-issue market price + Number of new
  32 | shares x Rights price
  33 | Total number of shares after rights shares
  34 | Note:
  35 | A company usually makes the right issue at a price that is lower than the current
  36 | market price
  37 | => TERP is taken into account of the above effect => Ensures comparability.
  38 | Step 2: Calculate the right issue fraction
  39 | Right issue fraction =
  40 | Pre-issue market price
  41 | TERP
  42 | Note:
  43 | We must calculate right issue fraction to compare pre-issue share value and
  44 | post-issue share value at the same basis (because pre-issue market price > TERP)
  45 | => Ensures Comparability.
  46 | TERP

  28 | EARNINGS PER SHARE
  29 | III. DILUTION EPS
  30 | 1. Dilution of share and its effect
  31 | Equity share capital may increase in the future due to the fact that the company may
  32 | have a number of securities that do not currently have the right to claim dividends
  33 | like ordinary shares but will convert into ordinary shares in the future.
  34 | The table below shows the effect of convertible instruments and options/warrants
  35 | on the company after converting ordinary shares:
  36 | Effect
  37 | Convertible instruments
  38 | Options/Warrants
  39 | Number of shares
  40 | Increase
  41 | Increase
  42 | Value of share capital
  43 | Increase
  44 | Increase
  45 | Change in resource structure
  46 | Decrease in debt
  47 | Increase in equity
  48 | Note:
  49 | Although,
  50 | both
  51 | issue
  52 | shares
  53 | and
  54 | convertible
  55 | financial
  56 | instruments
  57 | or
  58 | options/warrants lead to the dilution of shares. But the dilution of shares caused by
  59 | convertible financial instruments and options/warrants are likely unexpected due
  60 | to potential impacts (*). Therefore, EPS must be
  61 | altered by diluted EPS figure
  62 | attempts to provide more correctly potential impact of these additional shares.

   8 | the

  11 | ordinary shares in issue

  18 | •

  21 | The exercise price for options and the subscription price for warrants.
  22 | It serves as a warning
  23 | to equity shareholders

  25 | the
  26 | return
  27 | on
  28 | their investment may

  47 | EPS

  53 | Convertible instrument
  54 | Options & Warrants

  29 | EARNINGS PER SHARE
  30 | III. DILUTION EPS
  31 | 2. Calculation of diluted EPS
  32 | Example 8: Diluted EPS
  33 | In 20X9, Company X has a basic EPS of $1.35 based on earnings of $270,000 and
  34 | 200,000 ordinary $1 shares. It also had in issue $80,000 10% convertible loan note
  35 | which is convertible in two years’ time at the rate of 2 ordinary share for every $4 of
  36 | loan note. The rate of tax is 20%. Calculate the diluted EPS.
  37 | Guidance:
  38 | Step 1: Calculate the weighted average number of ordinary shares after dilution
  39 | Weighted
  40 | average
  41 | number of ordinary
  42 | shares after dilution
  43 | =
  44 | Weighted average
  45 | number of ordinary
  46 | shares before dilution
  47 | +
  48 | Number of
  49 | potential shares
  50 | Step 2: Calculate the adjusted net profit (loss) attributed to ordinary shareholders
  51 | Adjustment = Interest recognized in the period for dilutive potential ordinary shares
  52 | = $80,000 x 10% x (1-20%) = $6,400 (Interest based on profit after tax)
  53 | Current net profit (loss) attributed to ordinary shareholders = $270,000
  54 | Adjusted net profit (loss) attribute to ordinary shareholders
  55 | = $270,000 + $6,400 = $276,400
  56 | Step 3: Calculate Diluted EPS
  57 | Diluted EPS = $276,400 / 240,000 = $1.15
  58 | Because Basic EPS $1.35 > Diluted EPS $1.15 →Present basic & diluted EPS with above
  59 | amount.
  60 | = 200,000 + 40,000 ($80,000 x 2/4) = 240,000

   4 | III. DILUTION EPS
   5 | 2. Calculation of diluted EPS
   6 | Example 9:
   7 | On 1 October 20X4, Hoy Co had $2.5 million of equity share capital (shares of 50
   8 | cents each) in issue.
   9 | No new shares were issued during the year ended 30 September 20X5, but on that
  10 | date there were outstanding share options which had a dilutive effect equivalent to
  11 | issuing 1.2 million shares for no consideration.
  12 | Hoy’s profit after tax for the year ended 30 September 20X5 was $1,550,000.
  13 | In accordance with IAS 33 Earnings Per Share, what is Hoy’s diluted earnings per
  14 | share for the year ended 30 September 20X5?
  15 | A.
  16 | $0.25
  17 | B.
  18 | $0.41
  19 | C. $0.31
  20 | D. $0.42
  21 | Answer:
  22 | Step 1: Calculate weighted average number of ordinary shares after dilution
  23 | Step 2: Calculate adjusted net profit (loss) attribute to ordinary shareholders
  24 | Adjusted net profit (loss) attribute to ordinary shareholders = $1.55m
  25 | Step 3: Calculate Diluted EPS
  26 | Diluted EPS = $1.55m/6.2m = 0.25
  27 | The correct answer is A
  28 | Weighted average number of
  29 | ordinary shares after dilution
  30 | =
  31 | 2,500,000/0.5 +1,200,000
  32 | =
  33 | 6.2m
  34 | Your Notes
  35 | 642
  36 | EARNINGS PER SHARE
  37 | III. DILUTION EPS
  38 | 2. Calculation of diluted EPS
  39 | Example 10:
  40 | Farrah Co has the following results for the year ended 31 December 20X3.
  41 | Net profit for the year
  42 | $2,000,000
  43 | Weighted average number of ordinary shares outstanding during the year
  44 | 540,000
  45 | Average fair value of one ordinary share during the year
  46 | $3
  47 | Weighted average no. of shares under option issued for employee during the
  48 | year
  49 | 100,000
  50 | Exercise price for shares under option during the year
  51 | $2
  52 | Answer:
  53 | Step 1: Calculate number of shares that would have been issued
  54 | When an employee has no option, cash must pay to have 100,000 shares = 100,000 x 3 =
  55 | $300,000
  56 | In fact, they only pay 100,000 x $2 = $200,000 to exercise 2$/shares
  57 | Farrah Co would sell $200,000 with a price of $3  per share (full price), remaining share
  58 | which employees obtain => Company would sell free
  59 | The number of shares would be sold with full price = 200,000/3 = 66,667 shares
  60 | Number of share would be sold free = 100,000 – 66,667 = 33,333 shares
  61 | Step 2: Calculate the weighted average number of ordinary shares after dilution
  62 | Weighted average number of ordinary shares = 540,000 + 33,333 = 573,333
  63 | Step 3: Calculate Diluted EPS
  64 | Diluted EPS = $2,000,000/573,333 = $3,49
  65 | Required: Calculate diluted earnings per share.

   3 | INTERIM FINANCIAL REPORTING
   4 | IAS 34 INTERIM FINANCIAL REPORTING
   5 | 1. Scope
   6 | IAS 34 does not mandate the preparation of interim financial reports, but
   7 | it does prescribe minimum content and recognition and measurement
   8 | principles for those entities that do prepare them
   9 | provide interim reports at least at the end of
  10 | the first half of each financial year, and
  11 | make these reports available not more than
  12 | 60 days after the end of the interim period.
  13 | Publicly traded entities
  14 | are encouraged to:
  15 | Your Notes
  16 | 658
  17 | INTERIM FINANCIAL REPORTING
  18 | IAS 34 INTERIM FINANCIAL REPORTING
  19 | 2. Content of an interim report
  20 | an
  21 | interim
  22 | report
  23 | contains,
  24 | as
  25 | a
  26 | minimum, condensed versions of all
  27 | four
  28 | primary
  29 | statements,
  30 | selected
  31 | explanatory
  32 | notes
  33 | and
  34 | earnings
  35 | per
  36 | share.
  37 | An
  38 | entity
  39 | may
  40 | alternatively choose
  41 | to
  42 | prepare
  43 | full
  44 | financial statements
  45 | in accordance with
  46 | IAS 1 as its interim
  47 | report.
  48 | Comparative
  49 | figures
  50 | for
  51 | previous
  52 | interim
  53 | periods
  54 | and
  55 | previous
  56 | full years are required.
  57 | 01
  58 | 02
  59 | 03
  60 | IAS 34 requires that:

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 659
   3 | INTERIM FINANCIAL REPORTING
   4 | IAS 34 INTERIM FINANCIAL REPORTING
   5 | 3. Recognition and measurement
   6 | 01
   7 | The same accounting policies are required in the interim reporting as for
   8 | annual reporting, although changes in accounting policy might be made
   9 | at the interim stage rather than waiting for a year end. The frequency with
  10 | which interim reporting is carried out must not be allowed to affect the
  11 | annual result.
  12 | 02
  13 | For interim reporting, the use of year end practices with respect to
  14 | whether items should be anticipated or deferred is required. That is,
  15 | interim reports should largely be seen as periods in their own right.
  16 | 4. Disclosure
  17 | Notes to the financial statements must include disclosure of significant events
  18 | and transactions since the end of the last full period.
  19 | For further useful information
  20 | please scan this QR code
  21 | Your Notes

   3 | Question 1:
   4 | The correct answer is B. To prescribe which entities are required to publish
   5 | interim financial reports, how frequently and how soon after the end of the
   6 | reporting period.
   7 | The objective of this Standard is to prescribe the minimum content of an
   8 | interim financial report and to prescribe the principles for recognition and
   9 | measurement in complete or condensed financial statements for an interim
  10 | period. Timely and reliable interim financial reporting improves the ability of
  11 | investors, creditors, and others to understand an entity’s capacity to
  12 | generate earnings and cash flows and its financial condition and liquidity.
  13 | Question 2:
  14 | The correct answer is B. To make their interim financial reports available
  15 | not later than 60 days after the end of the interim period
  16 | The International Accounting Standards Committee encourages publicly
  17 | traded entities to provide interim financial reports that conform to the
  18 | recognition,
  19 | measurement,
  20 | and
  21 | disclosure
  22 | principles
  23 | set
  24 | out
  25 | in
  26 | this
  27 | Standard. Specifically, publicly traded entities are encouraged:
  28 | (a) to provide interim financial reports at least as of the end of the first half
  29 | of their financial year; and
  30 | (b) to make their interim financial reports available not later than 60 days
  31 | after the end of the interim period.

   1 | 667
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
   4 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
   5 | 1. Opening statement of financial position
   6 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
   7 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
   8 | KEY POINT
   9 | IFRS 1 sets out the procedures that must be followed when a company applies
  10 | IFRSs for the first time.
  11 | An IFRS statement of financial position should be prepared at the date of
  12 | transition to IFRSs.
  13 | The
  14 | date
  15 | of
  16 | transition
  17 | is
  18 | the
  19 | beginning of the earliest period for
  20 | which full comparative information
  21 | is prepared. Therefore if IFRSs are
  22 | first
  23 | adopted
  24 | in
  25 | the
  26 | financial
  27 | statements for the year ended 31
  28 | December
  29 | 20X5,
  30 | the
  31 | date
  32 | of
  33 | transition is 1 January 20X4.
  34 | The same accounting policies
  35 | should apply in the opening IFRS
  36 | statement of financial position
  37 | and
  38 | throughout
  39 | all
  40 | periods
  41 | presented
  42 | in
  43 | the
  44 | first
  45 | IFRS
  46 | financial
  47 | statements.
  48 | They
  49 | should
  50 | comply
  51 | with
  52 | IFRSs
  53 | effective at the end of the first
  54 | IFRS period.
  55 | 668
  56 | Your Notes
  57 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
  58 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
  59 | 2. Recognition
  60 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
  61 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
  62 | The reporting entity should eliminate previous GAAP assets and liabilities
  63 | from the opening statement of financial position if they do not qualify for
  64 | recognition under IFRSs.
  65 | For example, if the company’s previous GAAP had allowed accrual of liabilities for
  66 | “general reserves”, restructurings, future operating losses, or major overhauls
  67 | that do not meet the conditions for recognition as a provision under IAS 37, these
  68 | should be eliminated in the opening IFRS statement of financial position.
  69 | The company should recognise all assets and liabilities that are required to
  70 | be recognised by IFRS even if they were not recognized under previous
  71 | GAAP.
  72 | For example, IAS 37 requires recognition of provisions as liabilities including a
  73 | company’s obligations for restructurings, onerous contracts, decommissioning, etc.

================================================================================
PAGE 335
================================================================================
   1 | 669
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
   4 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
   5 | 3. Measurement
   6 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
   7 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
   8 | In preparing IFRS
   9 | estimates
  10 | retrospectively,
  11 | the
  12 | company must use the transactions and assumptions
  13 | that had been used to determine previous GAAP
  14 | estimates in periods before the date of transition to
  15 | IFRS,
  16 | provided
  17 | that
  18 | those
  19 | transactions
  20 | and
  21 | assumptions are consistent with IFRS.
  22 | The company should apply IFRS in measuring all
  23 | recognised assets and liabilities. Any adjustments
  24 | should be recognised directly in retained earnings or
  25 | equity at the date of transition to IFRSs.
  26 | 670
  27 | Your Notes
  28 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
  29 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
  30 | 4. Presentation
  31 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
  32 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
  33 | 5. Disclosure
  34 | A first-time adopter should make an explicit and
  35 | unreserved statement that its general purpose financial
  36 | statements comply with IFRSs for the first time.
  37 | For example, IAS 10 does not permit classifying dividends declared or
  38 | proposed after the reporting date as a liability at that date. If a company had
  39 | done so in its opening statement of financial position, then the dividends
  40 | would need to be reclassified as retained earnings.
  41 | The company should reclassify previous GAAP opening statement of
  42 | financial position items into the appropriate IFRS classification.

PAGE 336
================================================================================
   1 | 671
   2 | Your Notes
   3 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
   4 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
   5 | 6. Exceptions and exemptions
   6 | FIRST TIME ADOPTION OF INTERNATIONAL
   7 | FINANCIAL REPORTING STANDARDS
   8 | Other optional exemptions are available in respect of several areas of
   9 | accounting, including business combinations, share-based payment
  10 | transactions, leases, foreign exchange differences, borrowing costs and
  11 | compound financial instruments.
  12 | The following items must not be adjusted retrospectively on adoption of IFRSs:
  13 | Derecognition of financial
  14 | instruments
  15 | Hedge accounting
  16 | Non-controlling interests
  17 | Classification and measurement
  18 | of financial Embedded derivatives
  19 | Government loans

   1 | Your Notes

   3 | OVERVIEW
   4 | What will you learn?
   5 | What will you learn?
   6 | IFRS 17 INSURANCE CONTRACTS
   7 | Scope
   8 | Definition
   9 | Accounting treatment
  10 | INSURANCE CONTRACTS
  11 | Your Notes
  12 | 674
  13 | IFRS 17 INSURANCE CONTRACTS
  14 | 1. Scope
  15 | INSURANCE CONTRACTS
  16 | IFRS
  17 | 17
  18 | applies
  19 | to
  20 | all
  21 | insurance
  22 | contracts,
  23 | including
  24 | reinsurance contracts issued and held, as well as investment
  25 | contracts with discretionary participation features, provided
  26 | the entity issues such contracts.
  27 | 2. Definition
  28 | •
  29 | An insurance contract is defined as a contract under which one party (the
  30 | insurer)
  31 | accepts
  32 | significant
  33 | insurance
  34 | risk
  35 | from
  36 | another
  37 | party
  38 | (the
  39 | policyholder) by agreeing to compensate the policyholder if a specified
  40 | uncertain future event (the insured event) adversely affects the policyholder.
  41 | •
  42 | Significant insurance risk means that the probability and magnitude of the
  43 | potential variability in outcomes are substantial.

================================================================================
PAGE 338
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 675
   3 | IFRS 17 INSURANCE CONTRACTS
   4 | 3. Accounting treatment
   5 | INSURANCE CONTRACTS
   6 | Measurement Model: IFRS 17 uses the General Measurement Model (GMM) as
   7 | the default, also known as the Building Block Approach (BBA). It comprises:
   8 | Fulfillment Cash Flows
   9 | (FCF): Expected future
  10 | cash inflows and
  11 | outflows, adjusted for
  12 | timing and uncertainty
  13 | Discount Rate: Reflects
  14 | the characteristics of
  15 | the cash flows
  16 | Contractual Service
  17 | Margin (CSM):
  18 | Represents the
  19 | unearned profit from
  20 | the contract, which is
  21 | recognized as
  22 | revenue over the
  23 | service period.
  24 | Risk Adjustment for
  25 | Non-financial Risk:
  26 | Compensation
  27 | required for bearing
  28 | the
  29 | uncertainty
  30 | of
  31 | cash flows.
  32 | For further useful information
  33 | please scan this QR code
  34 | Your Notes
  35 | 676
  36 | Chapter 30 Quiz
  37 | Question 1:
  38 | IFRS 17 shall not be applied to which of the following?
  39 | A. Insurance contracts that the entity issues
  40 | B. Insurance contracts that the entity holds
  41 | C. Reinsurance contracts that the entity issues
  42 | D. Reinsurance contracts that the entity holds
  43 | E. None of the above
  44 | Question 2:
  45 | If the assessment indicates that the carrying value of its insurance
  46 | liabilities is not sufficient considering the estimated future cash flows,
  47 | what should be done?
  48 | A. The entire deficiency shall be reclassified to deferred liability
  49 | B. The entire deficiency shall be tested for impairment
  50 | C. The entire deficiency shall be recognised in profit or loss
  51 | D. The entire deficiency shall be recognised in other comprehensive
  52 | income
  53 | INSURANCE CONTRACTS

================================================================================
PAGE 339
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 677
   3 | Chapter 30 Quiz
   4 | Question 3:
   5 | Which of the following statements does not agree with IFRS 17?
   6 | A. An insurer shall not recognise as a liability any provisions for
   7 | possible future claims, if those claims arise under insurance
   8 | contracts that are not in existence at the end of the reporting period
   9 | B. An insurer shall remove an insurance liability from its statement of
  10 | financial position when, and only when the obligation specified in the
  11 | contract is discharged or cancelled or expires
  12 | C. An insurer shall offset reinsurance assets against the related
  13 | insurance liabilities
  14 | D. An insurer shall not offset income or expense from reinsurance
  15 | contracts against the expense or income from the related insurance
  16 | contracts
  17 | INSURANCE CONTRACTS
  18 | Your Notes
  19 | 678
  20 | Chapter 30 Quiz
  21 | Answer
  22 | INSURANCE CONTRACTS
  23 | Question 1:
  24 | The correct answer is B
  25 | IFRS 4 does not apply to insurance contracts that an entity holds because
  26 | the standard specifically addresses the accounting treatment of contracts
  27 | that an entity issues, where it acts as the insurer. Contracts that an entity
  28 | holds (i.e., contracts purchased to cover its own risks) are outside the
  29 | scope of IFRS 4 because they do not involve the entity's issuance of
  30 | insurance contracts to others.
  31 | Question 2:
  32 | The correct answer is C
  33 | If that assessment shows that the carrying amount of its insurance
  34 | liabilities (less related deferred acquisition costs and related intangible
  35 | assets) is inadequate in the light of the estimated future cash flows, the
  36 | entire deficiency shall be recognised in profit or loss.
  37 | Question 3:
  38 | The correct answer is C
  39 | An insurer shall not offset reinsurance assets against the related
  40 | insurance liabilities.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 681
   3 | I. PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES
   4 | BETWEEN IFRS AND UK GAAP
   5 | 1. UK GAAP AND FRS 102
   6 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
   7 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
   8 | Financial Reporting Standard 102 The Financial Reporting Standard Applicable in
   9 | the UK and the Republic of Ireland (FRS 102) replaced the individual standards of
  10 | UK GAAP on 1 January 2015 (more details regarding the introduction of FRS 102
  11 | are provided in Module 9).
  12 | 2. Principal differences between IFRS and UK GAAP
  13 | The following table highlights the main differences between IFRS and FRS 102:
  14 | Topic
  15 | Main differences between IFRS and FRS 102
  16 | Financial statements
  17 | presentation
  18 | IAS 1 formats for the financial statements differ from the
  19 | UK Companies Act formats used in FRS 102.
  20 | Cash flow statements
  21 | No noticeable differences.
  22 | Financial instruments
  23 | FRS 102 requires that basic financial instruments are
  24 | measured at amortised cost. IFRS 9 requires initial
  25 | recognition at fair value and applies more complex rules
  26 | for subsequent measurement.
  27 | Your Notes
  28 | 682
  29 | I. PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES
  30 | BETWEEN IFRS AND UK GAAP
  31 | 2. Principal differences between IFRS and UK GAAP
  32 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
  33 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
  34 | The following table highlights the main differences between IFRS and FRS 102:
  35 | Topic
  36 | Main differences between IFRS and FRS 102
  37 | Government grants
  38 | FRS 102 allows either the accruals or performance
  39 | method of recognising the grant in the statement of profit
  40 | or loss. IAS 20 only permits the accruals method.
  41 | Capitalisation of
  42 | borrowing costs
  43 | FRS 102 allows capitalisation or expensing of borrowing
  44 | costs whereas IAS 23 requires their capitalisation.
  45 | Retirement benefits
  46 | No noticeable differences.
  47 | Foreign currency
  48 | No noticeable differences.
  49 | Share based payment
  50 | transactions
  51 | Option pricing models are not always applied under FRS
  52 | 102 whereas the IFRS 2 treatment differs.
  53 | Deferred tax
  54 | FRS 102 has a ‘timing differences plus’ approach whereas
  55 | IAS
  56 | 12
  57 | uses
  58 | an
  59 | approach
  60 | based
  61 | on
  62 | “temporary
  63 | differences”.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 683
   3 | I. PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES
   4 | BETWEEN IFRS AND UK GAAP
   5 | 2. Principal differences between IFRS and UK GAAP
   6 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
   7 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
   8 | Topic
   9 | Main differences between IFRS and FRS 102
  10 | Intangible assets
  11 | other than goodwill
  12 | FRS 102 does not permit assets to have indefinite lives and
  13 | they are all subject to amortisation. IAS 38 does permit
  14 | intangible assets to have indefinite useful lives but these
  15 | must be subject to an annual impairment review.
  16 | Property, plant and
  17 | equipment
  18 | No noticeable differences.
  19 | Investment property
  20 | Under FRS 102, investment property is measured at fair
  21 | value. The cost model applied to PPE can be applied to
  22 | investment property rented to another group entity. IAS 40
  23 | offers a choice between both these methods.
  24 | Impairment of non-
  25 | financial assets
  26 | No noticeable differences.
  27 | Leases
  28 | FRS 102 is based on the old Leases Standard, IAS 17, and
  29 | requires lessees to classify leases as either finance leases
  30 | or operating leases. IFRS 16 applies a different model,
  31 | whereby lessees do not classify leases as either type.
  32 | Your Notes
  33 | 684
  34 | I. PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES
  35 | BETWEEN IFRS AND UK GAAP
  36 | 2. Principal differences between IFRS and UK GAAP
  37 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
  38 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
  39 | Topic
  40 | Main differences between IFRS and FRS 102
  41 | Inventories
  42 | No noticeable differences.
  43 | Provisions and
  44 | contingencies
  45 | No noticeable differences.
  46 | Events after the
  47 | reporting period
  48 | No noticeable differences.
  49 | Consolidation
  50 | There is no remeasurement of pre-existing or retained stakes
  51 | under FRS 102.
  52 | Business
  53 | combinations
  54 | (including goodwill)
  55 | Goodwill is not amortised under IFRS but it is under FRS 102.
  56 | FRS 102 allows intangible rights that are inseparable and arise
  57 | from contractual or legal rights to be recognised separately or
  58 | to be included with goodwill.
  59 | Discontinued
  60 | operations & assets
  61 | held for sale
  62 | FRS 102 does not deal with assets held for sale.
  63 | Investment in
  64 | associates
  65 | No noticeable differences.

   5 | 2. Principal differences between IFRS and UK GAAP
   6 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
   7 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
   8 | Topic
   9 | Main differences between IFRS and FRS 102
  10 | Investment in joint
  11 | ventures
  12 | No noticeable differences.
  13 | Related party
  14 | transactions
  15 | No noticeable differences.
  16 | Specialised activities
  17 | (agriculture)
  18 | There is a choice of measurement at cost or fair value
  19 | available under FRS 102 whereas IAS 40 requires
  20 | measurement at fair value.
  21 | Service concession
  22 | arrangements
  23 | FRS 102 addresses accounting by grantors whereas IFRS

  25 | 686
  26 | II. PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN IFRS AND US GAAP
  27 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
  28 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
  29 | IFRS
  30 | US GAAP
  31 | IAS 1 Presentation of
  32 | Financial Statements
  33 | Specific line items and one year comparative financial
  34 | information required by IAS 1.
  35 | US GAAP financial statements are usually prepared on a
  36 | going concern basis unless liquidation is imminent.
  37 | No
  38 | comparative
  39 | information
  40 | required
  41 | for
  42 | private
  43 | companies under US GAAP (though in practice a single
  44 | year is often presented).
  45 | Extraordinary items
  46 | Extraordinary items are prohibited by IAS 1.
  47 | Extraordinary items are permitted, but are restricted to
  48 | items that are both infrequent in occurrence and unusual
  49 | in nature (thus rare in practice). Negative goodwill is an
  50 | extraordinary item under US GAAP.
  51 | IAS 2 Inventories
  52 | IAS 2 requires that inventories are measured at the lower
  53 | of cost and net realisable value.
  54 | The LIFO (last in first out) assumption is prohibited by
  55 | IAS 2.
  56 | US GAAP requires that inventories are measured at the
  57 | lower
  58 | of
  59 | cost
  60 | and
  61 | market
  62 | value,
  63 | being
  64 | current
  65 | replacement cost, if using the LIFO or retail method.
  66 | Otherwise, inventories are measured at the lower of cost
  67 | and net realisable value.
  68 | The LIFO (last in first out) assumption is permitted (and
  69 | commonly used) by US GAAP.
  70 | 1. Principal differences between IFRS and US GAAP

  37 | Use enacted or “substantively enacted” tax rate

  12 | Impairment testing performed if indicators exist

  17 | impairment loss

  26 | goodwill

  28 | (CGU) or group of CGUs

  32 | Subsequent reversal of an impairment loss is

  39 | Contingent

  41 | Contingent
  42 | Assets
  43 | Recognition
  44 | criteria
  45 | Probable = ‘more likely than not’ (which refers to a
  46 | probability of over 50%)
  47 | Probable = ‘likely’ (which is widely interpreted as a
  48 | probability of more than 70%)
  49 | Measurement of
  50 | provisions
  51 | At the best estimate of the obligation:
  52 | •
  53 | Typically an expected value where a large
  54 | population of items is being measured
  55 | •
  56 | The most likely outcome where
  57 | a single
  58 | obligation is measured
  59 | At the most likely outcome; where there is no
  60 | single most likely outcome, the minimum amount
  61 | in the range of outcomes forms the measurement

 109 | of IFRS
 110 | General principle is retrospective application of IFRSs in

 112 | No specific standard under US GAAP.

 114 | Payments
 115 | The fair value of a transaction should be based on the
 116 | value of the goods or services received and only on the fair
 117 | value of the equity instruments if the fair value of the
 118 | goods and services cannot be reliably determined.
 119 | Either the fair value of the goods or services received, or
 120 | the equity instruments is used to value the transaction,

   8 | Combinations
   9 | Contingent
  10 | liabilities and

  13 | a

  17 | present
  18 | obligation

  20 | and

  34 | to measure

  46 | consolidation is
  47 | different

  51 | consider major voting rights.

  53 | accounting
  54 | policies of parent
  55 | company and
  56 | subsidiary

  63 | 692
  64 | II. PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN IFRS AND US GAAP
  65 | PRINCIPAL DIFFERENCES BETWEEN
  66 | IFRS AND UK GAAP/US GAAP
  67 | IFRS
  68 | US GAAP
  69 | IFRS 11 Joint
  70 | Arrangements
  71 | IFRS define a joint arrangement as an arrangement over
  72 | which two or more parties have joint control. Joint
  73 | arrangements may be ‘joint operations’ or ‘joint ventures’.
  74 | US GAAP does not define the ‘joint arrangement’ or ‘joint
  75 | operation’. The definition of a joint venture refers to a
  76 | jointly controlled activity conducted with the use of a
  77 | legal entity.
  78 | In line with IFRS, US GAAP requires that joint ventures
  79 | are generally accounted for using the equity method.
  80 | IFRS 15 Revenue
  81 | from Contracts with
  82 | Customers
  83 | Only minor differences exist relating to:
  84 | •
  85 | Collectability thresholds
  86 | •
  87 | Interim disclosure requirements
  88 | •
  89 | Early application and effective dates
  90 | •
  91 | Impairment loss reversal
  92 | •
  93 | Non-public entity requirements
  94 | IFRS 16 Leases
  95 | All leases, except leases with low value assets and lease
  96 | terms of less than 12 months, result in the lessee
  97 | recognising
  98 | an
  99 | asset
 100 | and
 101 | liability
 102 | in
 103 | the
 104 | financial
 105 | statement.
 106 | US GAAP requires lessees to classify leases as operating
 107 | leases or finance leases.
 108 | US GAAP does not specifically exclude low value assets
 109 | from this treatment.
 110 | 1. Principal differences between IFRS and US GAAP

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 695
   3 | I. THE FASB AND THE FUTURE
   4 | FOR US FINANCIAL REPORTING
   5 | 1. Overview
   6 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
   7 | FOR CHANGE
   8 | The convergence of IFRS Standards and US GAAP has been discussed
   9 | for many years.
  10 | Despite the promulgation of a number of common standards in recent
  11 | years, convergence now appears to be difficult to achieve. Instead, US
  12 | GAAP Standards and IFRS Standards will continue to coexist.
  13 | 2. Process
  14 | 01
  15 | Pre-2011: FASB and IASB aimed to fully converge on IFRS by
  16 | June 2011, formalized in the 2002 Norwalk Agreement and a 2006
  17 | memorandum. The G20 endorsed these efforts in 2009.
  18 | 02
  19 | 03
  20 | 2012: The SEC's report provided no clear recommendation on
  21 | adopting IFRS for US companies, though it suggested a possible
  22 | future move.
  23 | 2015 and beyond: The US confirmed US GAAP's dominance, with
  24 | the SEC Chair and Chief Accountant emphasizing this while
  25 | acknowledging ongoing interest in IFRS. The SEC committed to
  26 | participating in the development of high-quality international
  27 | standards.
  28 | Your Notes
  29 | 696
  30 | II. THE FRC AND THE FUTURE
  31 | FOR UK FINANCIAL REPORTING
  32 | 1. The UK Financial Reporting Council (FRC)
  33 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  34 | FOR CHANGE
  35 | The current financial reporting model in the UK is that listed companies
  36 | (in accordance with EU regulations) prepare accounts under IFRS, whilst
  37 | other companies choose to apply either UK GAAP or IFRS.
  38 | The UK Financial Reporting Council
  39 | (FRC) is committed to eliminating
  40 | differences between UK accounting standards and IFRS.
  41 | In August 2009, it announced a draft policy for implementation of
  42 | the IFRS for SMEs in place of the existing UK GAAP.
  43 | In response to public consultation the FRC modified its approach
  44 | and in late 2012/early 2013 the FRS for Small Entities (FRSSE) was
  45 | updated and three new standards were published:
  46 | • FRS 100 Application of Financial Reporting Requirements
  47 | • FRS 101 Reduced Disclosure Framework
  48 | • FRS 102 The Financial Reporting Standard applicable in the UK
  49 | and Republic of Ireland
  50 | These new standards took effect for accounting periods beginning
  51 | on or after 1 January 2015.

   3 | II. THE FRC AND THE FUTURE
   4 | FOR UK FINANCIAL REPORTING
   5 | 1. The UK Financial Reporting Council (FRC)
   6 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
   7 | FOR CHANGE
   8 | The UK Financial Reporting Council
   9 | (FRC) is committed to eliminating
  10 | differences between UK accounting standards and IFRS.
  11 | The FRC issued three further standards which form part of new UK
  12 | GAAP in 2014/2015:
  13 | •
  14 | FRS 103 Insurance Contracts
  15 | •
  16 | FRS 104 Interim Financial Reporting
  17 | •
  18 | FRS 105 The FRS applicable to the Micro-entities Regime
  19 | FRS 103 and 104 are effective for accounting periods beginning on
  20 | or after 1 January 2015 and FRS 105 for accounting periods
  21 | beginning on or after 1 January 2016.
  22 | Your Notes
  23 | 698
  24 | II. THE FRC AND THE FUTURE
  25 | FOR UK FINANCIAL REPORTING
  26 | 2. FRS 100
  27 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  28 | FOR CHANGE
  29 | FRS 100 outlines the new financial reporting framework and sets out which of
  30 | the new standard(s) should be applied by which companies.
  31 | Although all companies may apply IFRS, it is anticipated that most companies
  32 | will apply standards as follows:
  33 | Listed companies
  34 | IFRS (mandatory)
  35 | Small companies
  36 | FRSSE
  37 | Micro-entities
  38 | FRS 105
  39 | Other companies
  40 | FRS 102 (and FRS 103)
  41 | Unlisted companies that are
  42 | members of a group
  43 | FRS 101

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 699
   3 | 1. FRS 101
   4 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
   5 | FOR CHANGE
   6 | III. NEW UK GAAP
   7 | FRS 101 Reduced Disclosure Framework may be applied to the separate
   8 | financial statements of entities that are included in publicly available
   9 | consolidated financial statements
  10 | The standard cannot be applied to consolidated financial statements
  11 | The purpose of this standard is to enable companies that are members
  12 | of listed groups (and therefore have to prepare consolidation packages
  13 | using IFRS Standards) to apply the recognition and measurement
  14 | requirements of IFRS standards in their own statutory financial reports,
  15 | but without the complex disclosure requirements.
  16 | FRS 101 requires that EU adopted IFRS are applied however with:
  17 | • Certain amendments to the requirements of IFRS in order to comply
  18 | with the Companies Act, and
  19 | • Exemptions from a number of disclosures required by IFRS
  20 | Your Notes
  21 | 700
  22 | 2. FRS 102
  23 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  24 | FOR CHANGE
  25 | III. NEW UK GAAP
  26 | FRS 102 The Financial Reporting Standard is based upon the
  27 | IFRS for SMEs but with some significant modifications to
  28 | ensure compliance with UK company law and also allow
  29 | inclusion of certain accounting treatments permitted by full
  30 | IFRSs but not by the IFRS for SMEs.
  31 | FRS 102 is split into 36 sections and uses IFRS terminology
  32 | such
  33 | as
  34 | receivables
  35 | and
  36 | inventory
  37 | instead
  38 | of
  39 | old
  40 | UK
  41 | terminology such as debtors and stock
  42 | When issued, the FRC announced its intention to review this
  43 | standard
  44 | every
  45 | three
  46 | years
  47 | ("triennial
  48 | review")
  49 | to
  50 | make
  51 | necessary improvements as well as to reflect certain changes
  52 | with IFRS Standards and IFRS Standards for SMEs.
  53 | The first three-year review was completed in December 2017
  54 | with amendments taking effect from 2019, and the FRC has
  55 | announced that amendments made as a result of the second
  56 | review will take effect by 2024.

================================================================================
PAGE 351
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 701
   3 | 2. FRS 102
   4 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
   5 | FOR CHANGE
   6 | III. NEW UK GAAP
   7 | Amendments published since this standard was first issued include:
   8 | Part 1A was added in 2015. This section applies only to small entities. It
   9 | requires those entities to apply the full recognition and measurement
  10 | requirements of FRS 102, but significantly reduces the required disclosure.
  11 | The revisions are a result of the first three-year review. Most of these are
  12 | of an editorial nature and they do not reflect amendments to the IFRS
  13 | Standards as the FRC considers it important that FRS 102 is finalized
  14 | before substantive changes are made.
  15 | The next review is likely to amend FRS 102 to reflect the new and
  16 | relevant IFRS Standards.
  17 | Your Notes
  18 | 702
  19 | 3. Other FRS
  20 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  21 | FOR CHANGE
  22 | III. NEW UK GAAP
  23 | FRS
  24 | 103
  25 | Insurance
  26 | Contracts
  27 | provides
  28 | specific
  29 | accounting
  30 | requirements
  31 | for
  32 | entities
  33 | that
  34 | have
  35 | insurance
  36 | contracts
  37 | and are applying FRS
  38 | 102.
  39 | FRS
  40 | 104
  41 | Interim
  42 | financial
  43 | reporting
  44 | is
  45 | based on IAS 34 and
  46 | provides guidance for
  47 | entities that choose to
  48 | prepare
  49 | interim
  50 | financial reports.
  51 | FRS 105 FRS applies
  52 | to micro entities used
  53 | by
  54 | companies
  55 | identified
  56 | as
  57 | micro
  58 | entities under UK law.
  59 | Until the UK leaves the EU, the IFRS applied in the UK will be the IFRS
  60 | endorsed by the EU. The European Union's endorsement process requires
  61 | that new IFRS Standards and amendments issued by the Council be reviewed
  62 | to ensure compliance with EU law before being endorsed for use. At the time
  63 | of leaving the EU, the UK adopted EU-endorsed IFRS as UK-endorsed IFRS. In
  64 | future, the UK certification body will endorse the IFRS Standards and
  65 | amendments for use in the UK.

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 703
   3 | IV. COUNCIL AND UPDATING
   4 | ACCOUNTING STANDARDS
   5 | 1. Overview
   6 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
   7 | FOR CHANGE
   8 | The Board and other bodies of the IFRS Organization
   9 | are engaged in projects to issue new IFRS Standards
  10 | as well as improve existing IAS Standards and IFRS
  11 | Standards on an ongoing basis.
  12 | Some of these projects arise as a result of requests
  13 | from organizations and others are the result of post-
  14 | implementation reviews of IFRS Standards.
  15 | Minor amendments to existing IFRS Standards may be
  16 | included in the Annual Improvement program if certain
  17 | criteria are met:
  18 | • Post-implementation
  19 | reviews
  20 | will
  21 | take
  22 | place
  23 | approximately two years after an IFRS Standard
  24 | becomes mandatory
  25 | • Annual improvement program: To qualify for this
  26 | program, the revision needs to clarify or amend
  27 | existing guidance; Should not handle a change in
  28 | principle.
  29 | Your Notes
  30 | 704
  31 | IV. COUNCIL AND UPDATING
  32 | ACCOUNTING STANDARDS
  33 | 2. Implementation process
  34 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  35 | FOR CHANGE
  36 | When amending existing IFRS Standards or promulgating new or amended IFRS
  37 | Standards, the Board will follow an appropriate procedure:
  38 | Consulting on working program
  39 | Every
  40 | five
  41 | years,
  42 | the
  43 | Council
  44 | will
  45 | undertake
  46 | a
  47 | comprehensive program of review and consultation to
  48 | identify its priorities and develop its workplan.
  49 | Research programs
  50 | The Council will explore the issues, identify possible
  51 | solutions decide whether benchmarking is needed and
  52 | may issue a discussion paper to seek public views
  53 | The program sets the standard
  54 | Research and feedback on any discussion documents
  55 | are considered and a new Standard or amendments
  56 | will be proposed and published in a draft
  57 | Maintenance program
  58 | The Board will consult on the implementation of a new
  59 | or revised Standard to identify issues.

  63 | 04

   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 707
   3 | Module 9 Quiz
   4 | Question 1:
   5 | Which of the following statements is true?
   6 | A. US public companies may apply IFRS
   7 | B. UK public companies may apply IFRS
   8 | C. US public companies will all be required to apply IFRS in the future on
   9 | a date to be determined by the SEC
  10 | D. UK private companies may apply IFRS
  11 | Question 2:
  12 | Which UK entities should apply FRS 103?
  13 | A. Entities that have insurance contracts and apply FRS 101
  14 | B. Entities that have insurance contracts and apply FRS 102
  15 | C. Entities that prepare interim reports and apply FRS 101
  16 | D. Entities that prepare interim reports and apply FRS 102
  17 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  18 | FOR CHANGE
  19 | Your Notes
  20 | 708
  21 | Module 9 Quiz
  22 | Question 3:
  23 | Under what circumstances is a UK entity eligible to use FRS 101
  24 | Reduced Disclosure Framework?
  25 | A. If the entity is an unlisted member of a group and is preparing publicly
  26 | available consolidated financial statements for that group.
  27 | B. If the entity is a listed member of a group for which consolidated
  28 | financial statements are publicly available and is preparing its
  29 | separate financial statements.
  30 | C. If the entity is an unlisted member of a group for which consolidated
  31 | financial statements are publicly available and is preparing its
  32 | separate financial statements.
  33 | D. If the entity is listed and preparing publicly available consolidated
  34 | financial statements for that group.
  35 | FORTHCOMING PROPOSALS
  36 | FOR CHANGE

================================================================================
PAGE 355
================================================================================
   1 | Your Notes
   2 | 709
   3 | Answer
   4 | Question 1:
   5 | The correct answer is D
   6 | US companies may not apply the IFRS Standards and UK public
   7 | companies MUST apply the IFRS Standards.
   8 | Question 2:
   9 | The correct answer is B
  10 | FRS 103 applies to entities with insurance contracts using FRS 102
  11 | (general accounting standard). FRS 101 deals with presenting financial
  12 | statements, not specific to insurance contracts. Interim reports (FRS
  13 | 101/102) are not the deciding factor.
  14 | Question 3:
  15 | The correct answer is C
  16 | Option C is ideal for FRS 101 Reduced Disclosure Framework: unlisted
  17 | subsidiary of a publicly-available consolidated group. They can potentially
  18 | reduce disclosures in their separate financials, unlike publicly listed
  19 | entities or those preparing consolidated statements.
